## Abstract

QS I-GAUGE is an independent higher education rating system designed specifically for the Indian context. Developed as a partnership between QS Quacquarelli Symonds (UK), the organization behind the globally recognized QS World University Rankings, and an Indian team, it adapts the QS Stars methodology to reflect the unique challenges and opportunities of Indian higher education. The primary purpose of QS I-GAUGE is to provide an independent, data-driven evaluation of institutional quality, enabling universities and colleges to benchmark themselves nationally and internationally while supporting students, parents, and employers in making informed decisions about higher education choices. Unlike mandatory accreditation frameworks such as NAAC and NBA, QS I-GAUGE is voluntary and positions itself as a quality enhancement and benchmarking tool rather than a compliance mechanism. The rating structure awards institutions an overall badge ranging from Bronze, Silver, Gold, Diamond, Platinum, to Platinum+, while also providing detailed category-wise ratings that highlight specific strengths and weaknesses instead of a single cumulative grade. The methodology combines self-reported institutional data, validated externally by QS, with independent surveys of students, faculty, and alumni, ensuring credibility and transparency. A key feature is its reliance on stakeholder feedback, which captures real experiences and perceptions. Institutions are categorized into STEM, MIXED, or NON-STEM depending on the percentage of student enrollment in science and technology fields, ensuring that performance expectations and criteria weightage remain fair across different academic environments.

The assessment framework comprises nine criteria totaling 1400 points, divided into seven core and two advanced criteria. The core areas, worth 1200 points, include Teaching and Learning (250 points), Faculty Quality (200), Employability (200), Diversity and Accessibility (150), Facilities (150), Social Responsibility (150), and Governance and Structure (100). These indicators measure aspects such as faculty–student ratio, student satisfaction, completion rates, faculty qualifications, publications, career services, scholarships, infrastructure quality, sustainability practices, and governance transparency. Institutions further select two advanced criteria, worth 200 points in total, from Arts and Culture, Entrepreneurship, Research (mandatory for universities), Innovation, Internationalisation, and Academic Development, each carrying 100 points. This dual structure balances academic performance with broader social and developmental contributions.

QS I-GAUGE ratings are valid for two years, though institutions may reapply earlier if substantial improvements have been achieved. Beyond public ratings, institutions also receive a confidential developmental roadmap summarizing their strengths, areas requiring improvement, and strategic recommendations. By providing a granular, evidence-based, and internationally benchmarked evaluation, QS I-GAUGE enables institutions to identify performance gaps, enhance strategic planning, and strengthen reputation. For students and stakeholders, the system offers clarity and transparency, while for institutions, it provides both a marketing advantage and a structured path toward continuous improvement.

# 1. Introduction

QS I-GAUGE is a comprehensive higher education rating system designed specifically for India, launched in 2018 through a collaboration between QS Quacquarelli Symonds (UK), globally known for its QS World University Rankings, and the QS I-GAUGE team in India. Unlike accreditation frameworks such as NAAC or NBA, which are largely regulatory and mandatory in nature, QS I-GAUGE is a voluntary rating system that focuses on holistic institutional performance. It adopts and localizes the QS Stars methodology, using both qualitative and quantitative measures, to capture the diverse strengths of Indian higher education institutions (HEIs) and benchmark them against international best practices ‘while addressing India-specific challenges. The system evaluates institutions across seven Core Criteria— Teaching & Learning, Employability, Research, Facilities, Social Responsibility, Inclusiveness, and Governance & Structure—each with specific indicators and weightages, totaling 1200 points. In addition, institutions select two Advanced Criteria from six available—Arts & Culture, Entrepreneurship, Innovation, Internationalisation, Academic Development, and Research (mandatory for universities)—which contribute an additional 200 points, bringing the maximum to 1400 points. Based on their cumulative performance, institutions are awarded category ratings such as Diamond, Gold, or Silver, which not only act as a quality benchmark but also enhance institutional branding and credibility. The methodology emphasizes evidence-based evaluation: institutions submit structured data, which is validated externally by QS experts through documentation checks, audits, and stakeholder feedback (students, alumni, faculty, and employers). Valid for three years, ratings reflect both institutional strengths and areas for improvement, with early re-application permitted if substantial progress is demonstrated. By recognizing achievements in research output, employability outcomes, governance, and social responsibility, QS I-GAUGE provides actionable insights for strategic development. It also directly supports India’s National Education Policy (NEP 2020) by encouraging outcome-based teaching, global competitiveness, innovation, and inclusivity. For students and parents, the rating system acts as a trusted quality signal, while for institutions it opens up opportunities for enhanced international collaborations, increased visibility, diversified funding, and competitive positioning within the Indian and global higher education landscape.

# 2. QS I-GAUGE

## Overview

- QS I-GAUGE is an Indian higher education rating system based on the globally recognized QS Stars methodology, adapted to the Indian context.

- Developed through a partnership between QS Quacquarelli Symonds (UK) and the QS I-GAUGE team in India.

- Designed to assess and rate universities, colleges, and institutions on multiple performance indicators beyond just academics.

## Purpose

- Provide an independent, data-driven evaluation of institutional quality.

- Help institutions benchmark themselves nationally and internationally.

- Support students and parents in making informed choices about higher education in India.

## Rating Structure

- Institutions are awarded a "Diamond," "Gold," or "Silver" rating in different categories.

- Categories include core and specialized criteria, each with its own weightage. Core Criteria (common for all institutions)

- Teaching & Learning

- Employability

- Research

- Facilities

- Social Responsibility

- Inclusiveness

## Special Criteria (vary by institution type)

- **For example:** Medical, Engineering, Arts, and Management institutions may have sector-specific indicators.

## Methodology

- Uses a mix of self-assessment data (submitted by institutions) and external validation by QS.

- Incorporates both qualitative and quantitative metrics.

- Benchmarked against international best practices but localized for Indian higher education challenges.

## Benefits to Institutions

- Enhanced visibility and credibility in the education sector.

- Evidence-based inputs for strategic improvement.

- Marketing advantage for student recruitment.

## Difference from NAAC/NBA

- QS I-GAUGE is voluntary and focuses on a holistic rating, while NAAC/NBA are accreditation frameworks tied to regulatory compliance.

- QS I-GAUGE offers category-wise ratings rather than a single cumulative grade.

# 4. QS I-GAUGE Ratings Structure

## 1. Structure Overview

- **Total Criteria:** 9 (7 Core + 2 Advanced)

- **Core Criteria:** Mandatory for all institutions (Total: 1200 points)

- **Advanced Criteria:** Choose any 2 out of 6 (Total: 200 points)

- **Grand Total:** 1400 points (A + B)

## 2. Core Criteria (7 Categories — 1200 Points)

**These are mandatory for every institution and cover fundamental aspects of institutional performance:**

## 1. Teaching and Learning (TL) – 250 Points

- Curriculum design and relevance to industry needs.

- Student-centered teaching practices (e.g., flipped classrooms, project-based learning).

- Learning outcome assessments and continuous improvement processes.

## 2. Faculty Quality (FQ) — 200 Points

- Faculty qualifications (PhD percentage, experience).

- Research publications and academic contributions.

- Professional development programs for faculty.

## 3. Employability (EP) — 200 Points

- Graduate placement rates and salaries.

- Employer partnerships and recruitment drives.

- Internship programs, career guidance, and skill development workshops.

## 4. Diversity and Accessibility (DA) – 150 Points

- Gender balance and representation of minority groups.

- Scholarships for underprivileged students.

- Disability-friendly infrastructure and inclusion measures.

## 5. Facilities (FC) — 150 Points

- Quality of physical infrastructure (classrooms, labs, libraries).

- Access to modern ICT-enabled learning spaces.

- Sports, cultural, and recreational facilities.

## 6. Social Responsibility (SR) — 150 Points

- Community outreach programs.

- Sustainability and environmental initiatives.

- Volunteering and societal engagement projects.

## 7. Governance and Structure (GS) – 100 Points

- Strategic vision and mission alignment.

- Decision-making transparency.

- Stakeholder involvement in governance.

## 3. Advanced Criteria (Choose Any 2 — 200 Points)

**These allow institutions to showcase special strengths. Each selected category has 100 points:** 1. Arts and Culture (AC)

- Promotion of cultural diversity and heritage.

- Student participation in cultural events at national/international levels.

## 2. Entrepreneurship (ET)

- Entrepreneurship cells and incubation support.

- Funding support for start-ups and student ventures.

## 3. Research (RE) (Mandatory for Universities)

- Volume and impact of research publications.

- Patents filed and granted.

- Industry-funded research projects.

## 4. Innovation (IV)

- New product/process development.

- Innovation labs and hackathons.

## 5. Internationalisation (IS)

- Student and faculty exchange programs.

- International collaborations and joint degree programs.

## 6. Academic Development (AD)

- Introduction of new courses/programs.

- Interdisciplinary learning initiatives.

## 4. Scoring and Rating Levels

- **Diamond:** 85% and above.

- **Gold:** 70–84%.

- **Silver:** 55–69%.

- **Below Silver:** Less than 55% (Improvement required).

## 5. Key Features of QS I-GAUGE Evaluation

- **Evidence-Based:** Institutions must submit proof for each claim.

- **External Validation:** QS team verifies submissions through peer review and data checks.

- **Customised Benchmarking:** Combines global standards (QS Stars methodology) with local Indian context.

- **Validity:** Ratings valid for 3 years; early reapplication allowed upon significant improvements.

# 5. Categorisation of institutions

## 1. Purpose of Categorisation

- QS I-GAUGE rating indicators are customised according to the nature of the institution.

- Institutions are grouped into categories to ensure fair evaluation that reflects their discipline mix.

- Prevents STEM-heavy institutions from being compared unfairly with arts or humanities-focused institutions.

## 2. Categories of Institutions

STEM Category Definition Enrolment Percentage

Majority of students are enrolled

STEM in Science, Engineering, disciplines

Technology, \> 65% Mathematics

Example Institutions

Engineering universities, technology-focused institutes

MIXED Balanced mix of STEM and nonso_s59sTEM programs

NONMajority of students in non-STEM. 5095 or 0%STEM disciplines

## 3. Determining Category

Comprehensive universities with both engineering and arts faculties

## Arts, commerce, law, humanities colleges

- **Step 1:** Identify total student enrolment in the institution.

- **Step 2:** Count the number of students enrolled in STEM courses (Science, Technology, Engineering, Mathematics).

- **Step 3:** Calculate STEM percentage:

STEM %=Number of STEM students/Total students×100

- **Step 4:** Assign category based on percentage:

  - \>65% → STEM

  - 50–65% → MIXED

  - \< 50% or 0% → NON-STEM

## 4. Impact on Ratings

- The criteria weightage and performance expectations may differ slightly for STEM, Mixed, and Non-STEM to account for different academic environments.

- **Example:**

  - A STEM-focused institution may have stronger indicators for research, patents, and lab facilities.

  - A NON-STEM institution may be assessed more on cultural activities, arts engagement, and humanities research.

# 6. QS I-GAUGE Independent Survey Process

## 1. Purpose of Independent Surveys

- Surveys capture direct stakeholder feedback to complement quantitative institutional data.

- Ensures the rating reflects real user experiences — not just policy or infrastructure on paper.

- **Targets three key stakeholder groups:**

  - Students (current enrolment)

  - Faculty members (all departments)

  - Alumni (past graduates)

## 2. Mandatory Minimum Survey Participation Thresholds

Survey Group Minimum Response Rate Population Base

Student Survey ≥ 30% Total enrolled students (all years & courses)

Faculty Survey ≥ 60% Total faculty strength (across departments) Alumni Survey ≥ 10% Percentage based on current student population

- **Example:** If an institution has 2,000 students and 150 faculty:

  - Student survey must have ≥ 600 responses

  - Faculty survey must have ≥ 90 responses

  - Alumni survey must have ≥ 200 responses

## 3. Survey-Based Indicators

- Several rating indicators are based on survey results.

- **Example:** Overall Student Satisfaction (Student Survey Indicator)

  - **Maximum Points:** 30

  - **Scoring Logic:**

| **Criterion / condition** | **Points / scoring**                                  |
|---------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------|
| Score = 0                 | if satisfaction ≤ 60%                                 |
| Score = 30                | if satisfaction ≥ 90%                                 |
| Partial Score (0–30)      | if 60% \< satisfaction \< 90% (scaled proportionally) |

## 4. Scoring Formula Example

If Overall Student Satisfaction = 80%,

| **Criterion / condition**              | **Points / scoring**  |
|----------------------------------------|-----------------------|
| **First, check threshold:** 80% \> 60% | eligible for scoring. |

- **Scaling between 60% and 90%:**

Score=(80–60)/(90-60)×30

Score=20/30×30=20

Final Score = 20 points (out of 30)

## 5. Importance of Meeting Thresholds

- **If participation rates fall below the minimum threshold:**

  - The institution may lose points for that indicator.

  - The rating process may be delayed or invalidated.

- Ensuring high participation increases data reliability and rating credibility.

# 7. Institutional Data Submission Process in QS I-GAUGE

## 1. Purpose of Institutional Data

- Complements independent survey results by providing quantitative and qualitative evidence from the institution itself.

- Covers all indicators in the rating process that require documented proof rather than just perception-based survey scores.

- Ensures accuracy, transparency, and comparability in the rating process.

## 2. Data Submission Requirements

- **Coverage:** All data must relate to the last completed academic year, unless otherwise stated in the QS I-GAUGE guidelines.

- **Format:** Structured according to QS I-GAUGE’s prescribed templates (often in Excel or secure online portal forms).

- **Evidence:** Where applicable, supporting documents such as:

  - Annual reports

  - Placement reports

  - Faculty qualification lists

  - Infrastructure details

  - Policy documents and photographs

## 3. Validation Process

- **The QS I-GAUGE Analyst Team reviews the submitted data for:**

  - Completeness — ensuring all fields are filled.

  - Consistency – checking data alignment across multiple indicators. » Credibility — verifying claims through supporting evidence.

- If data is incomplete or unclear, QS I-GAUGE reserves the right to request additional information or clarifications.

- Institutions must respond promptly to maintain the evaluation timeline.

## 4. Examples of Data-Dependent Indicators

- Faculty–Student Ratio (requires official enrolment and faculty data)

- Library Holdings (requires inventory lists)

- Research Output (requires publication and citation data)

- Financial Aid Details (requires scholarship records)

## 5. Importance in the Rating Process

- **Direct Impact on Scores:** Many indicators are fully scored based on institutional data.

- **Accuracy in Benchmarking:** Ensures institutions are evaluated fairly against peers in similar categories (STEM, Mixed, Non-STEM).

- **Audit Readiness:** Data should be maintained for at least three years for possible reverification or audit.

# 8. QS I-GAUGE Rating Outcomes

## 1. Rating Classification

**QS I-GAUGE awards six badges in ascending order of performance, based on total points scored out of 1400:** Minimum pj, gge points

Bronze 350

Silver 560

Gold 770

Diamond 1050

Platinum 1260

Platinum+ 1325 Equivalent Additional Requirements,

25% None

40% None

55% None

75% None

90%

Must have Diamond in all core categories (Colleges) or Diamond in all core + Research (Universities)

95%

Must meet Platinum criteria AND hold IOE/INI status or be in Top 30 NIRF Overall Rankings

## 2. Special Conditions

- **For Colleges:** Platinum requires Diamond badge in all 7 core categories.

- **For Universities:** Platinum requires Diamond in all 7 core categories + Research.

- **Platinum+:**

  - Achieve Platinum performance plus

  - Hold Institute of Eminence (IOE) or Institute of National Importance (INI) status OR

  - Be ranked in Top 30 of NIRF Overall Rankings.

## 3. Audit and Publication Process

- **Independent Audit:** QS I-GAUGE conducts a rigorous validation of institutional data, survey results, and performance indicators.

- **Institutional Consent:** Final results are published only after approval from the participating institution.

- **Outcome Publication:** Ratings are made public, allowing institutions to market their achievements to students, parents, and stakeholders.

## 4. Developmental Roadmap

- **Along with ratings, each institution receives a confidential summary detailing:** o Strength areas.

  - Areas for improvement.

  - Suggested strategies for performance enhancement in future evaluations.

## 5. Validity of Ratings

- **Duration:** 2 years from the date of release.

- Institutions may reapply before expiry if significant improvements are made.

# 9. QS I-GAUGE Core Criterion: Teaching & Learning (TL)

**Core Criterion:** Teaching & Learning (TL) — 250 Points

## Purpose

- Measures how effectively an institution delivers its curriculum, supports student learning, and ensures strong academic outcomes.

- Balances quantitative performance metrics with stakeholder feedback from students.

- Covers teaching quality, learning environment, student engagement, and post-study progression.

Detailed Breakdown of Indicators

### TL1 – Faculty–Student Ratio (30 points)

- **What it Measures:** Availability of faculty in proportion to student enrolment.

- **Why it Matters:** Lower ratios indicate more personalised attention, better interaction, and improved learning outcomes.

- **Scoring Logic:**

| **Criterion / condition**                  | **Points / scoring**                                                                                                |
|--------------------------------------------|---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| **STEM:** Ideal = 10 students per faculty  | **30 points (scaled proportionally). - Non-STEM:** Ideal = 25 students per faculty → 50 points (scaled down to 30). |
| **Mixed:** Ideal = 20 students per faculty | 40 points (scaled down to 30).                                                                                      |

- **Data Source:** Institutional data with HR/faculty lists and enrolment figures. TL2 – Teaching Student Satisfaction (30 points)

- **What it Measures:** % of students satisfied with the teaching quality in their courses.

- **Why it Matters:** Reflects real-time learner perception of teaching effectiveness.

- **Scoring Logic:**

| **Criterion / condition** | **Points / scoring**  |
|---------------------------|-----------------------|
| ≥ 90% satisfaction        | 30 points.            |
| ≤ 60%                     | 0 points.             |
| 60–90%                    | proportional scoring. |

- **Data Source:** Independent QS I-GAUGE student survey.

### TL3 – Overall Student Satisfaction (30 points)

- **What it Measures:** General satisfaction of students with the institution as a whole.

- **Why it Matters:** Captures a broader perspective beyond classroom teaching (e.g., facilities, environment).

- **Scoring Logic:** Same as TL2.

- **Data Source:** Independent QS I-GAUGE student survey.

### TL4 – Completion Rate (30 points)

- **What it Measures:** % of students graduating within the scheduled timeframe.

- **Why it Matters:** High completion rates indicate strong academic support and effective teaching.

- **Scoring Logic:**

| **Criterion / condition** | **Points / scoring**      |
|---------------------------|---------------------------|
| 90% graduation rate       | 50 points (scaled to 30). |

- Lower rates scaled proportionally.

<!-- -->

- **Data Source:** Institutional graduation records.

### TL5 – Contact Hours per Week (40 points)

- **What it Measures:** Scheduled instructional time for full-time students.

- **Why it Matters:** Ensures adequate formal learning exposure.

- **Scoring Logic:**

| **Criterion / condition**           | **Points / scoring**      |
|-------------------------------------|---------------------------|
| **STEM:** 30 hours/week             | 20 points (scaled to 40). |
| **Non-STEM & Mixed:** 25 hours/week | 15 points (scaled to 40). |

- **Data Source:** Academic timetables, class schedules.

### TL6 – Self-Study Hours per Day (30 points)

- **What it Measures:** Average daily hours students spend on independent learning.

- **Why it Matters:** Encourages self-learning skills critical for lifelong learning.

- **Scoring Logic:**

| **Criterion / condition** | **Points / scoring**       |
|---------------------------|----------------------------|
| **STEM:** 2 hrs/day       | 5 points (scaled to 30).   |
| **Non-STEM:** 3 hrs/day   | 6 points (scaled to 30).   |
| **Mixed:** 2.5 hrs/day    | 5.5 points (scaled to 30). |

- **Data Source:** Student survey or learning logs.

### TL7 – Interactive Teaching / Best Practices (20 points)

- **What it Measures:** Engagement in interactive teaching methods OR adoption of best learning practices.

- **Why it Matters:** Improves critical thinking, collaboration, and engagement.

- **Options for Scoring:**

## 1. At least 20% teaching sessions as interactive (scaled up to 50%). 2. Implementation of best practices:

- Mentor–Mentee Programme (10 points).

- Peer group learning or remedial support (10 points).

- **Data Source:** Teaching plans, activity records, policy documents.

### TL8 – Learning Management System (LMS) (25 points)

- **What it Measures:** Availability and active use of an online platform for academic delivery.

- **Key Features Considered:**

  - Class schedules & academic calendars.

  - Exam registration, course syllabus, reading materials.

  - Lecture notes, discussion boards, announcements.

- **Data Source:** LMS screenshots, usage analytics.

### TL9 – Further Studies (15 points)

- **What it Measures:** % of graduates pursuing further education within 12 months.

- **Why it Matters:** Indicates progression to higher qualifications.

- **Scoring Logic:**

| **Criterion / condition** | **Points / scoring**     |
|---------------------------|--------------------------|
| 25% progression rate      | 5 points (scaled to 15). |

- **Platinum + Condition:** At least 1% of graduates enrolled in Top 500 QS-ranked institutions.

<!-- -->

- **Data Source:** Alumni tracking, admission confirmation letters.

## Scoring Approach

- Some indicators are survey-based (TL2, TL3, TL6) and rely on stakeholder perception.

- Others are data-based (TL1, TL4–TL9) and rely on institutional documentation.

- Scores are scaled proportionally where thresholds are defined.

## Role in Overall QS I-GAUGE Rating

- TL’s 250 points = largest share of core criteria total (1200 points).

- High TL performance significantly boosts overall badge level.

- Links directly to employability, student satisfaction, and academic excellence.

## Category Rating Outcome – Teaching & Learning Example (Max: 250 Points)

| **Badge** | **Minimum Points in Category / Equivalent % of Category Points / Meaning** |
|-----------|----------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| Bronze    | 65 / 26% / Basic performance, meets minimum expectations.                  |
| Silver    | 100 / 40% / Acceptable performance with room for improvement.              |
| Gold      | 140 / 56% / Strong performance across most indicators.                     |
| Diamond   | 190 / 76% / Excellent performance in nearly all indicators.                |
| Platinum  | 225 / 90% / Exceptional performance, close to full category potential.     |

## How Category Rating Works

- Each core and advanced category (e.g., Teaching & Learning, Faculty Quality, Research, etc.) is scored individually.

- The score for that category determines its own badge, independent of other categories.

- **For overall institutional rating:**

  - All category scores are summed and converted into a total out of 1400 points.

  - Additional rules apply for Platinum and Platinum+ at the institutional level.

## Why Category Badges Matter

- Provides granular insight into strengths and weaknesses in specific areas.

- Institutions can highlight top-performing categories in marketing (e.g., “Diamond in Teaching & Learning”).

- Helps in targeted improvement efforts for weaker categories before the next rating cycle.

# 10. Faculty –Student Ratio (FSR)

## 1. Purpose of Faculty–Student Ratio (FSR)

- **Goal:** Measure the teaching capacity of an institution by comparing the number of enrolled students to the number of full-time equivalent faculty members.

- **Why It Matters:**

  - Lower ratios generally allow for smaller class sizes, more personalised attention, and better learning outcomes.

  - It’s a key quality metric in QS I-GAUGE, QS World University Rankings, NAAC, and other accreditation/rating frameworks.

## 2. QS I-GAUGE Scoring Logic for FSR (TL1)

- **Indicator Code:** TL1 (part of Teaching & Learning — 250 points)

- **Definition:** Ratio of total number of students to the total number of instructors/faculty.

- **Scoring Rules by Institution Type:**

| **Criterion / condition**              | **Points / scoring**                                          |
|----------------------------------------|---------------------------------------------------------------|
| **STEM institutions:** Ideal = 10:1    | scaled to 30 points.                                          |
| **NonSTEM institutions:** Ideal = 25:1 | scaled to 50 points (then adjusted to TL category weighting). |
| **Mixed institutions:** Ideal = 20:1   | scaled to 40 points.                                          |

- **Calculation Formula:**

FSR=Total number of students enrolled (all levels)/ Total number of fulltime equivalent faculty

- **Full-time Equivalent (FTE):**

  - Part-time faculty are converted to full-time equivalent values based on teaching load.

  - Visiting/adjunct faculty may be excluded unless they have a regular teaching assignment.

## 3. Example Calculation

- **Example (STEM Institution):**

  - **Students:** 5,000

  - **Faculty (FTE):** 500

| **Criterion / condition**  | **Points / scoring** |
|----------------------------|----------------------|
| **FSR=5,000 + 500 = 10:**1 | Full 30 points.      |

- **If ratio = 15:**1 in STEM, the score is scaled proportionally:

Score=Ideal ratio/Actual ratio×Max Points

## 4. Data Requirements in QS I-GAUGE

- **Institution must provide:**

  - Latest academic year’s student enrolment numbers (by program level).

  - Full-time and part–time faculty count with designation.

  - Supporting HR and enrolment records.

- **Validation:** QS I-GAUGE analysts may cross-check with government databases, accreditation reports, and institutional HR records.

## 5. Strategic Importance

- **A favourable FSR:**

  - Improves student satisfaction scores (correlates with TL2, TL3 indicators).

  - Enhances employability outcomes due to better academic support. . Contributes significantly to QS I-GAUGE Teaching & Learning score and overall badge level.

## 6. Common Challenges for Institutions

- Rapid enrolment growth without proportional faculty hiring.

- Over–reliance on part-time or contractual faculty reducing FTE count.

- Misclassification of research-only staff as teaching faculty.

**Data** **You** **Submit**

- Total number of full-time equivalent (FTE) faculty teaching STEM courses.

  - Include regular, contract, and visiting faculty if they taught ≥ 1 full semester.

  - **Convert part-time faculty to FTE. (Example:** 2 half-time = 1 full-time) o Exclude admin-only positions.

## 3. Scaling to Points:

- **Full score (30 points) if ratio = 10:**1 or better.

- Lower points if ratio is worse, scaled down proportionally.

# 11. Teaching Student Satisfaction (TL2)

## 1. Purpose

- Measures how satisfied students are with the quality of teaching they receive in the institution.

- Captures direct learner feedback, making it one of the most important perceptionbased indicators in QS I-GAUGE.

- Ensures that teaching quality is evaluated not only through institutional data but also through the student experience.

## 2. QS I-GAUGE Scoring Logic (TL2)

- **Indicator Code:** TL2

- **Maximum Points:** 30 (out of 250 TL points)

- **Data Source:** Independent QS I-GAUGE Student Survey conducted online.

- **Scoring Rules:**

| **Criterion / condition** | **Points / scoring**    |
|---------------------------|-------------------------|
| ≥ 90% satisfaction        | 30 points (full score). |
| ≤ 60% satisfaction        | 0 points.               |

- Between 60% and 90% → proportional scaling.

<!-- -->

- **Formula for proportional score:**

Score=(Actual %–60)/(90-60)×30

(Only *applies* if *actual* % is between 60 and 90)

## 3. Example Calculation

- **Case 1:** Satisfaction = 80%

Score=(80–60)/30x30=20 points

| **Criterion / condition**      | **Points / scoring**        |
|--------------------------------|-----------------------------|
| **Case 2:** Satisfaction = 92% | Full 30 points.             |
| **Case 3:** Satisfaction = 55% | 0 points (below threshold). |

## 4. Survey Process in QS I-GAUGE

- **Target Respondents:** Current students from all academic years and programs.

- **Survey Distribution:** Institution circulates QS I-GAUGE’s online survey link.

- **Minimum Response Rate:** At least 30% of total student population must complete the survey (mandatory threshold).

- **Survey Design:**

  - **Students rate their agreement with statements about teaching quality. Questions typically cover:**

- Instructor subject knowledge.

- Clarity in teaching.

- Engagement in lectures.

- Feedback and doubt–clearing.

- Availability outside class hours.

## 5. Strategic Importance

- Directly influences Teaching & Learning category score.

- Strongly correlated with Overall Student Satisfaction (TL3).

- Institutions with active student feedback systems, regular faculty training, and interactive teaching methods tend to score higher.

- Low scores here may indicate gaps in pedagogy, faculty engagement, or communication.

## 6. Best Practices to Improve Score

- Continuous Faculty Development Programs (FDPs) focusing on teaching methods.

- Regular student feedback collection before QS I-GAUGE surveys to address concerns.

- Use of blended learning, case studies, and project-based learning for engagement.

- Recognising and rewarding high-performing faculty based on student feedback.

# 12. Overall Student Satisfaction (TL3)

## 1. Purpose

- Measures how satisfied students are overall with their institutional experience — covering academics, facilities, support services, campus life, and governance.

- Provides a holistic perception measure from the students’ perspective.

- Complements Teaching Student Satisfaction (TL2), which focuses only on classroom teaching quality.

## 2. QS I-GAUGE Scoring Logic (TL3)

- **Indicator Code:** TL3

- **Maximum Points:** 30 (out of 250 TL points)

- **Data Source:** Independent QS I-GAUGE Student Survey conducted online.

- **Scoring Rules:**

| **Criterion / condition** | **Points / scoring**    |
|---------------------------|-------------------------|
| ≥ 90% satisfaction        | 30 points (full score). |
| ≤ 60% satisfaction        | 0 points.               |

- Between 60% and 90% → proportional scaling.

<!-- -->

- **Formula for proportional score:**

Score=(Actual %–60)/(90-60)×30

(Applies if *actual* % is between 60 and 90)

## 3. Example Calculation

- **Case 1:** Satisfaction = 80%

Score=(80–60)/30x30=20 points

| **Criterion / condition**      | **Points / scoring**        |
|--------------------------------|-----------------------------|
| **Case 2:** Satisfaction = 95% | Full 30 points.             |
| **Case 3:** Satisfaction = 55% | 0 points (below threshold). |

## 4. Survey Process in QS I-GAUGE

- **Target Respondents:** All current students, across all academic years and programs.

- **Distribution:** Institution circulates QS I-GAUGE’s online survey link to students.

- **Minimum Response Rate:** At least 30% of the total student population must respond (mandatory for validity).

- **Survey Content:** Questions typically address:

  - Overall academic experience.

  - Learning environment.

  - Infrastructure and facilities.

  - Administrative efficiency.

  - Extracurricular and co-curricular opportunities.

  - Campus safety and inclusivity.

## 5. Strategic Importance

- Serves as a top-level performance indicator for student experience.

- Strongly correlates with student retention, alumni loyalty, and institutional reputation.

- High TL3 scores usually reflect balanced performance across academic, administrative, and campus life dimensions.

- Low TL3 scores may indicate systemic issues beyond teaching — e.g., poor facilities, lack of student support, or weak engagement.

## 6. Best Practices to Improve Score

- Maintain regular student satisfaction surveys internally — not just for QS I-GAUGE.

- Create Student Support Centres to resolve grievances promptly.

- Enhance campus amenities and extracurricular offerings.

- Involve students in decision-making through councils and committees.

- Ensure visible improvements based on student feedback. Programme Level

# 13. Completion Rate (TL4)

## 1. Purpose

- Measures the percentage of students who successfully graduate on time — within the scheduled program duration.

- Acts as a direct academic performance and retention metric for the institution.

- Indicates the institution’s ability to support students academically and keep them engaged until graduation.

## 2. QS I-GAUGE Scoring Logic (TL4)

- **Indicator Code:** TL4

- **Maximum Points:** 30 (out of 250 TL points)

- **Scoring Rules:**

| **Criterion / condition** | **Points / scoring**                          |
|---------------------------|-----------------------------------------------|
| 90% completion rate       | full 30 points (scaled from a 50-point base). |

- Lower rates are scaled proportionally downwards.

<!-- -->

- **Formula for proportional score:**

Score=Actual Completion Rate/90% Benchmark×30

(Capped at a *maximum* of 30 points)

## 3. Definition of Completion Rate

- **Numerator:** Number of students who graduated on time (in the standard duration of their program) in the most recent academic year.

- **Denominator:** Total number of students scheduled to graduate in that year.

- **Formula:**

Completion Rate(%)=Graduated On TimeScheduled to Graduatex100

## 4. Example Calculation

| **Criterion / condition**                           | **Points / scoring**                    |
|-----------------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------|
| **Example 1:** 450 of 500 students graduate on time | 450/500×100=90% ⇒ 30 points             |
| **Example 2:** 400 of 500 graduate on time          | 400/500×100=80% ⇒ 80/90×30≈26.67 points |
| **Example 3:** 300 of 500 graduate on time          | 300/500×100=60% ⇒ 60/90×30=20 points    |

## 5. Data Sources in QS I-GAUGE

- Institutional academic records for the most recent graduating batch.

- Examination office data confirming number scheduled vs number graduated.

- May be cross–verified against official transcripts issued.

## 6. Strategic Importance

- **A high completion rate signals:**

  - Effective teaching & academic support.

  - Strong student engagement.

  - Low dropout/attrition rates.

- **Alow completion rate may indicate:**

  - Academic difficulty without adequate support.

  - Poor retention strategies.

  - Weak tracking of at-risk students.

## 7. Best Practices to Improve Completion Rate

- Early intervention for academically struggling students.

- Academic mentoring and tutoring programs.

- Flexible learning plans for students facing challenges.

- Strengthening student advising and progress tracking.

# 14. Contact Hours per Week (TL5)

## 1. Purpose

- Measures the amount of formal, direct instructional time students receive from faculty each week.

- Reflects the institution’s commitment to structured learning and faculty-student engagement.

- Helps determine whether teaching delivery meets disciplinary expectations (STEM vs Non-STEM).

## 2. QS I-GAUGE Scoring Logic (TL5)

- **Indicator Code:** TL5

- **Maximum Points:** 40 (out of 250 TL points)

- **Data Source:** Institutional timetables for full-time students only.

- **Benchmark Contact Hours:**

| **Criterion / condition**                        | **Points / scoring**                                                  |
|--------------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------------------|
| **STEM Institutions:** 30 hours/week             | full score (scaled to 20 points, then doubled for weighting to 40).   |
| **Non-STEM & Mixed Institutions:** 25 hours/week | full score (scaled to 15 points, then proportionally adjusted to 40). |

- **Scaling:**

  - Lower hours proportionally reduce the score.

  - **Formula:**

Score=Actual Contact Hours/Benchmark Hours×Max Points

## 3. Definition of Contact Hours

- **Included:** Lectures, tutorials, practical/lab sessions, supervised project work.

- **Excluded:**

  - Self-study hours (covered in TL6).

  - Unsupervised lab time.

  - Informal study groups.

## 4. Example Calculations

- **Case 1– STEM Institution:**

  - Actual hours = 28/week

| **Criterion / condition** | **Points / scoring**   |
|---------------------------|------------------------|
| Benchmark = 30            | 28/30×40=~37.33 points |

- **Case 2 —- Non-STEM Institution:**

  - Actual hours = 20/week

| **Criterion / condition** | **Points / scoring** |
|---------------------------|----------------------|
| Benchmark = 25            | 20/25×40=32 points   |

## 5. Data Collection in QS I-GAUGE

- Institutions must submit official academic timetables for all programs and years.

- QS I-GAUGE analysts verify that contact hours are faculty-led and timetabled.

- May request attendance records to ensure scheduled hours are actually delivered.

## 6. Strategic Importance

- **Higher contact hours generally:**

  - Enhance learning continuity.

  - Improve student satisfaction scores (linked to TL2 and TL3).

  - Support stronger completion rates (TL4).

- **Very low contact hours may indicate:**

  - Resource shortages.

  - Over–reliance on self-learning without structured guidance.

## 7. Best Practices to Maintain/Improve Contact Hours

- Balance lectures with tutorials and lab sessions for active learning.

- Use blended learning to supplement (not replace) scheduled contact time.

- Track delivery against the timetable to avoid reductions due to cancellations. pep piscipli Acade Te Deli Le Lab Tut Studio Oth Total Thres TL Da V N art ne Type mic r ver ct s/Pr ori s/Clini er Cont hold 5 ta e 0 me (STEM Level my ur acti als cs/Se Con act Hour Sc So ri t nt/ Non(UG/P /S Mo es cals /W minar tact Hour s (by or urc fi e Pro STEM/ G/PhD e de (hr (hrs ork s (hrs s discip e e / e s gra Mixed) /Mixe m (FT/ s/ /wk sho (hrs/ /wk (hrs/ line) (0 Lin r m d) es PT) wk ) ps wk) ) wk) -k / te) (hr 40 Ors/ )wwkn)er Ex STEM UG 2 FT 16 8 4 2 0 30 30 4 am 0 0 ple 2

# 15. Self-study Hours per Day (TL6)

## 1. Purpose

- Measures the average daily hours students spend on independent learning outside formal contact hours.

- Captures the institution’s ability to cultivate self-directed learning habits and promote academic discipline.

- Reflects how well the institution encourages students to engage in preparation, revision, and research beyond classroom instruction.

## 2. QS I-GAUGE Scoring Logic (TL6)

- **Indicator Code:** TL6

- **Maximum Points:** 30 (out of 250 TL points)

- **Data Source:** Independent survey of students + supporting documentation (study policies, workload expectations).

- **Benchmarks for Full Score:**

  - **STEM Institutions:** 2 hours/day (scaled to 5 hours for maximum score). Non-STEM Institutions: 3 hours/day (scaled to 6 hours for maximum score).

  - **Mixed Institutions:** 2.5 hours/day (scaled to 5.5 hours for maximum score).

- **Scaling:**

  - Actual reported hours are compared against the benchmark.

  - **Formula:**

Score=Actual Avg. Hours/Benchmark Hours×Max Points

- Scores are capped at the maximum even if actual hours exceed the benchmark.

## 3. Definition of Self-Study

- **Included:**

  - Reviewing lecture notes.

  - Reading recommended/reference materials.

  - Completing assignments and projects independently.

  - Research work outside class.

- **Excluded:**

  - Group discussions without structured academic intent.

  - Passive activities like watching unrelated videos or social media use.

## 4. Example Calculations

- **Case 1– STEM Institution:**

  - Average daily hours = 1.8

| **Criterion / condition** | **Points / scoring** |
|---------------------------|----------------------|
| Benchmark = 2             | 1.8/2x30=27 points   |

- **Case 2 —- Non-STEM Institution:**

  - Average daily hours = 2.4

| **Criterion / condition** | **Points / scoring** |
|---------------------------|----------------------|
| Benchmark = 3             | 2.4/3x30=24 points   |

## 5. Data Collection & Verification

- Primarily based on student survey responses.

- **QS I-GAUGE analysts may cross-check with:**

  - Institutional workload policies.

  - Assignment schedules.

  - Learning management system (LMS) logs showing student activity.

## 6. Strategic Importance

- **Strong self-study culture improves:**

  - Completion rates (TL4).

  - Teaching satisfaction (TL2) and overall satisfaction (TL3). . Graduate readiness for research and professional problem–solving.

- **Low scores here can indicate:**

  - Overloaded timetables leaving no room for independent study. . Lack of motivation or inadequate academic support.

## 7. Best Practices to Improve Self-Study Scores

- Encourage flipped classroom models.

- Provide clear reading lists and preparatory materials.

- Set regular formative assessments to drive independent study habits.

- Integrate library and online resources into learning plans.

# 16. Interactive Teaching (TL7)

## 1. Purpose

- Evaluates the extent and quality of interactive learning methods used by the institution.

- Shifts focus from traditional one-way lectures to student engagement, participation, and collaboration in the learning process.

- Recognises innovative teaching practices that promote deep learning, critical thinking, and problem-solving.

## 2. QS I-GAUGE Scoring Logic (TL7)

- **Indicator Code:** TL7

- **Maximum Points:** 20 (out of 250 TL points)

- **Scoring Options:**

## A. Based on Percentage of Interactive Sessions

| **Criterion / condition**                                     | **Points / scoring**                                                            |
|---------------------------------------------------------------|---------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| If at least 20% of teaching hours involve interactive methods | score is proportionally scaled up to 50% interactive teaching = full 20 points. |

## B. Based on Best Practices in Learning (if interactive teaching % data is unavailable) o 10 points each for implementing:

1\. Mentor–Mentee Programme – regular academic/personal guidance sessions.

2\. Peer Group Learning Support / Remedial & Additional Support for Learning – structured peer mentoring or remedial classes.

- Maximum = 20 points.

## 3. What Counts as Interactive Teaching

- **Included:**

  - Group discussions and debates.

  - Case-based learning.

  - Problem-solving workshops.

  - Role-playing & simulations.

  - Laboratory sessions with active student participation.

  - Student–led presentations & flipped classrooms.

  - Interactive use of technology (polls, quizzes, live feedback tools).

- **Excluded:**

  - Passive listening to lectures (even if slides are shown).

  - Solely instructor–led content without student interaction.

## 4. Example Scenarios

- **Case 1 – Percentage Method:**

  - Interactive teaching = 35% of total class hours.

| **Criterion / condition**       | **Points / scoring** |
|---------------------------------|----------------------|
| Benchmark for full points = 50% | 35/50×20=14 points   |

- **Case 2 — Best Practices Method:**

| **Criterion / condition**          | **Points / scoring** |
|------------------------------------|----------------------|
| **Mentor–Mentee Programme:** Yes   | 10 points.           |
| **Remedial Learning Support:** Yes | 10 points.           |

- Total = 20 points.

## 5. Data Collection & Verification

- **Reported via institutional data submission with:**

  - Timetable/class schedules showing interactive activities.

  - Sample session plans.

  - Evidence of peer learning/remedial classes.

- QS I-GAUGE may also cross–verify with student survey results to validate claims.

## 6. Strategic Importance

- Strongly correlates with student satisfaction (TL2) and completion rates (TL4).

- Builds collaboration, communication, and higher-order thinking skills in students.

- Helps meet accreditation requirements for outcome-based education (OBE) and experiential learning.

## 7. Best Practices to Improve Interactive Teaching Scores

- Adopt flipped classroom models for higher engagement.

- Integrate project-based learning into every course.

- Use technology like Kahoot!, Mentimeter, or LMS-integrated quizzes for real-time interaction.

- Train faculty in active learning pedagogy.

Interactive Teaching — Tracker

- **Frequency:** One-time, Weekly, Monthly, Semester-wise, Other.

- **Proof:** Photos, Reports, Feedback, LMS Evidence, Other.

# 17. Learning Management System (LMS)

## 1. Purpose

- Evaluates the availability and effective use of a digital Learning Management System to enhance teaching, learning, and academic administration.

- Recognises institutions that use technology to centralise resources, improve accessibility, and support blended learning models.

## 2. QS I-GAUGE Scoring Logic (TL8)

- **Indicator Code:** TL8

- **Maximum Points:** 25 (out of 250 TL points)

- Points are awarded based on both presence and actual usage of LMS features.

- A high score requires evidence that students and faculty actively use the platform — not just that it exists.

## 3. What Counts as LMS

## An LMS is an integrated online platform that supports:

- Academic administration (course enrolment, attendance, gradebook)

- Content delivery (reading materials, lecture slides, recorded sessions)

- Communication (announcements, discussion boards, chat)

- Assessment & feedback (online quizzes, assignment submission, grading)

- Tracking & reporting (student progress, analytics, deadlines)

## Examples:

- Moodle

- Blackboard

- Canvas

- Google Classroom

- MS Teams with integrated course management

- Institution’s own custom-built LMS

## 4. Scoring Considerations

**To get close to the full 25 points, your LMS should provide and actively use the following:** 1. Class schedules — timetable accessible online.

2\. Academic calendar —- updated semester/yearly academic events.

3\. Exam registration — online exam enrolment & hall tickets.

4\. Course syllabus & outline – available for every course.

5\. Reading materials & lecture notes — downloadable in multiple formats.

6\. Class bulletin boards — announcements, discussion forums.

7\. Recorded lectures & e-learning modules — for asynchronous access.

8\. Integration with online assessment tools — for quizzes and assignments.

9\. Tracking system – attendance, performance reports, student activity logs.

## 5. Evidence Required

- Screenshots or live demo of the LMS.

- Usage analytics — number of students/faculty logged in and actively using features.

- Examples of course pages showing syllabus, assignments, lecture materials.

- Student and faculty feedback (through surveys) showing satisfaction with the LMS.

## 6. Example Scenarios

- Case 1 – Basic LMS (Low Score ~10 points)

  - Only stores PDF notes and timetables.

  - No online assessments, announcements, or usage tracking.

- Case 2 — Fully Featured but Low Usage (Medium Score ~15–18 points) - LMS has all core functions, but \<50% of faculty actively upload materials or conduct assessments.

- Case 3 – Fully Featured and High Usage (Full Score 25 points)

  - 90%+ of courses have complete materials uploaded.

  - All communication, assignments, and evaluations run through LMS. - High satisfaction scores from student surveys.

## 7. Strategic Importance

- Improves student accessibility to resources anytime, anywhere.

- Encourages digital literacy for both faculty and students.

- Strengthens evidence for accreditation and blended learning adoption.

- Links with other TL indicators (self-study hours, interactive teaching).

## 8. Best Practices to Maximise Points

- Make LMS usage mandatory for all courses.

- Train faculty to design engaging course pages.

- Include interactive modules like quizzes, polls, and discussion boards.

- Integrate Al-based analytics to identify struggling students early.

- Conduct student orientation on LMS features.

- **Frequency:** One-time, Weekly, Monthly, Semester-wise, Other.

- **Proof:** Screenshot, Report, LMS Export, URL, Other.

# 18. Further Studies

## 1. Purpose

- Measures the proportion of students who pursue further education within 12 months of graduation.

- Focuses on an institutions academic quality and brand value as reflected in graduates being accepted into higher–level programmes in reputable institutions.

- Encourages universities and colleges to prepare students for postgraduate education in India and abroad.

## 2. QS I-GAUGE Scoring Logic (TL9)

- **Indicator Code:** TLS

- **Maximum Points:** 15 (out of 250 TL points).

- **Scoring is proportion-based:**

  - 25% of graduates pursuing further studies = full score (15 points).

  - Scaled linearly if the percentage is between 0 and 25%.

  - 0% = 0 points.

## Special Note for Platinum+ Rating:

- At least 1% of graduates must be admitted into institutions ranked Top 500 in the latest QS World University Rankings.

## 3. Definition of "Further Studies"

- **Includes full-time, part-time, or distance-learning programmes leading to:** - Master’s degrees (e.g., M.Sc., M.A., MBA, M.Tech.)

  - Doctoral programmes (Ph.D., D.Litt., etc.)

  - Professional postgraduate diplomas (e.g., PGDM, PGDCA)

- Can be in India or abroad.

- Only students who formally enrolled and commenced the programme count.

## 4. Evidence Required

## Institutions must submit:

- List of graduates who enrolled in further studies within 12 months of graduation.

- **Proof of admission:**

  - Admission letters or enrolment certificates.

  - Email confirmations from the admitting institution.

  - For overseas universities, official offer letters with acceptance confirmation.

- **Tracking mechanism:**

  - Alumni follow-up surveys.

  - Departmental records.

  - Registrar’s data.

## 5. Scoring Examples

## Example 1 – High Performance

- **Graduating Class Size:** 400

- **Students in Further Studies (within 12 months):** 120 (30%)

| **Criterion / condition** | **Points / scoring** |
|---------------------------|----------------------|
| Above 25% threshold       | Full 15 points.      |

## Example 2 — Medium Performance

- **Graduating Class Size:** 500

- **Students in Further Studies:** 75 (15%)

- **Scaled score:** (15%/25%)×15=9 points.

## Example 3 —- Low Performance

- **Graduating Class Size:** 350

- **Students in Further Studies:** 20 (5.7%)

- **Scaled score:** (5.7%/25%)×15≈3.42 points.

## 6. Strategic Importance

- **Strong further study numbers suggest:**

  - High academic quality (students competitive for admissions).

  - Good counselling and mentoring for career progression. » Effective alumni engagement and tracking.

- Also reflects positively on Employability (EP).

## 7. Best Practices to Maximise Points Teaching & Learning (TL) and indirectly supports

1\. Create a dedicated Higher Education Cell for career counselling towards postgraduate admissions.

2\. Maintain alumni tracking systems with periodic follow-ups.

3\. Provide exam preparation support (e.g., GATE, GRE, GMAT, IELTS, UPSC).

4\. Build collaborations/MoUs with top Indian and foreign universities for direct entry or exchange programmes.

5\. Celebrate alumni achievements in further studies to inspire current students. # 19. QS I-GAUGE Core Criterion: Faculty Quality (FQ)

Core Criterion – Faculty Quality (FQ) — 200 Points

## 1. Purpose

- Measures the academic strength, qualifications, retention, satisfaction, development, and research output of faculty members.

- Recognises that quality teaching and research depend heavily on faculty credentials, stability, and exposure.

- Balances academic qualifications with professional/industry engagement and ongoing development.

## 2. Detailed Breakdown of Indicators

### FQ1 – Faculty Qualification (20 Points)

- **Objective:** Evaluate the highest qualifications of teaching staff.

- **Colleges:**

  - 15 points if 100% faculty have National Eligibility Test (NET) or State Eligibility Test (SET) qualification.

  - For professional colleges (Engineering/Medical/Architecture) → 100% with Master’s or Postgraduate degree.

- **Universities:**

  - 15 points if 100% faculty hold a PhD or equivalent terminal degree awarded by a recognised institution in India or abroad (AIU-recognised).

- **Bonus:** 5 points for % of faculty with International PhDs (scaled proportionally with 5% benchmark).

- **Why it Matters:** Higher qualifications generally correlate with deeper subject expertise, research ability, and academic leadership.

### FQ2 – Retention Rate (40 Points)

- **Objective:** Measure stability of the teaching workforce.

- **Scoring:**

| **Criterion / condition**       | **Points / scoring**                    |
|---------------------------------|-----------------------------------------|
| 70% faculty employed \> 3 years | full 40 points (scaled proportionally). |

- **Why it Matters:** High retention improves continuity in teaching, departmental stability, and long-term academic planning.

### FQ3 – Faculty Satisfaction (40 Points)

- **Objective:** Capture faculty perception of the institution as an employer.

- **Data Source:** Independent QS I-GAUGE faculty survey.

- **Scoring:**

| **Criterion / condition** | **Points / scoring** |
|---------------------------|----------------------|
| ≥ 90% satisfaction        | 40 points.           |
| ≤ 60% satisfaction        | 0 points.            |
| Between 60–90%            | proportional score.  |

- **Why it Matters:** Satisfied faculty are more engaged, productive, and committed to institutional growth.

### FQ4 – Industry or External Academic Exposure (40 Points)

- **Objective:** Evaluate faculty experience beyond the classroom.

- **Option 1:**

| **Criterion / condition**                                                                 | **Points / scoring** |
|-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|----------------------|
| % of faculty with ≥ 2 years’ work experience in non-academic organisations. 50% benchmark | scaled to 20 points. |

- **Option 2:**

  - % of faculty with consultancy or industrial projects as part of their academic work.

| **Criterion / condition** | **Points / scoring** |
|---------------------------|----------------------|
| 60% benchmark             | scaled to 40 points. |

- **Why it Matters:** External exposure strengthens industry relevance of teaching, supports applied research, and enhances employability focus.

### FQ5 – Faculty Development Programme (FDP) (40 Points)

- **Objective:** Measure ongoing faculty skill enhancement.

- **Scoring:**

  - % of faculty who have completed an FDP (as mandated by statutory authorities) in last 2 years.

| **Criterion / condition** | **Points / scoring** |
|---------------------------|----------------------|
| 50% benchmark             | scaled to 25 points. |

- **Why it Matters:** FDP participation ensures faculty are up-to-date with latest teaching methodologies, research trends, and technological tools.

### FQ6 — Publications per Faculty (20 Points)

- **Objective:** Measure research productivity at the individual faculty level.

- **Data Source:** Scopus-indexed publications over the last 5 years.

- **Scoring:**

| **Criterion / condition**    | **Points / scoring**  |
|------------------------------|-----------------------|
| **STEM:** 5 publications     | full points (scaled). |
| **Non-STEM:** 3 publications | full points (scaled). |
| **Mixed:** 4 publications    | full points (scaled). |

- **Why it Matters:** Demonstrates contribution to global academic knowledge and strengthens institutional research reputation.

## 3. Scoring Approach

- **Survey-Based Indicator:** FQ3 (Faculty Satisfaction).

- **Data-Based Indicators:** FQ1, FQ2, FQ4, FQ5, FQ6.

- **Evidence Sources:**

  - Faculty qualification records and certificates.

  - HR retention data.

  - FDP attendance records.

  - Scopus/Research database publication lists.

  - Proof of industry projects or consultancy.

## 4. Importance in QS I-GAUGE

- Faculty Quality is the second-highest weighted core category after Teaching & Learning.

- Strong performance in FQ supports higher Teaching & Learning scores and improves institutional prestige.

- Directly linked to research quality, employability outcomes, and academic innovation.

# 20. Faculty Qualification

## 1. Purpose

- Evaluates the academic qualifications of teaching staff to ensure that the institution meets or exceeds national and global academic standards.

- Recognises institutions where faculty possess highest-level degrees in their disciplines and, in some cases, international exposure.

- Reflects the quality of instruction, research capability, and global outlook.

## 2. QS I-GAUGE Scoring Logic (FQ1)

- **Indicator Code:** FQ1

- **Maximum Points:** 20 (out of 200 Faculty Quality points)

- **Divided into two scoring components:**

Component

Colleges

Universities

International PhD

Criteria Points 100% faculty with NET/SET qualification (for non-professional; s courses). For Engineering/Medical/Architecture colleges, 100% pointsfaculty with Master’s or Postgraduate degree. 100% faculty with Ph.D. or equivalent terminal degree from an 15 institution recognised by a statutory authority in India or by AIU forygintsforeign Phos.

Additional 5 points for the proportion of faculty with ans internationally awarded Ph.D. scaled from 5% = full 5 points down to points0%.

## 3. Key Definitions

- **NET (National Eligibility Test) / SET (State Eligibility Test):** National or state-level exams qualifying a candidate for Assistant Professor roles in India (UGC/CSIR standards).

- **Terminal Degree:** The highest academic degree available in a particular field (Ph.D., D.Litt., etc.).

- **International Ph.D.:** Awarded by a foreign institution recognised by AIU; may be full time or joint doctoral programmes.

## 4. Evidence Required

## Institutions must submit:

- Faculty roster with name, designation, department, and qualification.

- Degree certificates (Ph.D./Master’s/NET/SET proof).

- Recognition documents for foreign degrees (AIU equivalence).

- Percentage calculation sheet for compliance check.

## 5. Scoring Examples

## Example 1 – University

| **Criterion / condition**         | **Points / scoring**            |
|-----------------------------------|---------------------------------|
| 100% faculty hold Ph.D.s          | 15 points.                      |
| 8% of faculty hold foreign Ph.D.s | (8%/5%)×5=5 points (maxed out). |

- Total FQ1 Score = 20/20.

## Example 2 – College

| **Criterion / condition**                       | **Points / scoring** |
|-------------------------------------------------|----------------------|
| All faculty have NET/SET or required PG degrees | 15 points.           |
| No foreign Ph.D.s                               | 0 points.            |

- Total FQ1 Score = 15/20.

## Example 3 – Partial Compliance

- **University with 90% Ph.D.qualified faculty → Scaled proportionally:**

(90%/100%)×15=13.5 points.

| **Criterion / condition** | **Points / scoring** |
|---------------------------|----------------------|
| 2% foreign Ph.D.s         | (2%/5%)×5=2 points.  |

- Total FQ1 Score = 15.5/20.

## 6. Strategic Importance

- High faculty qualification percentage strengthens Faculty Quality (FQ) score and contributes to overall QS I-GAUGE rating.

- **Institutions with strong Ph.D. presence often:**

  - Deliver more research-driven teaching.

  - Publish more in indexed journals (linked to FQ6 — Publications per faculty). Attract international collaborations and grants.

## 7. Best Practices to Maximise Points

1\. Recruit faculty with Ph.D.s or encourage current faculty to pursue doctoral studies.

2\. Sponsor faculty exchange programmes to increase foreign Ph.D. representation.

3\. Maintain accurate HR records with all qualifications documented.

## 4. Recognise and promote faculty academic achievements to inspire peers. Faculty with Php/ Terminal Degree (count)

# 21. Retention Rate

## 1. Purpose

- Measures faculty stability and continuity in an institution.

- **A high retention rate indicates:**

  - Job satisfaction among faculty.

  - Organisational stability in academic delivery.

  - Lower recruitment and training costs.

- Longserving faculty members tend to understand the institution’s culture and pedagogy better, benefiting teaching and research quality.

## 2. QS I-GAUGE Scoring Logic (FQ2)

- **Indicator Code:** FQ2

- **Maximum Points:** 40 (out of 200 Faculty Quality points).

- **Definition:** Percentage of faculty members who have been employed at the institution for more than three years.

## Scoring formula:

- **Full Score (40 points):** Achieved when 70% or more of total faculty have been employed for over three years.

- If less than 70%, the score is proportionally scaled down to zero.

## Formula:

Score=(% Faculty with \>3 years tenure)/70%))x40

(If *percentage* exceeds 70%, it is *capped* at 70% for *scoring* purposes.)

## 3. Evidence Required

## Institutions must provide:

1\. Faculty service records (with joining dates).

## 2. A summary table showing:

- Total number of faculty.

- Number with more than three years’ continuous service.

- Percentage calculation.

3\. Supporting HR/payroll records for verification.

## 4. Example Calculations

## Example 1 – High Retention

- **Total faculty:** 100

- **Faculty with \>3 years service:** 75

- % Retention = 75/100x100=75%

- Since ≥70%, full 40 points awarded.

## Example 2 – Medium Retention

- **Total faculty:** 100

- **Faculty with \>3 years service:** 50

- % Retention = 50%50\\50%

- Score = 50/70x40=28.57 points.

## Example 3 —- Low Retention

- **Total faculty:** 50

- **Faculty with \>3 years service:** 20

- % Retention = 40%40\\40%

- Score = 40/70x40=22.86 points.

## 5. Strategic Importance

- Directly linked to faculty satisfaction (FQ3) and academic stability.

- **Institutions with high turnover often face:**

  - Disruption in teaching continuity.

  - Reduced research momentum.

  - Increased administrative costs in recruitment/training.

## 6. Best Practices to Maximise Points

1\. Retention-focused HR policies — competitive pay, promotion opportunities, transparent appraisals.

2\. Faculty engagement programmes — mentoring, leadership roles, research incentives.

3\. Professional growth support — funding for conferences, workshops, higher education.

## 4. Work-life balance initiatives — flexible schedules, wellness programs, housing facilities. Unit

# 22. Faculty Satisfaction

## 1. Purpose

- Measures how satisfied faculty members are with the institution as a place of work.

- **Focuses on:**

  - Work environment

  - Career growth opportunities

  - Support for research/teaching

  - Institutional policies & governance

- High faculty satisfaction generally correlates with better teaching quality, student outcomes, and retention rates (FQ2).

## 2. QS I-GAUGE Scoring Logic (FQ3)

- **Indicator Code:** FQ3

- **Maximum Points:** 40 (out of 200 Faculty Quality points).

- **Definition:** Proportion of faculty members who indicate satisfaction in the Faculty Survey.

## Scoring Scale:

- **Full Score (40 points):** Achieved when 90% or more of faculty report being satisfied.

- **No Score (0 points):** If 60% or less report being satisfied.

- **Proportional Score:** If satisfaction percentage is between 60% and 90%, the score is scaled linearly.

## Formula:

Score={0,

(Satisfaction–60)/(90–60)×40, 40,

## 3. Survey Process & Evidence

if Satisfaction≤60% if 60%-\<Satisfaction\<90% if Satisfaction≥90%}

- Based on the Independent Faculty Survey conducted by QS I-GAUGE.

- **Minimum response threshold:**

  - At least 60% of total faculty population must respond for the survey results to be valid.

- **Questions usually include:**

  - Satisfaction with work environment.

  - Opportunities for professional growth.

  - Administrative support.

  - Research and teaching facilities.

  - Work-life balance.

- Institutions cannot self-report; the survey is conducted online directly with faculty.

## 4. Example Calculations

## Example 1 – High Satisfaction

- **Faculty surveyed:** 100

- **Responses marked “Satisfied” or “Very Satisfied”:** 92

| **Criterion / condition** | **Points / scoring** |
|---------------------------|----------------------|
| Satisfaction rate = 92%   | Full 40 points.      |

## Example 2 — Medium Satisfaction

- **Faculty surveyed:** 80

- **Satisfied:** 64

- Satisfaction rate = 80%

- Score = (80–60)/(90–60)×40=20/30×40=26.67 points.

## Example 3 — Low Satisfaction

- **Faculty surveyed:** 70

- **Satisfied:** 40

| **Criterion / condition** | **Points / scoring** |
|---------------------------|----------------------|
| Satisfaction rate = 57%   | 0 points.            |

## 5. Strategic Importance

- Directly impacts faculty retention, research productivity, and institutional reputation.

- Links with FQ2 (Retention Rate) — dissatisfaction leads to higher turnover.

- Higher satisfaction attracts better faculty talent.

## 6. Best Practices to Maximise Points

1\. Transparent governance – clear policies on promotions, workload, and research support.

2\. Professional development – training, funding for conferences, and sabbaticals.

3\. Recognition & incentives — awards, performance bonuses, public appreciation.

4\. Infrastructure & facilities — high-quality classrooms, labs, research centres.

## 5. Feedback loops – regular anonymous faculty surveys with follow-up actions. unit survey

# 23. Work Experience Outside Academia / External Exposure

## 1. Purpose

This indicator measures how much real-world exposure faculty members have beyond purely academic teaching roles.

It acknowledges that industry experience or practical consultancy work enriches teaching quality, makes curriculum more industry-relevant, and improves student employability.

## 2. Two Alternate Pathways

## Institutions can claim points via one of two routes:

## Option 1 – Work Experience Outside Academia

- Percentage of faculty with at least two years of part-time or full-time work in an external non-academic organisation.

- **Examples:**

  - Engineers working in R&D labs or manufacturing companies before joining academia.

  - Lawyers in legal practice before becoming law faculty.

  - Doctors with hospital practice before teaching in a medical college.

- **Scoring:** 50% faculty with such experience = full 40 points (scaled down proportionally).

## Formula:

Score=% Faculty with ≥ 2 years non-academic experience/50%x40

(Max 40 points)

## Option 2 — External Exposure as Part of Academia

- Percentage of faculty engaged in external consultancy projects or industry-linked academic projects.

- **Consultancy could be:**

  - Funded research for companies.

  - Industry-sponsored training programs.

  - Collaborative product development.

- **Scoring:** 60% faculty with such exposure = full 40 points (scaled down proportionally). Formula:

Score=% Faculty involved in consultancy/industry projects/60%×40

## 3. Evidence Requirements

## The institution must provide:

- **Option 1:**

  - Verified faculty CVs showing non-academic experience.

  - Work experience letters from companies.

- **Option 2:**

  - Consultancy/project contracts or MOUs.

  - Proof of project completion (reports, invoices, acknowledgements).

- Minimum faculty population to be considered = entire teaching staff (not just one department).

## 4. Example Scenarios

## Scenario A – Outside Work Experience

- **Total faculty:** 100

- **Faculty with ≥ 2 years non-academic experience:** 35%

- Score = 35/50x40=28 points.

## Scenario B– Consultancy Projects

- **Total faculty:** 80

- **Faculty with consultancy/industry project exposure:** 48%

- Score = 48/60x40=32 points.

## 5. Strategic Importance

- Faculty with industry exposure can integrate case studies, applied examples, and problem–solving skills into teaching.

- Helps bridge the academic-industry gap, improving QS I-GAUGE’s Employability (EP) score indirectly.

- Encourages industry collaborations for internships, placements, and joint research.

## 6. Best Practices to Maximise Points

1\. Recruit faculty with relevant industry experience in key departments.

2\. Encourage faculty participation in consultancy or industrial projects annually.

3\. Maintain an indus– try partnership database to connect faculty with real-world assignments.

4\. Track and document all such engagements for QS I-GAUGE submission. Faculty with Option1 Consultancy/Industr % (Nonial Projects (count) Acad Exp)

# 24. Faculty Development Programme (FDP)

The Faculty Development Programme (FDP) indicator evaluates how well an institution invests in upskilling and reskilling its faculty members through structured training programs. The idea is to ensure that teaching staff stay updated with evolving pedagogy, technology, research methods, and industry trends.

## 2. Indicator Definition

- Measures the proportion of faculty who have undergone an FDP within the last two years.

- FDP must be mandated or recognised by the respective statutory authority (e.g., UGC, AICTE, MCI, BCI, PCI).

- Applies to all departments in the institution.

## 3. Scoring Model

- **Full score:** If 50% or more of faculty have completed an FDP in the last two years, the institution gets 25 points (scaled proportionally).

## Formula:

Score=%Faculty who completed FDP in last 2 years/50%×25

(Maximum = 25 points)

## 4. What Counts as an FDP

- **Recognised FDPs include:**

  - AICTE/UGC-approved short-term training programmes.

  - Quality Improvement Programmes (QIP).

  - Teaching and Learning enhancement workshops.

  - MOOCs for pedagogy (e.g., SWAYAM, NPTEL FDPs).

  - Industry–led faculty training with a structured curriculum.

  - Subject-specific research methodology programs.

- **Duration:** Usually minimum 1 week or 30+ instructional hours (can vary by authority).

## 5. Evidence Requirements

## Institutions must provide:

- Certificates of FDP completion for each participating faculty.

- FDP programme agenda/schedule.

- Faculty attendance list.

- Statutory approval/recognition of FDP.

- Consolidated FDP participation report signed by the head of institution.

## 6. Example Scenarios

## Scenario A – High FDP Coverage

- **Total faculty:** 120

- **Faculty attended FDP in last 2 years:** 72 (60%)

- Score = 60/50%25=25 points (full score).

## Scenario B— Moderate FDP Coverage

- **Total faculty:** 80

- **Faculty attended FDP:** 28 (35%)

- Score = 35/50%x25=17.5 points.

## 7. Strategic Importance

- **High FDP participation improves:**

  - Teaching Quality (TL)

  - Faculty Satisfaction (FQ3)

  - Employability (EP) through updated curriculum delivery.

- Statutory bodies often make FDP participation mandatory for promotions.

## 8. Best Practices to Maximise Points

1\. Plan an annual FDP calendar covering subject-specific and pedagogy—related training.

2\. Ensure every faculty attends at least one FDP every 2 years.

3\. Collaborate with reputed organisations (IITs, NITs, industry leaders) for FDP delivery.

4\. Maintain a centralised FDP participation record for easy QS I-GAUGE submission. unit (Dept/School /Institution) Total

# 25. Publications per Faculty

## 1. Purpose

The Publications per Faculty metric evaluates research productivity at the individual faculty level by measuring how many peer-reviewed journal papers indexed in Scopus® each faculty member produces on average over the last five years.

## It is intended to:

- Encourage a strong research culture.

- Promote knowledge dissemination through reputed journals.

- Benchmark faculty research performance against international norms.

## 2. Indicator Definition

- **Formula:**

Publications per Faculty=Total Scopus

## indexed publications in last 5 years/Total number of faculty members

- The “faculty” count here refers to full-time faculty members only.

- Only Scopus® indexed publications count — other publications (like UGC Care journals not in Scopus) are not considered.

## 3. Scoring Model

## QS I-GAUGE applies discipline-specific scaling:

Institution Type Publications Threshold (last 5 years) for Full Score Maximum Points

STEM 5 publications per faculty 20 points Mixed 4 publications per faculty 20 points Non-STEM 3 publications per faculty 20 points

- If your per-faculty publications are below the threshold, points are scaled proportionally.

- **Formula for scaled score:**

Score=Actual Publications per Faculty/Threshold Publications×20

(Capped at 20)

## 4. Example Calculations

## STEM Institution

- **Faculty:** 100

- **Total Scopus-indexed publications in last 5 years:** 400

- **Publications per faculty:** 400/100=4

- **Threshold:** 5

- Score = 45×20=16 points.

## Non-STEM Institution

- **Faculty:** 50

- **Total publications:** 100

- **Publications per faculty:** 100/50=2

- **Threshold:** 3

- Score = 2/3×20~13.33 points.

## 5. Data Validation Requirements

## To claim points, institutions must provide:

- Scopus® publication list (CSV/Excel export from Scopus).

- Faculty roster for the period under consideration.

- Link to Scopus Author IDs for verification.

- Evidence that all publications fall within the last 5 years.

## 6. Strategic Importance

- Boosts institutional reputation in research rankings.

- Contributes indirectly to Employability (EP) via innovation and industry collaboration.

- Supports Research (RE) if chosen as an Advanced Criterion.

## 7. Best Practices to Improve Score

1\. Faculty Research Support — Provide seed grants, paid access to databases, and dedicated research time.

2\. Collaboration Networks — Encourage faculty to collaborate internationally to increase high-impact publications.

3\. Publication Workshops — Train faculty in research methodology and manuscript writing.

4\. Incentive Schemes — Offer financial/recognition incentives for publishing in Scopusindexed journals.

## 5. Track Progress — Maintain a Scopus publication dashboard updated quarterly. piscipline Type (sTEM/Non
