# 26. QS I-GAUGE Core Criterion: Employability (EP)

Core Criterion —- Employability (EP) — 200 Points

## 1. Purpose

- Evaluates how well the institution prepares students for employment and career success.

- Focuses on campus employer engagement, career guidance, student/alumni satisfaction, and extracurricular involvement that supports workplace readiness.

## 2. Detailed Breakdown of Indicators

### EP1 – Campus Employer Presence / Career Counselling (40 points)

- **Option 1:**

  - Measures the number of hiring companies visiting campus in the last 12 months.

| **Criterion / condition**                                                           | **Points / scoring** |
|-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|----------------------|
| **Benchmark:** 2% of total student population or 200 companies (whichever is lower) | scaled to 40 points. |

- Mandatory for Engineering and Management–focused institutions.

<!-- -->

- **Option 2:** (Alternative for other institutions)

  - **Career counselling services scored at 10 points each for:**

1\. Having at least one full-time/part-time career counsellor and a Career Development Cell.

2\. Conducting interview/training/CV writing workshops in the last 12 months.

3\. Providing an online career portal with job access.

## 4. Hosting at least one on-campus career fair in the last 12 months. EP2 —- Employment Rate (40 points)

- % of graduates employed within 12 months (excluding further study).

| **Criterion / condition** | **Points / scoring** |
|---------------------------|----------------------|
| **Benchmark:** 90%        | scaled to 40 points. |

- Lower rates proportionally reduce score.

### EP3 – Student Satisfaction with Career Services (30 points)

- Based on independent QS I-GAUGE student survey.

| **Criterion / condition**       | **Points / scoring** |
|---------------------------------|----------------------|
| **Benchmark:** 75% satisfaction | scaled to 30 points. |

### EP4 – Alumni Satisfaction with Institution (30 points)

- Based on independent QS I-GAUGE alumni survey.

| **Criterion / condition**       | **Points / scoring** |
|---------------------------------|----------------------|
| **Benchmark:** 75% satisfaction | scaled to 30 points. |

### EP5 – Alumni Satisfaction with Career Success (30 points)

- Based on alumni survey responses on career success after graduation.

| **Criterion / condition** | **Points / scoring** |
|---------------------------|----------------------|
| **Benchmark:** 75%        | scaled to 30 points. |

### EP6 – Extracurricular Activities Participation (30 points)

- % of students involved in clubs, societies, or associations on campus.

| **Criterion / condition**        | **Points / scoring** |
|----------------------------------|----------------------|
| **Benchmark:** 30% participation | scaled to 30 points. |

## 3. Scoring & Data Sources

- **Survey-Based Indicators:** EP3, EP4, EP5.

- **Data-Based Indicators:** EP1, EP2, EP6.

- **Evidence Required:**

  - Employer visit records.

  - Career services event logs.

  - Student/alumni participation lists.

  - Employment placement reports.

# 27. Campus Employer Presence (or Career Counselling Services)

## 1. Purpose

## This indicator measures how effectively an institution:

- Connects students with employment opportunities via on-campus recruitment (placement drives),

## OR

- Provides career counselling services to guide employability skills.

It recognises that not all institutions have heavy engineering/management colleges), so career counselling services can be an alternative pathway to score here.

## 2. Two Possible Approaches

## A. Campus Employer Presence

- Counts the number of unique hiring companies that visited the campus in the last 12 months to recruit students.

- **Target threshold:**

  - At least 2% of the total student population

  - OR 200 companies (whichever is lower)

- Full score is awarded if this threshold is met.

- If fewer companies visit, the score is scaled proportionally.

## Formula:

Score=Number of Hiring Companies/Target Number×40

(Maximum 40 points)

## B. Career Counselling Services (Alternative Option)

If employer presence is low, institutions can score points by providing structured career services.

## Points are distributed as follows (10 points each):

## 1. Dedicated Career Counsellor(s)

- At least one full-time or part-time counsellor.

- Must have a Career Development Cell/Office on campus.

## 2. Career Workshops

- Number of interview skills, training sessions, or CV writing workshops conducted in the last 12 months.

## 3. Online Career Portal

- Institution maintains/facilitates an online portal with job postings, internship opportunities, and recruiter access.

## 4. Career Fair

- At least one on-campus career fair in the last 12 months involving multiple employers.

| **Criterion / condition**           | **Points / scoring** |
|-------------------------------------|----------------------|
| If all four components are in place | Full 40 points.      |

## 3. Scoring

- **Campus employer presence route:** Score is based on recruiter count relative to threshold.

- **Career counselling route:** 10 points per valid sub-indicator, up to 40 points.

## 4. Example Calculations

## Example 1 – Campus Employer Presence

- **Total students:** 2,000 students in job preparation and

## recruiter inflow (especially non

- Target companies = min(2%×2000,200)=40

- **Actual companies:** 30

- Score = 3040×40=30 points.

## Example 2 – Career Counselling Services

| **Criterion / condition**   | **Points / scoring** |
|-----------------------------|----------------------|
| Dedicated Career Counsellor | Yes (10 points)      |
| 5 Career Workshops          | Yes (10 points)      |
| Online Career Portal        | Yes (10 points)      |
| Career Fair                 | No (0 points)        |

- Total Score = 30 points.

## 5. Data Validation Requirements

## Institutions must provide:

- **For employer presence:**

  - Placement records with company names, dates, and number of students placed.

- **For career counselling:**

  - Official circulars, event reports, photos, or web links proving activities. Career portal screenshots and URLs.

## 6. Strategic Importance

- This metric strongly influences Employability perception among prospective students and recruiters.

- Enhances institution’s industry connect and brand visibility.

- Helps improve alumni career outcomes (linked to EP4 and EP5).

## 7. Best Practices to Improve Score

## 1. Strengthen Industry Relations — internships, and placement drives. Regularly invite recruiters for guest lectures,

2\. Dedicated Placement Officers — Hire industry-experienced placement coordinators.

3\. Alumni Involvement – Leverage alumni working in reputed companies to bring in recruiters.

4\. Hybrid Approach — Maintain both employer visits and strong career counselling infrastructure to maximise points.

## 5. Annual Career Calendar — Publish a structured placement/career events calendar. unit

# 28. Employment Rate

## 1. Purpose

This metric evaluates how successfully an institution places its graduates into jobs within 12 months of graduation, excluding those who pursue further studies. **It** reflects:

- The institution’s industry relevance

- Effectiveness of placement activities

- Quality of academic programs in meeting employer needs

## 2. Indicator Definition

- Employment Rate (%) =

Number of graduates employed within 12 months/Total graduates excluding those in further studies×100

## 3. Scoring Method

- **Full Points:** 40 points if 90% or more graduates are employed within 12 months.

- **Minimum Score:** 0 points if ≤ 50% employment rate.

- **Scaling:** If the employment rate is between 50% and 90%, the score is proportionally scaled.

## 4. Example Calculations

## Example 1 – High Performance

- **Total graduates:** 800

- **Graduates pursuing further studies:** 100

- **Graduates employed within 12 months:** 630

- Employment rate = 630/(800-100)×100=90%

- Score = 40 points (Full score).

## Example 2 — Moderate Performance

- **Total graduates:** 500

- **Further studies:** 50

- **Employed within 12 months:** 300

- Employment rate = 300/(500–50)×100=66.67%

- Score =(66.67–50)/40×40=16.67

## 5. Data Validation Requirements

## Institutions must submit:

- Verified placement records (signed by placement cell head or registrar)

- Employer offer letters or appointment confirmations

- Alumni follow-up surveys or tracer studies for graduates employed off-campus

- Exclusion list of graduates pursuing further studies (with admission proofs)

## 6. Strategic Importance

- A high employment rate signals strong institutional reputation among recruiters.

- Boosts the institution’s QS I-GAUGE Employability badge score.

- Directly influences student enrolment decisions.

## 7. Best Practices to Improve Score

1\. Strengthen Corporate Tie-ups — Regular MoUs with industry for internships and recruitment.

2\. Dedicated Placement Training —- Resume writing, mock interviews, and group discussion practice.

3\. Industry-Centric Curriculum — Embed employer feedback into program design.

4\. Alumni Career Network — Use alumni to refer current students for openings.

## 5. Placement Tracking System — Maintain real-time placement dashboards to monitor targets. unit

# 29. Student Satisfaction with Career Services

## 1. Purpose

This metric measures how satisfied current students are with the career guidance and support services provided by the institution.

## It reflects:

- Quality and relevance of career development programs

- Accessibility and effectiveness of placement support

- Student perception of how well the institution prepares them for employment

## 2. Indicator Definition

- Collected via the Independent Student Survey conducted as part of QS I-GAUGE rating.

- **Students are asked to rate their satisfaction with on-campus career guidance services, including:**

  - Placement cell effectiveness

  - Career counselling sessions

  - Internship opportunities

  - Job fairs and recruitment drives

  - Access to career resources (portals, job boards, employer talks)

## 3. Scoring Method

- **Maximum Points:** 30 points

- **Full Score (30 points):** Achieved if ≥ 75% of student respondents indicate satisfaction.

- **Minimum Score (0 points):** If ≤ 50% satisfaction.

- **Proportional Scaling:** Between 50% and 75% satisfaction, points are awarded proportionally.

(for satisfaction rates between 50% and 75%)

## 4. Example Calculations

## Example 1 – High Satisfaction

- **Number of valid student survey responses:** 1,200

- **Students satisfied:** 960 (80%)

- Satisfaction % = 960/1200×100=80%

- Score = 30 points (Full score).

## Example 2 — Moderate Satisfaction

- **Responses:** 800

- **Students satisfied:** 520 (65%)

- Satisfaction % = 65%

- Score = (65–50)/25×30=18 points.

## 5. Data Validation Requirements

- **Must meet minimum survey participation threshold:**

≥ 30% of total student population.

- QS I-GAUGE conducts and validates the survey to ensure integrity.

- Responses must represent all years and courses fairly.

## 6. Strategic Importance

- Directly impacts Employability (EP) category badge.

- Indicates how well an institution is aligning career services with student needs.

- High scores strengthen institutional marketing to prospective students.

## 7. Best Practices to Improve Score

1\. Enhance Career Counselling — Provide personalised one-on-one guidance.

2\. Improve Internship Support — Partner with industry for structured internship programs.

3\. Regular Skill Workshops — Resume building, LinkedIn branding, interview preparation.

4\. Digital Career Portal — Allow students to access jobs, internships, and company profiles online.

## 5. Feedback Loops — Collect regular student feedback on placement activities and act on suggestions. unit

# 30. Alumni Satisfaction with Institution

## 1. Purpose

This metric measures how satisfied alumni are with their overall institutional experience after graduation.

## It focuses on:

- Quality of education they received

- Support services during their time as students

- Value of the degree in their personal and professional life

This is important because alumni opinions are often more reflective of long-term benefits compared to current students.

## 2. Indicator Definition

- Collected via the Independent Alumni Survey conducted as part of QS I-GAUGE.

- **Alumni are asked to rate their satisfaction with the institution based on:** - Academic quality

  - Faculty support

  - Industry exposure during studies

  - Campus facilities & infrastructure

  - Networking opportunities

  - Institutional reputation

## 3. Scoring Method

- **Maximum Points:** 30 points

- **Full Score (30 points):** Achieved if ≥ 75% of alumni respondents indicate satisfaction.

- **Minimum Score (0 points):** If ≤ 50% satisfaction.

- **Proportional Scaling:** Between 50% and proportionally.

## 4. Example Calculations

## Example 1 – High Alumni Satisfaction

- **Valid alumni survey responses:** 300

- **Alumni satisfied:** 240 (80%)

- Satisfaction % = 240/300×100=80%

- Score = 30 points (Full score).

## Example 2 — Moderate Alumni Satisfaction

- **Responses:** 250

- **Alumni satisfied:** 150 (60%)

- Satisfaction % = 60%

- Score = (60–50)/25×30=12 points.

## 5. Data Validation Requirements

- Minimum survey

Alumni survey responses must be at least 10% of the current total student population.

- Alumni surveyed should represent different graduating batches, ideally spanning at least 3–5 years.

- QS I-GAUGE ensures survey integrity and unbiased sampling.

## 6. Strategic Importance

- **Strong alumni satisfaction supports:**

  - Institutional branding (word-of-mouth, rankings, global recognition) o Placement partnerships (alumni in industry often help with recruitment) Fundraising and alumni donations

75% satisfaction, points are awarded

## participation threshold:

- A key differentiator when comparing institutions in QS I-GAUGE scoring.

## 7. Best Practices to Improve Score

1\. Strengthen Alumni Relations — Regular events, networking meets, and recognition programs.

2\. Showcase Alumni Achievements — Promote success stories in marketing materials.

3\. Provide Continuous Learning Opportunities — Short courses, certifications for alumni.

4\. Active Alumni Office — Dedicated staff to maintain alumni engagement.

5\. # 31. Alumni Satisfaction with Career Success

## 1. Purpose

This indicator measures how satisfied alumni are with their career progress after graduating from the institution.

It assesses the long-term professional impact of the education received, not just the immediate job placement.

## It reflects:

- Career growth opportunities

- Industry relevance of skills learned

- Professional recognition and stability

- Salary growth and job satisfaction

## 2. Indicator Definition

- Captured through the Independent Alumni Survey in the QS I-GAUGE process.

- **Alumni are asked to evaluate their career success on factors such as:** Job position and role alignment with their degree

  - Career advancement opportunities

  - Salary growth since graduation

  - Professional skill application in the workplace

  - Overall career stability and satisfaction

## 3. Scoring Method

- **Maximum Points:** 30 points

- **Full Score (30 points):** Awarded if ≥ 75% of surveyed alumni report career satisfaction.

- **Minimum Score (0 points):** If ≤ 50% career satisfaction.

- **Proportional Scaling:** Between 50% and 75%, points are awarded proportionally. (only applicable when satisfaction is between 50% and 75%)

## 4. Example Calculations

## Example 1 – High Career Success Satisfaction

- **Alumni survey respondents:** 300

- **Alumni satisfied with career:** 240 (80%)

- Career Satisfaction % = 240/300×100=80%

- Score = 30 points (Full).

## Example 2 — Moderate Career Success Satisfaction

- **Alumni respondents:** 200

- **Alumni satisfied with career:** 120 (60%)

- Career Satisfaction % = 60%

- Score = (60–50)/25×30=12 points.

## 5. Data Validation Requirements

- **Minimum participation threshold:** Alumni survey responses should be at least 10% of the current total student population.

- Responses must represent alumni from various graduating batches to avoid bias.

- Data integrity and authenticity are verified by QS I-GAUGE.

## 6. Strategic Importance

## High alumni career satisfaction reflects:

- Quality and relevance of academic programs

- Strong industry connections and placements

- Effective career guidance and mentoring

- Brand reputation in the job market

It is closely linked to the institution’s ability to attract future students and build trust with employers.

## 7. Best Practices to Improve Score

1\. Industry-Aligned Curriculum – Regularly update programs to meet evolving job market demands.

2\. Alumni Career Support – Provide lifelong access to career counselling and job portals.

3\. Networking Events — Facilitate industry networking and alumni–employer meets.

4\. Alumni Mentorship Programs — Pair fresh graduates with established alumni for career guidance.

5\. Showcase Alumni Career Achievements — Publicly recognize promotions, awards, and milestones. atu Alu Grad Program mn mni uatio Graduat i ID Nam n ed From e Year

# 32. Extracurricular Activities

## 1. Purpose

The Extracurricular Activities indicator measures the extent of student engagement in nonacademic pursuits within the campus ecosystem.

## It evaluates the institution’s ability to promote:

- Holistic student development beyond academics

- Leadership and teamwork skills

- Creativity and innovation

- Networking and soft skills essential for career success

Strong extracurricular participation is seen as a positive predictor of employability, as employers often value well-rounded graduates with diverse experiences.

## 2. Indicator Definition

- Percentage of students actively involved in Clubs, Student Societies, or Associations within the institution.

- **These may include:**

  - Academic and technical clubs (coding club, robotics, etc.)

  - Arts, music, and cultural groups

  - Sports teams and athletic associations

  - Volunteer groups, community service teams

  - Entrepreneurship and innovation cells

  - Debate and public speaking forums

## 3. Scoring Method

- **Maximum Points:** 30 points

- **Full Score (30 points):** If ≥ 30% of the total student population are members of at least one club or student association.

- **Minimum Score (0 points):** If ≤ 10% student participation.

- **Proportional Scaling:** Between 10% and 30%, points are awarded proportionally. Formula:

Score=(Participation %–10)/20×30

(only applicable when participation is between 10% and 30%)

## 4. Example Calculations

## Example 1 – High Participation

- **Total student population:** 3,000

- **Students in clubs:** 1,050 (35%)

- Participation % = 1050/3000×100=35%

- Score = 30 points (Full score).

## Example 2 — Moderate Participation

- **Total students:** 2,000

- **Students in clubs:** 300 (15%)

- Participation % = 15%

- Score = (15–10)/20×30=7.5 points.

## 5. Data Validation Requirements

- Membership lists from student clubs/associations must be provided.

- Should represent active participation, not just sign-ups.

- Participation count must be from the last academic year.

## 6. Strategic Importance

## High extracurricular participation correlates with:

- Better student engagement and retention

- Higher soft skill acquisition

- Increased employability through leadership roles and teamwork experience

- Institutional branding through competitions, festivals, and outreach events

## 7. Best Practices to Improve Score

1\. Diverse Range of Clubs — Cater to academic, cultural, sports, and social interests.

2\. Mandatory Club Participation — Encourage or integrate into curriculum credit hours.

3\. Institutional Support — Provide budgets, facilities, and mentoring for student–led activities.

4\. Recognition & Awards – Celebrate active members with certificates, transcripts, and awards.

5\. External Exposure – Facilitate participation in inter-college and national–level competitions.

Extracurricular Activities — Tracker

## Purpose:

- Track extracurricular activities (sports, culture, clubs, fests) with participants and outcomes.

## How to use:

1\) Enter one row per activity in Activity Data.

2\) Capture details (title, type, department /club, date, participants, outcomes, proof).

3\) Use dropdowns for Activity Type, Frequency, Proof Type.

4\) Provide URLs/screenshots as evidence.

5\) Use Summary for KPIs and Activity_Matrix for year x type analysis.

## Key definitions:

- **Activity Type:** Sports, Cultural, Technical, Social Service, Club, Other.

- **Frequency:** One–time, Monthly, Semester-wise, Annual, Other.

- **Proof:** Photos, Report, Certificate, URL, Other.

# 33. QS I-GAUGE Core Criterion: Diversity and Accessibility (DA)

Core Criterion — Diversity and Accessibility (DA) — 150 Points

## 1. Purpose

- Measures the extent of cultural, regional, gender, and socio-economic diversity in the institution.

- Evaluates policies and practices that ensure equal opportunities for students and faculty regardless of background or ability.

- Recognises institutions that support inclusion, accessibility, and equitable participation.

## 2. Detailed Breakdown of Indicators

### DA1 – Out of State Students (25 points)

- **Scoring:** 1 point per 1% of students from a state or union territory other than the institution’s home location.

- **Why it Matters:** Encourages student mobility and cross-cultural exchange. DA2 – International Diversity (20 points)

- **Students:** 15 points if 10 nationalities represented, scaled down proportionally.

- **Faculty:** 5 points if 5 nationalities represented, scaled down proportionally.

- **Why it Matters:** Promotes a global learning environment.

### DA3 – National Diversity for Students (20 points)

- **Option 1:** Number of states/UTs represented among students (10 scaled to 5 points).

- **Option 2:** If local-state focused, number of districts represented (10 scaled down to 0).

- **Why it Matters:** Reflects geographical diversity in student intake.

### DA4 – Expenditure on Scholarships & Bursaries (15 points)

| **Criterion / condition**                                         | **Points / scoring** |
|-------------------------------------------------------------------|----------------------|
| At least 1% of total revenue spent on non-refundable scholarships | full score.          |

- Lower percentages scored proportionally.

## DAS – Student Scholarships / Fee Waiver (10 points)

- % of students receiving merit-based scholarships or tuition waivers.

| **Criterion / condition** | **Points / scoring** |
|---------------------------|----------------------|
| **Benchmark:** 10%        | full score.          |

## DAG – Differently Abled (20 points)

- **10 points each for:**

1\. Number of differently-abled students enrolled.

2\. Institutional policy to support differently-abled students.

## DA?7 – Gender Ratio (20 points)

| **Criterion / condition**                                | **Points / scoring**                    |
|----------------------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------|
| **Students:** 2:3 male-tofemale ratio scaled down to 1:1 | 10 points (full points if all- female). |
| **Faculty:** 80% female                                  | 10 points, scaled down to 50%.          |

### DA8 – Financial Equality (10 points)

- **5 points each for:**

1\. Policy to provide scholarships based on socio-economic status.

## 2. Number of students availing financial aid on income-based criteria. DAS — Regional & Linguistic Diversity of

## Faculty (10 points)

- **5 points each for:**

1\. Number of distinct native states/UTs among faculty (15 scaled to 5).

2\. Number of distinct mother tongues spoken by faculty (15 scaled to 5).

## 3. Scoring & Data Sources

- **Data-Based Indicators:** All DA indicators are institutional data-driven.

- **Evidence Required:**

  - Student & faculty demographic lists.

  - Scholarship policy & disbursement records. » Disability support policy.

  - Gender ratio statistics.

# 34. Out of State Students

## 1. Purpose

**The Out of State Students metric measures the geographical diversity of the student body. It evaluates:**

- How attractive the institution is to students beyond its home state or union territory.

- The institution’s national appeal and ability to provide a multi-cultural learning environment.

A high proportion of out-ofstate students indicates that the institution’s reputation, quality of education, and campus life draw students from across India.

## 2. Indicator Definition

- Percentage of total students whose permanent address is outside the state/union territory where the institution is located.

- Includes all years and courses (UG, PG, diploma, etc.).

- Foreign students do not count here — they are covered under the International Diversity (DA2) indicator.

## 3. Scoring Method

- **Maximum Points:** 25 points

- **Formula:** 1 point is awarded for every 1% of students from outside the state/UT. Score=Out of State %x1

- Full score (25 points) is given when ≥ 25% of students are from other states/UTs.

- If the percentage exceeds 25%, the score remains capped at 25.

## 4. Example Calculations

## Example 1 – High Diversity

- **Total students:** 4,000

- **Out-of–state students:** 1,200

- Out–of–state % = 1200/4000×100=30%

- Score = 25 points (capped at 25).

## Example 2 — Moderate Diversity

- **Total students:** 2,500

- **Out–of–state students:** 350

- Out–of–state % = 350/2500×100=14%

- Score = 14 points.

## 5. Data Validation Requirements

- Students’ permanent address proof from admission records.

- Data should be from the last academic year.

- Must exclude exchange students who stayed less than one semester.

## 6. Strategic Importance

- Higher out–of–state representation enriches cultural diversity and peer learning.

- Helps improve national visibility of the institution.

- Positively impacts employability by building a multi-state professional network for students.

## 7. Best Practices to Improve Score

1\. Targeted Recruitment Drives in other states/UTs.

2\. Special Scholarships for out–of-state students.

3\. Student Exchange Programs within India (MoUs with other universities).

4\. Marketing in National Educational Fairs and participation in central-level admission platforms.

5\. On-campus Accommodation to make relocation easier for out-of-state students.

# 35. International Diversity

## 1. Purpose

The International Diversity metric evaluates the global appeal of an institution — how well it attracts and integrates students and faculty from different countries. It measures:

- The extent to which the campus has a multi-cultural, global environment.

- Opportunities for cross-cultural exposure and international networking.

- The institution’s alignment with internationalisation goals in higher education.

## 2. Indicator Definition

## DAZ has two sub-components:

1\. Student International Diversity — Measures the number of nationalities represented in the student body.

2\. Faculty International Diversity — Measures the number of nationalities represented among faculty members.

## 3. Scoring Method

- **Total Maximum Points:** 20 points

  - 15 points for international student nationalities

  - 5 points for international faculty nationalities

## a) Student Nationalities (15 points)

- Maximum score for 10 or more distinct student nationalities.

- **Formula:**

Score=No. of Student Nationalities/10x15

- Capped at 15 points.

## b) Faculty Nationalities (5 points)

- Maximum score for 5 or more distinct faculty nationalities.

- **Formula:**

Score=No. of Faculty Nationalities/5×5

- Capped at 5 points.

## 4. Example Calculations

## Example 1 – High Diversity

| **Criterion / condition**     | **Points / scoring**               |
|-------------------------------|------------------------------------|
| **Student nationalities:** 12 | Full 15 points (cap reached at 10) |
| **Faculty nationalities:** 4  | 4/5×5=4 points                     |

- Total Score = 19 points

## Example 2 — Moderate Diversity

| **Criterion / condition**    | **Points / scoring** |
|------------------------------|----------------------|
| **Student nationalities:** 6 | 6/10×15=9 points     |
| **Faculty nationalities:** 2 | 2/5×5=2 points       |

- Total Score = 11 points

## 5. Data Validation Requirements

- **For students:** Passport copies, visa documents, or national ID showing country of origin.

- **For faculty:** Employment records with proof of nationality.

- Data must reflect current academic year.

## 6. Strategic Importance

- **High international diversity:**

  - Enhances cultural learning and global perspectives in the classroom. . Improves international rankings and partnerships.

  - Attracts foreign collaborations and research exchange.

## 7. Best Practices to Improve Score

1\. Establish international admission offices or agents in target countries.

2\. Offer scholarships and fee waivers for foreign students.

3\. Promote English-medium programs to attract non-native speakers.

4\. Create international faculty recruitment drives.

## 5. Partner with foreign universities for joint degree programs and student exchanges. Student ID

# 36. National Diversity for Students / Diversity of Students within the State

## 1. Purpose

This indicator measures how well an institution attracts students from a variety of geographical regions within India.

## It highlights:

- The institution’s reach beyond its immediate local area.

- Exposure of students to different cultures, languages, and perspectives within India.

- For state-based diversity, it measures how inclusive the institution is within its own state by admitting students from different districts.

## 2. Two Alternative Measures

Institutions are scored on one of the following, depending on which yields higher diversity representation:

## Option 1 – National Diversity for Students

- Counts the number of States or Union Territories in India represented by the student population.

- Full score is given for representation from 10 or more States/UTs.

## Scoring Method (National Level):

Score=Number of States/UTs Represented/10x20

(Capped at 20 points; scaled down to a minimum of 5 points if low diversity.) Option 2 — Diversity of Students within the State

- Applicable when most students are from the same state.

- Counts the number of districts within that state represented in the student body.

- Full score is given for 10 or more districts.

## Scoring Method (State Level):

Score=Number of Districts Represented/10x20

(Capped at 20 points; scaled down to 0 if no diversity.)

## 3. Example Calculations

## Example 1 – National Diversity

- **States represented:** 8

- **Score:** 8/10%x20=16 points.

## Example 2 – State-Level Diversity

- **Districts represented:** 6

- **Score:** 6/10x20=12 points.

## 4. Data Validation Requirements

- Enrolment records with student’s permanent address.

- Categorisation into State/UT or District based on official boundaries.

- Data must be for the current academic year.

## 5. Strategic Importance

- Enhances cultural diversity on campus, leading to richer learning experiences.

- Shows the institution’s attractiveness beyond its local catchment area.

- Can help in building a national brand presence.

## 6. Best Practices to Improve Score

1\. Strengthen out–of-state recruitment through targeted campaigns.

2\. Offer reserved seats or scholarships for students from underrepresented regions.

3\. Participate in national-level education fairs.

4\. Promote state-level outreach in multiple districts to improve internal diversity.

5\. Develop bridge or orientation programs to help students from different backgrounds adapt. Student Student Program Year of ID Name Study State / District UT of (if localOrigin state focus) Nationali Remarks ty

# 37. Expenditure on Scholarships and Bursaries

## 1. Purpose

This indicator evaluates how much of an institutions annual revenue is reinvested into nonrefundable financial support for students.

## It reflects the institution’s commitment to:

- Making education accessible to economically disadvantaged students.

- Supporting merit and need-based aid.

- Encouraging inclusive enrolment across socio-economic backgrounds.

## 2. Definition

## Scholarships and bursaries here mean:

- Non-refundable monetary awards.

- Provided either for academic merit, sports/cultural achievement, or financial need.

- Includes tuition fee waivers, cash awards, or direct payments towards student expenses.

- **Does NOT include:**

  - Refundable deposits.

  - Loans.

  - Payments tied to work contracts.

## 3. Scoring Criteria

- To score points, the institution must spend at least 1% of its total annual revenue on scholarships and bursaries.

- If \<1%, the score is zero.

- Full score for this indicator = 15 points.

## Formula:

Scholarship Expenditure %=Total scholarship funds awarded in last academic year/Total instit utional revenue of last academic year×100

## 4. Example Calculations

## Example 1 — Meets Threshold

- **Total annual revenue:** ¥100 Crores

- **Scholarships/bursaries awarded:** ₹1.5 Crores

- % expenditure = 1.5/100×100=1.5%

- Score = 15 points (meets ≥1% threshold).

## Example 2 – Below Threshold

- **Revenue:** 50 Crores

- **Scholarships awarded:** 330 Lakhs

- % expenditure = 0.3/50×100=0.6%

- Score = 0 points.

## 5. Data Validation Requirements

## Institutions must submit:

1\. Audited financial statements for the last academic year.

2\. Breakdown of scholarship/bursary expenditure (with proof such as student award lists).

3\. Evidence that awards were non-refundable and student-focused.

## 6. Strategic Importance

- Directly improves affordability for students.

- Enhances inclusivity and social equity.

- Strengthens reputation as a socially responsible institution.

- Positively impacts student satisfaction and retention rates.

## 7. Best Practices to Improve Score

1\. Allocate minimum 1–2% of revenue annually to scholarships.

2\. Develop a transparent scholarship policy with clear eligibility.

3\. Maintain detailed records for quick audit verification.

4\. Publicise scholarship programs to attract diverse applicants.

## 5. Partner with alumni and corporate donors to expand bursary funds. record p scholarshi Type p/Bursary Name

# 38. Student Scholarships/Fee Waiver

## 1. Purpose

This indicator measures how many students directly benefit from financial assistance provided by the institution in the form of scholarships or tuition fee waivers. **It** demon-

## strates the institution’s:

- Commitment to affordability

- Support for merit and equity

- Inclusivity in higher education

## 2. Definition

## For QS I-GAUGE scoring:

- **Scholarships / Fee Waiver refers to:**

  - Full or partial reduction in tuition fees granted by the institution. - Can be merit-based (academic, sports, cultural) or need-based (financial hardship).

- **NOT included:**

  - Refundable deposits.

  - Loans (even if interest-free).

  - External scholarships from government or NGOs (only institutional awards count).

## 3. Scoring Criteria

- The maximum score for this indicator = 10 points.

- The proportion of students whose tuition fees are fully or partially covered by the institution is considered.

- **Scaled as:**

| **Criterion / condition**                                    | **Points / scoring**               |
|--------------------------------------------------------------|------------------------------------|
| 10% of total student population receiving scholarship/waiver | full 10 points.                    |
| Less than 10%                                                | points scaled down proportionally. |
| 0%                                                           | 0 points.                          |

## Formula:

Score=(Number of students receiving scholarship/waiverTotal enrolled students)×(10/0.10) (Capped at 10 points)

## 4. Example Calculations

## Example 1 – Full Score

- Total students = 4,000

- Students receiving scholarship/waiver = 450

- % =450/4000×100=11.25%

- Since ≥10%, score = 10 points.

## Example 2 – Scaled Score

- Total students = 5,000

- Students with scholarship = 250

- % =250/5000×100=5%

- Score = 5/10x10=5 points.

## Example 3 – No Score

| **Criterion / condition** | **Points / scoring** |
|---------------------------|----------------------|
| Scholarship % = 0%        | Score = 0.           |

## 5. Data Validation Requirements

## Institutions must submit:

1\. List of students receiving scholarships/fee waivers (with roll numbers and percentage of fee covered).

2\. Breakdown of merit-based vs need-based awards.

3\. Policy documents outlining scholarship/waiver criteria.

4\. Proof that the funding is from institutional resources, not solely external agencies.

## 6. Strategic Importance

- Directly supports student diversity and equity.

- Improves student retention and completion rates.

- Enhances public perception of the institution’s accessibility.

- Encourages talent acquisition by attracting bright students regardless of income.

## 7. Best Practices to Improve Score

1\. Target ≥10% coverage of enrolled students annually.

2\. Introduce multi-category scholarships (merit, need, sports, cultural).

3\. Maintain transparent application and selection process.

4\. Publicise the scholarship policy widely to boost applications.

5\. Use donor/alumni funding to expand coverage without straining budgets.

# 39. Differently Abled

## 1. Purpose

This indicator evaluates how well the institution supports and accommodates students with disabilities or special needs.

## It reflects:

- The institutions commitment to inclusivity

- Compliance with legal accessibility standards in education

- Readiness to provide equal learning opportunities to all

## 2. Scoring Structure

## Maximum score: 20 points

- **10 points each for:**

## 1. Number of differently abled students on campus

- Measures actual inclusion in enrollment.

2\. Institution’s policy accommodating needs of differently abled students - Assesses formal commitments and provisions.

## 3. Component Details

## A. Number of Differently Abled Students (10 points)

- Refers to students with physical, sensory, cognitive, or learning disabilities.

- Full score (10 points) given for active enrollment of differently abled students.

- Score scaled down proportionally if representation is low.

- Encourages admission outreach and support for these students.

## B. Institutional Policy (10 points)

- **A documented, approved policy that:**

  - Details reasonable accommodations (special facilities, exam arrangements, accessible learning materials).

  - Aligns with The Rights of Persons with Disabilities Act, 2016 (India) or equivalent standards.

  - Covers admissions, infrastructure, teaching methods, and student support services.

## 4. Example Scoring

1\. Enrollment data for differently abled students (type of disability optional for privacy).

2\. Copy of the institutional policy on disability inclusion.

3\. Evidence of implementation (photos of facilities, service records, assistive technology usage).

## 4. Compliance proof with Accessibility Guidelines (e.g., AICTE norms, UGC guidelines). 6. Strategic Importance

- Strengthens social responsibility credentials.

- Enhances student diversity.

- Complies with national disability regulations.

- Improves public image and ranking performance.

## 7. Best Practices to Improve Score

- Actively recruit differently abled students through outreach and scholarships.

- Provide assistive technologies (screen readers, hearing aids, Braille materials).

- Maintain barrier-free infrastructure (ramps, tactile paths, elevators, wide corridors).

- Train faculty on inclusive pedagogy.

- Collaborate with NGOs and government bodies for accessibility improvement. Student Student Progra ID Name m

# 40. Gender Ratio

## 1. Purpose

The Gender Ratio indicator measures gender diversity and equality among both students and faculty members.

## It aims to:

- Promote balanced representation of genders in higher education.

- Encourage institutions to create inclusive environments for learning and teaching.

- Monitor and reduce gender disparity in academic participation.

## 2. Scoring Structure

## Maximum score: 20 points

- **10 points each for:**

## 1. Student Gender Ratio

## 2. Faculty Gender Ratio

## 3. Component Details

## A. Student Gender Ratio (10 points)

- **Ideal ratio:** Male : Female = 2 : 3 (approximately 40% male, 60% female) o This ratio is considered balanced for scoring purposes.

  - Scores are scaled down if the actual ratio deviates from this benchmark.

- **Special note:**

  - Full score (10 points) is awarded automatically if the institution has allwomen students.

## B. Faculty Gender Ratio (10 points)

- **Ideal benchmark:** 80% female faculty scaled down to 50%.

- Measures gender balance among teaching staff.

- Encourages diversity in leadership and teaching roles.

- Aims to break gender stereotypes in faculty hiring.

## 4. Example Scoring Table

Scenario

40% male, 60% female students; 80% female faculty

50% male, 50% female students; 60% female faculty

70% male, 30% female students; 30% female faculty

100% women students; 50% female faculty 5. Data & Validation Requirements

1\. Student enrollment breakdown by gender.

2\. Faculty list with gender data.

Minimum for sgacuity

3\. Historical trend (optional but beneficial) showing improvement in gender diversity.

4\. Evidence of policies for gender inclusion (scholarships, women safety cells, maternity/paternity leave policies).

## 6. Strategic Importance

- Demonstrates commitment to gender equality in education and employment.

- Aligns with Sustainable Development Goal 5 (Gender Equality).

- Boosts institutional reputation among students, parents, and international partners.

- Helps attract scholarships and grants targeted at gender diversity.

## 7. Best Practices to Improve Score

- Targeted outreach to underrepresented gender groups in certain disciplines (e.g., encouraging women in STEM).

- Offer scholarships and mentorship programs for gender balance.

- Implement inclusive hiring policies for faculty.

- Conduct gender sensitivity training for all staff and students.

- Ensure safe campus environments (women’s cells, anti-harassment policies, CCTV). Student ID Student Program Name

# 41. Financial Equality

## 1. Purpose

The Financial Equality indicator evaluates how well an institution supports students from lower socio-economic backgrounds through scholarships, fee waivers, and financial aid policies.

It ensures that financial status is not a barrier to accessing quality education.

## 2. Scoring Structure

## Maximum score: 10 points

- **5 points each for two components:**

1\. Scholarship Policy accommodating socio-economic status.

2\. Number of students availing financial aid based on income criteria.

## 3. Component Details

## A. Scholarship Policy (5 points)

- Awarded if the institution has a formal, documented policy to provide financial aid, scholarships, or fee waivers based on socio-economic status.

- **The policy must:**

  - Define eligibility criteria (e.g., family income limit, BPL status). . Outline application & verification procedures.

  - Ensure fair and transparent selection.

## B. Students Availing Financial Aid (5 points)

- Proportion of students who receive income-based aid or fee waivers from the institution.

- **Points are awarded if:**

  - The institution maintains verified records of beneficiaries.

  - Aid is provided directly from institutional funds (not just external sources).

## 4. Example Scoring

Scenario Scholarship Policy % of Students with Aid Score Documented policy + 15% students get aid Yes

Documented policy + 5% students get aid Yes

No formal policy + 10% students get aid No

No policy + 0% students get aid No

## 5. Data & Validation Requirements

## Institutions must provide:

Above threshold 10 Below threshold 8 Meets threshold 5 None 0

1\. Copy of the scholarship policy with socio-economic inclusion clauses.

2\. Annual financial aid report listing number of beneficiaries and total amount distributed.

3\. Breakdown by income level of aided students.

4\. Evidence of fund allocation in the institutional budget.

## 6. Strategic Importance

- Promotes equitable access to higher education.

- Aligns with Sustainable Development Goal 4 (Quality Education) and SDG 10 (Reduced Inequalities).

- Improves student retention by reducing dropouts due to financial constraints.

- Enhances the institution’s social responsibility profile.

## 7. Best Practices to Improve Score

- Create a dedicated scholarship & financial aid office.

- Collaborate with NGOs, alumni, and corporate CSR funds for scholarships.

- Publicize the availability of aid during admissions.

- Offer flexible payment plans for tuition fees.

- Review and adjust aid allocation annually to match inflation & living costs. stude stude Progr Year Categ Famil nt ID nt am of ory y Nam Study Inco e me Brack et Aid

- Enter awards row-by-row in DA8_Financial_Aid; ensure Aid Type = Income-Based and Approved = Yes for DA8 counting.

- Set B2 (Total Students) and adjust B5 (Benchmark%) if your policy defines a different threshold.

- Flip Policy (B6) to Yes when the SES-based scholarship policy is formally approved and documented.

- Read DA8 score directly in Scoring!B9 for submissions.

## Optional enhancements (I can add quickly):

- Data quality rules (flag missing Category/Income for income-based awards).

- Academic year filter to score by year.

- Auto-proofing (require Evidence/URL when Approved = Yes).

- Pivot-style departmental breakdown for NAAC/QS uploads.

# 42. Regional and Linguistic Diversity of Faculty

## 1. Purpose

The Regional and Linguistic Diversity of Faculty indicator measures how diverse an institution’s teaching staff is in terms of geographic origin and native language. It recognizes institutions that attract faculty from different regions and with varied culturallinguistic backgrounds, promoting inclusivity, global perspective, and a richer academic environment.

## 2. Scoring Structure

## Maximum score: 10 points

- **5 points each for two components:**

1\. Regional Diversity – Number of distinct States/Union Territories represented by faculty members.

2\. Linguistic Diversity – Number of distinct mother tongues spoken by faculty members.

## 3. Component Details

## A. Regional Diversity (5 points)

- **Measurement:**

  - Count the number of different States/UTs in India from which faculty members originate.

  - Full score awarded if 15 or more States/UTs are represented. Score scaled down proportionally for fewer regions.

## Example Scaling (Illustrative):

States/UTs Represented Points (out of 5)

15 or more 5

103.3

51.6

1–20.5

## B. Linguistic Diversity (5 points)

- **Measurement:**

  - Count the number of different native languages spoken by faculty members as their mother tongue.

  - Full score awarded if 15 or more languages are represented.

  - Scaled down for fewer languages.

## Example Scaling (Illustrative):

Languages Represented Points (out of 5)

15 or more 5

103.3

51.6

1–20.5

## 4. Data & Validation Requirements

## Institutions must provide:

1\. Faculty database with permanent address and native state/UT.

2\. Self-declared language data from faculty records or HR forms.

3\. Evidence of diversity in faculty recruitment policies and practices.

## 5. Strategic Importance

- Encourages geographic and linguistic inclusivity.

- Enhances cultural exchange and global outlook among students.

- Aligns with national integration and diversity policies.

- Attracts talent from across regions, improving teaching quality and research collaborations.

## 6. Best Practices to Improve Score

- Recruit from national job pools rather than local-only hiring.

- Partner with national recruitment boards and faculty exchange programs.

- Promote inclusive hiring policies ensuring diverse applicant pools.

- Offer language training and cultural integration programs for faculty from different regions.

# 43. QS I-GAUGE Core Criterion: Facilities (FC)

Core Criterion — Facilities (FC) — 150 Points

## 1. Purpose

- Evaluates the quality, availability, and accessibility of campus infrastructure and student support facilities.

- Assesses both physical amenities and service-related provisions that enhance the learning and living experience.

## 2. Detailed Breakdown of Indicators

### FC1 – Facilities on Campus (20 points)

- **Scoring:** 2 points per facility (max 20 points) for having:

## 1. Swimming pool

## 2. Fitness gym

## 3. Indoor sporting facilities

## 4. Outdoor sporting facilities

## 5. Synthetic athletics track

## 6. Full-time sports coach

## 7. Mess/Canteen

## 8. Recreation room/Safe assembly area

## 9. Faculty accommodation/staff quarters

## 10. Bank/ATM

- **Rationale:** Measures diversity and completeness of campus amenities. FC2 — Medical Facilities (10 points)

- **5 points each for:**

1\. Presence of Health/Medical Centre or collaboration with hospitals/clinics.

## 2. At least 1 full-time medical staff member / ambulance service. FC3 – Student Satisfaction with

## Sanitation, Hygiene, and Infrastructure (20 points)

| **Criterion / condition**          | **Points / scoring**                                                                                            |
|------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| **Survey-based:** 90% satisfaction | 20 points, ≤ 60% → 0 points, scaled in between. FC4 —- On-campus Catering — Quality & Affordability (10 points) |

- **Survey-based:** Student satisfaction with food quality and price.

| **Criterion / condition** | **Points / scoring** |
|---------------------------|----------------------|
| 90% satisfaction          | full score.          |

### FC5 – Student Accommodation (10 points)

- Beds in student residences as % of first-year student intake.

| **Criterion / condition** | **Points / scoring**                     |
|---------------------------|------------------------------------------|
| 80% coverage              | 10 points, scaled proportionally to 50%. |

### FC6 – Information Technology Services (20 points)

- **Survey-based:** Satisfaction with high-speed Wi–Fi access on campus.

| **Criterion / condition** | **Points / scoring**    |
|---------------------------|-------------------------|
| 80% satisfaction          | 20 points, otherwise 0. |

### FC7 – Management Information Systems (10 points)

- Existence and use of an MIS for student admissions, exams, and record management. FC8 — Student Safety (20 points)

- **10 points:** % of students feeling safe (survey-based; 90% scaled to 60%).

- **2 points each for policies:** Anti-ragging/Anti-bullying Committee, Sexual Harassment Committee.

- **2 points each for facilities:** Warden office, Fire extinguishers/drills, CCTV/security office.

### FC9 – Library Facilities (10 points)

- **Survey-based:** Student and faculty satisfaction with library services.

| **Criterion / condition** | **Points / scoring** |
|---------------------------|----------------------|
| 90% satisfaction          | full score.          |

### FC10 – Facilities for Differently Abled (20 points)

- **4 points each for having:**

## 1. Ramps

## 2. Railings

## 3. Special toilets

## 4. Visual–aid centre

5\. User-friendly lift for differently-abled individuals.

## 3. Scoring Approach

- **Survey-Based Indicators:** FC3, FC4, FC6, FC8 (part), FC9.

- **Data-Based Indicators:** FC1, FC2, FC5, FC7, FC8 (part), FC10.

- **Evidence Required:** Facility inventory, student/faculty survey results, policy documents, infrastructure photos, and service agreements.

# 44. Facilities on Campus

## 1. Purpose

The Facilities on Campus indicator measures the availability of key physical and recreational infrastructure that supports the overall student experience, faculty well-being, and institutional functionality.

It recognizes institutions that invest in sports, wellness, residential, and basic amenities for a holistic educational environment.

## 2. Scoring Structure

- **Maximum Points:** 20

- **Allocation:** 2 points per facility (10 facilities in total).

- **Formula:**

Score=Number of facilities present×2

- No scaling—points are directly based on the number of facilities available.

## 3. List of Facilities Considered

## Each facility counts equally (2 points each):

1\. Swimming Pool – On-campus pool for sports training, fitness, and recreation.

2\. Fitness Gym – Well-equipped gymnasium with cardio, strength training, and freeweight areas.

3\. Indoor Sporting Facilities — Indoor courts/halls for sports like badminton, basketball, table tennis, squash, etc.

4\. Outdoor Sporting Facilities — Playgrounds, cricket pitches, football fields, tennis courts, or track fields.

5\. Synthetic Athletics Track — Modern track for running, athletics, and competitive training.

6\. Full-time Sports Coach — Dedicated professional(s) for sports training and athletic development.

7\. Mess/Canteen —- On-campus dining with hygienic food and affordable options.

8\. Recreation Room / Safe Assembly Area — Space for leisure, cultural activities, or safe congregation during emergencies.

9\. Faculty Accommodation / Staff Quarters —- On-campus housing for faculty/staff to promote community living.

10\. Bank / ATM – On-site banking services or ATMs for convenience.

## 4. Data & Validation Requirements

## Institutions must provide:

- Infrastructure list with photographs of facilities.

- Maintenance records to confirm facilities are operational.

- Usage statistics (e.g., number of students using the gym or sports facilities).

- Staff employment records (for sports coach, etc.).

## 5. Strategic Importance

- Improves student satisfaction and well-being.

- Encourages sports participation and healthy lifestyles.

- Enhances campus attractiveness for prospective students.

- Facilitates faculty retention through on-campus housing.

- Strengthens QS I-GAUGE Facilities Category score, contributing toward Platinum readiness.

## 6. Best Practices to Improve Score

- Develop multi-purpose indoor sports complexes to cover multiple facility points.

- Maintain 24/7 accessible recreation and wellness spaces.

- Partner with banks and food service providers to set up on-campus outlets.

- Keep facilities well-maintained and conduct regular audits.

# 45. Medical Facilities

## 1. Purpose

The Medical Facilities indicator evaluates the institution’s capacity to provide timely healthcare access and emergency support to students, faculty, and staff.

It ensures that on-campus health services are not just available, but adequately staffed and equipped to handle both routine and urgent medical needs.

## 2. Scoring Structure

- **Maximum Points:** 10

- **Allocation:** 5 points each for two specific components:

## (i) Presence of Health/Medical Centre

- Can be on-campus or through a formal working collaboration with an external hospital/clinic located nearby.

- Should cater to first-aid, primary care, and preventive health check-ups. (ii) Full-time Medical Staff or Emergency Response

- At least one full-time qualified medical staff (doctor or nurse) stationed at the medical centre.

- OR contractual arrangements with hospitals/clinics for on-call doctors. Must also have an ambulance facility for emergencies.

## 3. Data & Validation Requirements

## Institutions must provide:

- Photographs of the medical centre and ambulance (if applicable).

- Staff employment records (appointment letters, qualification proof).

- MoUs or agreements with partnered hospitals/clinics.

- Emergency response SOP (Standard Operating Procedure).

## 4. Strategic Importance

- Increases student & parent confidence in campus safety.

- Meets AICTE/UGC basic infrastructure guidelines.

- Positively impacts student satisfaction scores under sanitation and safety.

- Supports compliance for international student requirements (especially in QS I-GAUGE Diversity & Accessibility category).

## 5. Best Practices to Improve Score

- Ensure 24x7 medical availability (via shifts or tie-ups).

- Maintain fully stocked first-aid kits in all departments and hostels.

- Conduct annual health check-up camps.

- Train hostel wardens and sports staff in basic first aid & CPR.

- Have a dedicated health awareness program (e.g., mental health workshops, vaccination drives).

# 46. Student Satisfaction with Sanitation, Hygiene, and Infrastructure

## 1. Purpose

This indicator measures students’ perception and satisfaction with the cleanliness, hygiene standards, and quality of physical infrastructure across the campus.

It ensures that institutions maintain a healthy, safe, and conducive learning environment in compliance with national health and safety norms.

## 2. Scoring Structure

- **Maximum Points:** 20

- **Scoring Basis:**

| **Criterion / condition**     | **Points / scoring** |
|-------------------------------|----------------------|
| Satisfaction percentage ≥ 90% | Full 20 points.      |
| Satisfaction percentage ≤ 60% | O points.            |

- If satisfaction falls between 60% and 90%, points are calculated proportionally.

(Only if 60% \< *satisfaction* \< 90%)

## 3. Survey Methodology & Thresholds

- **Source:** Independent student survey conducted by QS I-GAUGE.

- **Response Threshold:**

  - Minimum 30% of total enrolled students (all years & courses) must respond for the score to be valid.

- **Questions in the survey may cover:**

  - Cleanliness of classrooms, laboratories, and washrooms.

  - Waste disposal and recycling facilities.

  - Drinking water quality.

  - Lighting, ventilation, and general maintenance of buildings.

## 4. Data & Validation Requirements

## Institutions must:

- Provide survey response reports (raw data + % satisfied).

- Maintain photographic evidence of sanitation facilities.

- Submit cleaning & maintenance schedules.

- Share annual infrastructure audit reports (if any).

## 5. Strategic Importance

- Directly affects overall student satisfaction score in QS I-GAUGE.

- Links to Facilities, Social Responsibility, and Governance categories.

- Higher hygiene ratings improve international student recruitment appeal.

- Reduces risk of public health complaints and negative publicity.

## 6. Best Practices to Improve Score

- Implement daily cleaning checklists for all campus zones.

- Conduct monthly sanitation audits by an external agency.

- Install hand sanitiser dispensers in common areas.

- Provide safe drinking water stations with regular quality checks.

- Set up a student feedback channel for reporting hygiene issues.

# 47. On-campus catering — Quality and Affordability

## 1. Purpose

This indicator measures students’ satisfaction with food quality and pricing at campus–based dining facilities (mess, canteen, food courts, etc.).

## It reflects an institution’s commitment to:

- Providing nutritious, hygienic, and affordable meals.

- Ensuring cost-effectiveness for students from diverse economic backgrounds.

- Supporting student health, productivity, and well-being.

## 2. Scoring Structure

- **Maximum Points:** 10

- **Scoring Basis:**

| **Criterion / condition**     | **Points / scoring** |
|-------------------------------|----------------------|
| Satisfaction percentage ≥ 90% | Full 10 points.      |
| Satisfaction percentage ≤ 60% | O points.            |

- Between 60% and 90%, the score is calculated proportionally. (Only if 60% \< *satisfaction* \< 90%)

## 3. Survey Methodology & Thresholds

- **Source:** Independent student survey by QS I-GAUGE.

- **Response Threshold:**

  - At least 30% of total enrolled students must respond for validity.

- **Typical survey questions include:**

  - Taste, freshness, and variety of food served.

  - Compliance with hygiene standards.

  - Availability of vegetarian/vegan and culturally sensitive meals.

  - Perception of food prices relative to quality.

  - Waiting times and cleanliness in dining spaces.

## 4. Data & Validation Requirements

## Institutions must:

- Provide survey reports with satisfaction percentage.

- Submit sample weekly menus and pricing structures.

- Share food safety compliance certificates (FSSAI licence or equivalent).

- Provide evidence of mess/canteen contracts or in-house management policies.

## 5. Strategic Importance

- Strongly impacts overall student satisfaction and retention rates.

- Links to Facilities, Diversity & Accessibility, and Social Responsibility.

- Acts as an indirect contributor to international student appeal.

- Plays a role in QS I-GAUGE'’s holistic campus experience evaluation.

## 6. Best Practices to Improve Score

- Introduce student food committees to review quality and menu planning.

- Conduct monthly kitchen inspections for hygiene compliance.

- Provide subsidised meal plans for economically disadvantaged students.

- Rotate menus to offer variety and cultural inclusivity.

- Display nutritional value information at food counters.

# 48. Student Accommodation

## 1. Purpose

This indicator evaluates whether the institution provides sufficient and accessible oncampus housing for students, particularly first-year students, to ensure:

- Smooth transition into university life.

- Safe, convenient, and affordable living arrangements.

- Better integration into campus culture and academic activities.

## 2. Scoring Structure

- **Maximum Points:** 10

- **Measurement Basis:** Proportion of available beds in student residences relative to the number of first-year students.

- **Scaling Rule:**

| **Criterion / condition**                                | **Points / scoring** |
|----------------------------------------------------------|----------------------|
| If 80% or more of firstyear students can be accommodated | Full 10 points.      |
| If 50% or less can be accommodated                       | O points.            |

- Between 50% and 80%, points are proportional.

(Only if 50% \< Accommodation \< 80%)

## 3. Data Requirements & Validation

## Institutions must provide:

- Total number of first-year students (latest academic year).

- Number of beds available in on-campus hostels/residences.

- Allocation policy (e.g., priority for first-year, out-of-state, or international students).

- **Evidence:** hostel brochures, allotment lists, photographs, maintenance contracts.

## 4. Key Evaluation Factors

## ‘While the primary score is quantitative, QS I-GAUGE analysts may also consider:

- Facilities within hostels (Wi-Fi, security, housekeeping, laundry, dining hall).

- Safety measures (CCTV, wardens, access control).

- Affordability for different student categories.

- Accessibility for differently abled students.

## 5. Strategic Importance

- **Higher on-campus accommodation capacity:**

  - Improves student satisfaction & retention.

  - Attracts out-of-state and international students.

  - Strengthens the Diversity & Accessibility score.

- Plays a direct role in overall campus experience and ranking appeal.

## 6. Best Practices to Improve Score

- Expand housing capacity through new blocks or collaborations with nearby private residences.

- Prioritise first-year students in hostel allocation.

- Maintain transparent allotment and fee structures.

- Implement student feedback systems for hostel services.

- Provide 24/7 security and medical assistance.

# 49. Information Technology Services

## 1. Purpose

This indicator evaluates how effectively an institution provides high-speed internet access via Wi-Fi across campus to support teaching, learning, research, and administrative activities.

It measures both infrastructure capability and user satisfaction among students and faculty.

## 2. Scoring Structure

- **Maximum Points:** 20

- **Measurement Basis:**

  - Proportion of faculty and student responses indicating satisfaction with the speed, reliability, and availability of Wi–Fi/internet services.

  - Full Score = 80% or higher satisfaction rate.

  - **Zero Score = If the satisfaction rate falls below 80% (as per survey results). Scoring Rule:**

Score={20 if satisfaction≥80%

0 if satisfaction\<80%}

(No *partial* *scaling* is *mentioned* — it’s a *binary* scoring.)

## 3. Data Requirements & Validation

## Institutions must provide:

- Survey results from both students and faculty showing satisfaction levels.

- Coverage map or evidence of Wi–Fi infrastructure (e.g., number of hotspots, floor plans).

- Bandwidth details (e.g., Mbps/Gbps capacity, number of concurrent users supported).

- Uptime logs from IT department or internet service provider (ISP).

## 4. Key Evaluation Factors

## ‘While the scoring is survey-based, the following operational aspects affect satisfaction:

- **Speed:** Sufficient for video conferencing, online classes, research downloads.

- **Coverage:** Full campus coverage (classrooms, labs, hostels, libraries, common areas).

- **Reliability:** Minimal downtime or connectivity issues.

- **Security:** Encrypted connections, access control (student/faculty login credentials).

- **Capacity:** Ability to handle peak usage without slowdowns.

## 5. Strategic Importance

- High-quality **IT** services are essential for blended learning, online examinations, and digital libraries.

- Plays a major role in Teaching & Learning (TL), Faculty Quality (FQ), and Facilities (FC) scores.

- Boosts student and faculty satisfaction survey indicators across multiple QS I-GAUGE categories.

## 6. Best Practices to Improve Score

- Ensure minimum 1 Gbps campus internet backbone for medium–to-large institutions.

- Deploy enterprise-grade Wi-Fi access points with load balancing.

- Conduct periodic speed and connectivity audits.

- Offer dedicated IT support desks for troubleshooting.

- Implement feedback loops so users can report connectivity issues instantly.

# 50. Management Information Systems (MIS)

## 1. Purpose

This indicator assesses whether an institution has implemented a Management Information System (MIS) for student-related administrative processes and how effectively it is used. MIS serves as a centralized platform for data storage, retrieval, and process automation across academic and administrative functions.

## 2. Scoring Structure

- **Maximum Points:** 10

- **Measurement Basis:**

  - **Full points are awarded if the institution has a functioning, actively used MIS for:**

## 1. Student admissions

## 2. Examinations

3\. Record keeping (attendance, grades, transcripts, fee records, etc.) o 0 points if the MIS is absent or non-functional.

- This is a binary scoring — either the criteria are met fully, or they are not.

## 3. Key Features of an MIS for Full Points

## An eligible MIS should have:

- **Admission Management:** Online application, document uploads, seat allocation, fee payment tracking.

- **Examination Management:** Exam scheduling, hall ticket generation, online assessments, grading automation.

- **Academic Records:** Attendance logs, grade history, transcripts, certificates.

- **User Access:** Secure login for students, faculty, and administrators.

- **Integration:** Links with Learning Management Systems (LMS) and finance modules.

- **Reporting & Analytics:** Customizable reports for decision-making.

## 4. Data Requirements for QS I-GAUGE Validation

## Institutions must provide:

- System screenshots or demo access to the MIS.

- Process documentation showing coverage of admissions, exams, and records.

- Usage logs to prove active engagement (e.g., number of users, frequency of logins).

- Vendor or in-house development details (for credibility).

## 5. Strategic Importance

- Supports institutional governance and data-driven decision making.

- Reduces administrative errors and duplication of work.

- Improves transparency and efficiency in student-related processes.

- Positively impacts student satisfaction in other indicators like Teaching & Learning (TL) and Facilities (FC).

## 6. Best Practices to Maximize Points

- **Choose a robust, scalable MIS platform (examples:** ERPNext, Fedena, SAP Student Lifecycle, TCS iON, PeopleSoft Campus Solutions).

- Ensure mobile-friendly access for students and faculty.

- Provide training sessions for faculty, staff, and students to use MIS features effectively.

- Regularly update and maintain the system to avoid downtime.

- Integrate MIS with digital payment gateways and document verification tools.

# 51. Student Safety

## 1. Purpose

This indicator evaluates how well an institution ensures the safety, security, and well-being of its students on campus, including perception of safety, policy measures, and phys– ical safety infrastructure.

It combines survey-based satisfaction scores with the existence of formal policies and facilities.

## 2. Scoring Structure

**Maximum Points:** 20 (combined from perception, policies, and facilities)

## Breakdown:

- **Perception-based score:**

  - 10 points for student responses indicating they feel safe and secure on campus without any time or mobility constraints.

  - **Scaled:**

- 90% satisfaction = Full 10 points

- 60% or less = 0 points

- Between 60%–90% = Pro-rated points

- **Policy-based score (2 points each):**

  - \(i\) Anti-Ragging / Anti-Bullying Committee

  - \(ii\) Sexual Harassment Prevention Committee (often aligned with POSH Act compliance)

- **Facility-based score (2 points each):**

  - \(i\) Warden Office (for hostels/residences)

  - \(ii\) Fire Extinguishers / Fire Drills

  - \(iii\) CCTV Cameras on Campus / Security Office

## 3. Evidence Required for Full Points

- **Survey Data:** Student satisfaction survey results specifically addressing safety perceptions.

- **Policy Documents:** Official notifications of safety committees, with meeting minutes in the last 12 months.

- **Facility Records:**

  - Proof of CCTV coverage, warden appointments, and firefighting equipment maintenance.

  - Safety drill records and incident response procedures.

## 4. Strategic Importance

- Affects student retention and satisfaction.

- Impacts parent trust and admissions.

- Complies with legal requirements like the UGC Anti-Ragging Regulations (2009) and the Sexual Harassment of Women at Workplace Act (2013).

## 5. Best Practices to Maximize Score

- Maintain 24/7 security staff with ID checks at campus gates.

- Ensure full CCTV coverage in critical areas.

- Conduct bi-annual fire and evacuation drills with documentation.

- Display emergency contact numbers across campus.

- Train wardens and hostel staff in first aid and emergency protocols.

- Conduct awareness workshops for students on anti-ragging and safety rights.

# 52. QS I-GAUGE – Student Safety Readiness Checklist

## A. Perception of Safety (Survey-based) — 10 Points

## Student Safety Perception Survey:

## "Include a clear question in the student satisfaction survey:

- Do you *feel* *safe* and secure on *campus* *without* any *time* or *mobility* constraints?"

**I”** Achieve at least 90% positive responses for full points (≥ 60% minimum to avoid zero score).

**I"** Collect survey responses from ≥ 30% of total student population (per QS I-GAUGE threshold).

**I”** Archive survey raw data and summary report for submission.

## B. Safety Policies — 4 Points (2 Points Each)

## 1. Anti-Ragging / Anti-Bullying Committee

I" Official policy document in place, compliant with UGC Anti-Ragging Regulations (2009).

**I”** Committee composition list (with student and faculty representation).

**I”** Annual meeting minutes + action taken report.

## 2. Sexual Harassment Prevention Committee (POSH)

I" Policy aligned with Sexual Harassment of Women at Workplace (2013).

I" List of members trained in handling complaints.

**I”** Awareness workshops conducted at least once a year.

## C. Safety Infrastructure — 6 Points (2 Points Each)

## 1. Warden Office (for Hostels/Residences)

**I”** Appointment letter(s) for wardens.

**I"** Dedicated space for the warden office with visible signage.

## 2. Fire Safety Measures

**I"** Fire extinguishers installed and inspected regularly (with maintenance logs).

**I”** At least one documented fire drill in the last 12 months.

## 3. CCTV Coverage & Security Office

**\[¥** CCTV installed in all critical areas (gates, corridors, hostels, parking).

I" Security control room or designated office for surveillance.

I” Footage retention policy (minimum 30 days).

I~ Emergency contact numbers displayed in public spaces.

I" First-aid training for wardens and hostel staff.

# 53. Library Facilities

## Objective:

To measure how satisfied students and faculty are with the library’s services, resources, and infrastructure. The score is based on survey feedback.

## Scoring Method

- **Survey Question Example:**

  - How satisfied are you with the library facilities and services available at your institution?"

- **Population Thresholds (per QS I-GAUGE Independent Survey Requirement):**

  - **Students:** Responses from at least 30% of total student population. » Faculty: Responses from at least 60% of total faculty population.

- **Point Allocation:**

  - Full 10 points = 90% or more respondents indicate satisfaction. . Scaled Down if between 60% and 90% satisfaction.

  - 0 points if ≤ 60% satisfaction.

Key Components That Influence Satisfaction Scores

**To achieve a 90%+ satisfaction rate, ensure the following:**

## 1. Physical Infrastructure

- Sufficient seating capacity for peak student hours.

- Digital workstations with internet access.

- Comfortable reading/study spaces (individual & group).

- Accessibility features for differently abled users (ramps, low-height desks).

## 2. Collection & Resources

- **Diverse collection:** textbooks, reference books, journals, magazines, newspapers.

- **Digital resources:** e-books, e-journals, databases (e.g., Scopus, IEEE, JSTOR).

- Institutional repository for research publications, dissertations.

## 3. Technology & Digital Services

- Integrated Library Management System (LMS) (e.g., Koha, Libsys) for cataloguing and borrowing.

- Online catalogue (OPAC) for easy search.

- Remote access to e-resources through VPN or login portals.

## 4. Service Quality

- Well-trained, approachable library staff.

- Extended operating hours during exams.

- Reservation system for study rooms.

- Regular orientation sessions for new students.

## 5. Support & Engagement

- Interlibrary loan facilities.

- Reading clubs, author talks, or book exhibitions.

- Feedback mechanism for suggestions and complaints.

Evidence Required for QS I-GAUGE Audit

Requirement

Survey Results Photos/Videos Resource List LMS Access

Evidence

Raw data + summary of student & faculty responses Library interiors, seating, computers, accessible facilities Updated inventory of books, journals, e-resources Screenshot of catalogue/search portal

Requirement Evidence

Usage Statistics Footfall data, issue/return statistics

Accessibility Proof Photos or documents showing ramps, special desks, etc.

## Full Score Strategy (10/10 Points):

- Conduct a well-structured awareness drive before the survey to encourage participation.

- Ensure digital & physical resources are balanced to meet diverse needs.

- Address common pain points (Wi-Fi speed, seating availability, outdated books) before survey distribution.

# 54. Facilities for Differently Abled

- Purpose

  - To assess how well the institution has integrated physical accessibility and inclusive infrastructure for students, faculty, and staff with disabilities. Encourages compliance with accessibility norms (e.g., Persons with Disabilities Act, 2016) and promotes equal participation in academic and campus life.

- Scoring Structure (Total 20 points)

  - **4 points each for the following 5 key infrastructure elements:**

1\. Ramps — Wheelchair-friendly access to academic blocks, hostels, and key facilities.

2\. Railings — Support handrails along ramps and stairs for mobility assistance.

3\. Special Toilets — Restrooms designed for accessibility, including space for wheelchairs and grab bars.

4\. Visual–aid Centre – Assistive technologies for visually impaired individuals (Braille books, screen readers, magnifiers).

5\. User-friendly Lift for Differently Abled – Elevators with wide doors, low control panels, and audio/visual indicators.

- Documentation Requirements

  - Photos of the facilities in use.

  - Campus layout map highlighting accessible areas.

  - Policy or commitment statement on accessibility.

- Best Practices for Platinum–level Performance

  - Install tactile paths for visually impaired navigation.

  - Provide sign language interpreters during academic events.

  - Maintain a grievance redressal mechanism for differently-abled students.

  - Integrate inclusive ICT tools (captioned videos, adaptive keyboards).

- Impact on Institutional Ranking

  - Demonstrates social responsibility and compliance with national/international standards.

  - Enhances student diversity and accessibility scores under QS I-GAUGE’s core criteria.
