# 55. Differently Abled Policy

## 1. Policy Objective

- **Purpose:**

  - To ensure equal access, safety, dignity, and independence for differently-abled students, faculty, staff, and visitors across the campus.

  - To comply with Rights of Persons with Disabilities Act, 2016 (India), UN Convention on the Rights of Persons with Disabilities, and relevant building/accessibility codes (e.g., NBC, ISO 21542).

- **Scope:**

  - Applicable to all academic, administrative, residential, recreational, and service areas of the institution.

  - Covers design, construction, maintenance, and usage of key accessibility infrastructure.

## 2. Facility Standards & Implementation Guidelines

## A. Ramps

- **Purpose:** Provide smooth, step-free access to entrances, exits, and key facilities.

- **Design Standards:**

  - **Gradient not steeper than 1:**12 for independent wheelchair use. Minimum clear width: 1200 mm.

  - Non-slip surface with tactile indicators at start/end.

  - Handrails on both sides, height 760–900 mm from ramp surface.

- **Placement:**

  - At all building entrances used by students or public.

  - Linked to main pedestrian circulation routes.

- **Maintenance:**

  - Quarterly inspection for wear, cracks, or water pooling.

  - Immediate repair of surface damage.

## B. Railings

- **Purpose:** Support mobility and stability for persons with reduced strength, vision, or balance.

- **Design Standards:**

  - Continuous, smooth, rounded grip diameter 30–50 mm.

  - Mounted at dual heights (760–900 mm for adults, 600 mm for children).

  - Ends returned to wall or ground to prevent snagging.

- **Placement:**

  - Along all ramps, staircases, and corridors with elevation change. - Near toilets and seating areas for additional support.

- **Maintenance:**

  - Annual tightening/re-anchoring.

  - Replace damaged or corroded sections immediately.

## C. Special Toilets

- **Purpose:** Ensure independent and safe restroom use for wheelchair users and persons with limited mobility.

- **Design Standards:**

  - Door width ≥ 900 mm, outward opening or sliding.

  - **Turning space:** 1500 mm diameter for wheelchair maneuvering.

  - Grab bars on adjacent walls (horizontal & vertical).

  - Lowered washbasin (height 800 mm), lever-type taps.

  - Emergency alarm cord reachable from floor level.

- **Placement:**

  - Minimum one per building block or floor.

  - Located along main accessible route.

- **Maintenance:**

  - Weekly cleaning and functional check of alarm and grab bars. Maintain water supply and waste disposal systems at all times. D. Visual-Aid Centre

- **Purpose:** Provide assistive technology and learning materials for persons with visual impairment.

- **Facilities & Services:**

  - Screen-reading software, braille printers, tactile graphics.

  - Accessible e-book repository.

  - Audio-described learning resources.

  - Trained staff for technology assistance.

- **Placement:**

  - Within central library or dedicated resource centre.

- **Maintenance:**

  - Annual software/hardware updates.

  - Weekly functional checks of assistive devices.

## E. User-Friendly Lift for Differently-Abled Individuals

- **Purpose:** Provide vertical circulation where ramps/stairs are impractical.

- **Design Standards:**

  - Door width ≥ 900 mm, cabin size ≥ 1100 mm × 1400 mm.

  - Control panel height 900–1200 mm, with tactile and braille markings. » Visual and audio floor indicators.

  - Power backup for emergency use.

- **Placement:**

  - At main building vertical cores serving all floors.

- **Maintenance:**

  - Monthly safety inspection and lubrication.

  - Annual certification by licensed lift inspector.

## 3. Governance & Monitoring

- **Accessibility Committee:**

  - Chaired by a senior administrator, with representation from students and faculty with disabilities.

  - Oversees compliance, budget allocation, and feedback collection.

- **Audits:**

  - Annual accessibility audit with scoring against national/international standards.

  - Quarterly inspection reports filed with the Estate/Maintenance department.

- **Feedback Mechanism:**

  - Online form & helpdesk for reporting accessibility issues.

  - Resolution within 7–14 working days.

## 4. Training & Awareness

- **Staff Training:**

  - Security, maintenance, and faculty trained in assisting differently-abled persons.

- **Sensitisation Programs:**

  - Annual workshops to build awareness among students and staff.

- **Emergency Preparedness:**

  - Specific evacuation protocols for differently-abled individuals, including drills.

## 5. Budget & Continuous Improvement

- **Budget Allocation:**

  - Minimum 1% of infrastructure budget earmarked for accessibility upgrades and maintenance.

- **Review Cycle:**

  - Biennial policy review incorporating user feedback, technology trends, and updated legal requirements.

# 56. QS I-GAUGE Core Criterion: Social Responsibility (SR)

Core Criterion — Social Responsibility (SR) – 150 Points

## 1. Purpose

- Measures the commitment of the institution towards social impact, community engagement, and environmental sustainability.

- Recognises institutions that integrate social responsibility into education by involving students, staff, and resources in meaningful societal contributions.

## 2. Detailed Breakdown of Indicators

### SR1 – Mechanisms/Systems for Social Involvement (30 points)

- **Definition:** % of students actively participating in NCC, NSS, Red Cross, Eco Clubs, or similar community activities.

| **Criterion / condition**              | **Points / scoring**                             |
|----------------------------------------|--------------------------------------------------|
| **Scoring:** 10% student participation | full 30 points; scaled proportionally below 10%. |

- **Rationale:** Encourages student engagement in service-oriented activities. SR2 —- Community Outreach *Activities* (30 points)

- **Definition:** Measures total community service hours per student per year.

- **Scoring:**

Total hours in outreach/Total student population

| **Criterion / condition**      | **Points / scoring** |
|--------------------------------|----------------------|
| 10% of studentequivalent hours | full 30 points.      |

- **Rationale:** Rewards institutions where students actively contribute to community development.

### SR3 — Recognition for Social Development (30 points)

- **Definition:** Awards, medals, or official appreciation for social development from nonaffiliating organisations over the last 5 years.

- **Scoring:** 2 points per award (max 30 points).

- **Rationale:** Validates institutional contributions through external recognition. SR4 — *Environmental* Impact (50 points)

- **Definition:** Implementation of environmental policies.

- **Scoring:** 10 points each for:

## 1. Sustainability (Green Audit Certification)

## 2. Energy Conservation

## 3. Water Conservation

## 4. Transportation Initiatives

## 5. Waste Recycling

- **Rationale:** Encourages environmental stewardship as part of social responsibility. SR5 — Funding for *Social* Outreach (10 points)

- **Definition:** Grants or financial contributions by the institution to community/charity work in the last 12 months.

- **Scoring:**

| **Criterion / condition**                                 | **Points / scoring** |
|-----------------------------------------------------------|----------------------|
| **If turnover ≤ 10 crores:** ≥ 1% allocated               | full points.         |
| **If turnover \> 10 crores:** ≥ ¥50 lakhs or 1% allocated | full points.         |

- **Rationale:** Promotes financial commitment to social initiatives.

## 3. Scoring Approach

- **Data-Based Indicators:** SR1, SR2, SR3, SR4, SR5 (all require documentary evidence).

- **Evidence Required:**

  - Participation records for NCC/NSS/Red Cross activities.

  - Outreach event logs with student hours.

  - Award certificates or letters of appreciation.

  - Environmental audit reports and policy documents. Financial records of donations/grants.

## 4. Category Rating Outcome — Social Responsibility (150 Points)

# 57. Mechanisms/Systems for Social Involvement

- Definition & Purpose

  - Refers to the formal structures, policies, and programs established by an institution to encourage student participation in socially beneficial activities. - The aim is to cultivate civic responsibility, leadership skills, empathy, and a sense of community service among students.

- Applicable Programs & Units

  - NCC (National Cadet Corps) — Military-style discipline and service-oriented training.

  - NSS (National Service Scheme) — Focus on social development, community welfare, and volunteerism.

  - Red Cross / Youth Red Cross Units — Activities related to health, safety, and humanitarian aid.

  - Eco Clubs – Environmental protection, sustainability projects, and awareness drives.

  - Other Socially Engaging Activities – NGOs collaboration, blood donation camps, literacy programs, disaster relief, women empowerment initiatives, health awareness camps.

- QS I-GAUGE Scoring Method

  - **Indicator Code:** SR1

  - **Points Allocation:**

- 30 points *total.*

- Based on proportion of students actively participating in these programs.

- A 10% student participation rate is the full benchmark (scaled proportionally).

(Capped at 30 points).

- Evidence Required

  - Official enrolment lists for NCC/NSS/Red Cross/Eco Club members.

  - Photographic evidence, activity reports, and event participation records.

  - MoUs with NGOs, government bodies, or community organizations.

- Best Practices for Higher Scores

  - Encourage *broad* student *participation* across departments.

  - Provide institutional credits or certificates for active involvement.

  - Organize at least 4 major community/social engagement events per year.

  - Showcase measurable community impact (e.g., trees planted, people served). Activi Type (NCC/NSS/Red Stude Stu ty Cross/Eco nt den Name Club/Other) Name t ID Depar Particip Hours Evide Verifie tment ation Contrib nce d Date uted Link (Yes/N o)

# 58. Community Outreach Activities

## 1. Definition & Purpose

- **Community Outreach Activities (Indicator Code:** SR2) measure the institution’s active engagement with the local, regional, or global community beyond its campus boundaries.

- **Purpose:**

  - Foster social responsibility and civic engagement among students. » Use institutional resources and student talent to positively impact society. . Bridge the gap between academia and real-world social needs.

## 2. Examples of Eligible Activities

- Education & Literacy Programs — Teaching in rural schools, digital literacy workshops, tutoring disadvantaged children.

- Health & Wellness Camps – Free medical check-ups, vaccination drives, mental health awareness sessions.

- Environmental Initiatives — Tree plantation, clean-up drives, waste management projects, water conservation awareness.

- Community Development — Infrastructure repair in rural areas, skill development workshops, women empowerment programs.

- Disaster Relief & Humanitarian Aid – Support during floods, earthquakes, pandemics, or crises.

## 3. QS I-GAUGE Scoring Model

- **Maximum Points:** 30 points.

- **Formula Inputs:**

  - Total number of hours spent by students on outreach activities in the last academic year.

  - Divide by total student population to get average outreach hours per student.

  - **Benchmark:** 10% (meaning the average student engages in outreach for hours equivalent to at least 10% of a typical academic workload).

## 4. Evidence Required

- List of outreach programs conducted in the past year.

- Attendance sheets & participation records.

- Photographic and video documentation.

- MoUs or letters from partner communities/NGOs confirming participation.

- Impact metrics (e.g., number of people benefitted, measurable outcomes).

## 5. Best Practices for Higher Scores

- Institutionalize Outreach — Include it in curriculum or as part of credit-based courses.

- Diverse Initiatives — Cover different social needs (health, education, environment).

- Partnerships —- Work with NGOs, local government bodies, or corporate CSR wings.

- Documentation — Maintain detailed reports with before-and-after impact analysis.

- Student Leadership — Encourage student–led projects for sustainability. Outreach

# 59. Recognition for Social Development

## 1. Definition & Purpose

- **Recognition for Social Development (Indicator Code:** SR3) evaluates how well an institution’s social, environmental, and community engagement initiatives are acknowledged and rewarded by credible external agencies.

- **Purpose:**

  - To measure the quality and impact of social initiatives, not just their quantity. . To ensure recognition is coming from independent, non-affiliating bodies (e.g., NGOs, government departments, international organizations, media, or industry bodies).

  - To motivate institutions to achieve excellence and visibility in social responsibility efforts.

## 2. What Qualifies as Recognition

- **Awards, medals, or commendations for:**

  - Community service and rural development.

  - Sustainability and environmental conservation.

  - Educational outreach and literacy promotion.

  - Humanitarian aid and disaster relief.

  - Diversity, inclusion, and gender equality initiatives.

- **Recognition from:**

  - Government ministries or departments (national/state level). NGOs, industry associations, or reputed international bodies (e.g., UN agencies, *WWF,* Red Cross).

  - Prestigious media houses or global ranking agencies for social impact.

## 3. QS I-GAUGE Scoring Model

- **Maximum Points:** 30 points.

- **Formula:**

  - 2 points per award /recognition received in the last 5 years.

  - Only awards from non-affiliating organizations count (awards from the university’s own governing body or affiliating university are excluded). No upper limit on the number of awards considered, but the cap for scoring remains 30 points.

## 4. Evidence Required

- Copies of award certificates, citations, or commendation letters.

- Official communication from awarding bodies.

- Media coverage or press releases confirming the award.

- Photographs or videos from award ceremonies.

- Documentation showing the link between the award and the institution’s social development initiative.

## 5. Best Practices to Achieve Higher Scores

- Plan high-impact, innovative projects that address pressing social or environmental issues.

- Engage in national and international competitions focused on social good (e.g., Smart India Hackathon – Social Sector challenges, UN SDG awards).

- Collaborate with recognized NGOs and CSR programs for joint initiatives.

- Maintain a central repository of awards and recognitions with clear documentation.

- Publicize achievements to attract further recognition.

# 60. Environmental Impact

## 1. Definition & Purpose

- **Environmental Impact (Indicator Code:** SR4) evaluates how well an institution integrates sustainability, environmental conservation, and eco-friendly operations into its governance, campus life, and community outreach.

- **Purpose:**

  - Encourage institutions to adopt long-term sustainable practices. Measure the institution’s commitment to reducing its carbon footprint. . Reward initiatives that contribute to UN Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), particularly SDG 6 (Clean Water), SDG 7 (Clean Energy), SDG 11 (Sustainable Cities), SDG 12 (Responsible Consumption), SDG 13 (Climate Action), and SDG 15 (Life on Land).

## 2. Scoring Model

- **Maximum Points:** 50 points.

- **Breakdown:**

  - **10 points for each of the following policies, actively in place and implemented:** 1. Sustainability (Green Audit Certificate)

- Regular Green Audits conducted by accredited agencies. - Certification showing compliance with environmental regulations.

## 2. Energy Conservation Policy

- Energy–efficient buildings, LED lighting, solar panels, energy management systems.

## 3. Water Conservation Policy

- Rainwater harvesting, wastewater treatment & recycling, water-efficient fixtures.

## 4. Transportation Policy

- Promoting public transport, electric shuttle services, bicycle lanes, carpooling.

## 5. Waste Recycling Policy

- Segregated waste bins, composting units, e-waste management, paper recycling.

## 3. Evidence Required

- Policy documents approved by institutional authorities.

- Audit reports and certifications (e.g., Green Audit Certificate, Energy Audit Report, ISO 14001).

- Photographs/videos of implemented measures.

- **Data & metrics:**

  - Amount of renewable energy generated.

  - Percentage reduction in electricity/water consumption.

  - Quantity of waste recycled annually.

- Collaboration documents with sustainability partners or NGOs.

## 4. Best Practices to Achieve Higher Scores

- Get annual green audits from reputed agencies (mandatory for full points in Sustainability).

- Set measurable targets for energy, water, and waste reduction.

- Integrate sustainability modules into the curriculum.

- Create student-led green clubs to promote eco-consciousness.

- Organize environmental awareness events (e.g., World Environment Day, Earth Hour).

- Publish an annual sustainability report.

# 61. Funding for Social Outreach

## 1. Definition & Purpose

- **Funding for Social Outreach (Indicator Code:** SR5) measures the financial commitment of an institution towards community service, social responsibility, and charitable initiatives.

- **Purpose:**

  - Evaluate how much budget allocation is dedicated to community development projects.

  - Encourage institutions to financially support activities beyond academics that benefit society.

  - Strengthen the link between higher education institutions and sustainable social change.

## 2. Scoring Model

- **Maximum Points:** 10 points.

- **Criteria:**

  - For institutions with annual turnover up to 10 crores → Must allocate ≥ 1% of turnover to community/charity work.

  - For institutions with annual turnover above ¥10 crores → Must allocate ≥ 1% of turnover or ¥50 lakhs (whichever is lower) to community/charity work. Funding must be uti-

## lized in the last 12 months for:

- Local community development programs.

- Support for NGOs, orphanages, rural development, skill training for underprivileged groups.

- Health camps, disaster relief, and other humanitarian aid.

## 3. Evidence Required

- Audited financial statements showing expenditure on social outreach.

- Breakdown of spending with program details.

- Invoices/receipts for donations or project costs.

- Reports on outcomes/beneficiaries.

- Photographs, videos, and media coverage of funded activities.

## 4. Best Practices to Achieve Full Points

- Allocate a fixed budget percentage annually for CSR/social outreach.

- Partner with reputed NGOs for transparent fund utilization.

- Ensure impact measurement through beneficiary feedback and project outcome reports.

- Maintain a public disclosure section on the institution’s website listing all funded outreach programs.

- Link funding initiatives to SDGs (Sustainable Development Goals) for added credibility. Fundi Amount Date of Purpose ng Allocated Allocati /Benefici Sourc (INR) on ary e Institutional Turnover (INR)

# 62. QS I-GAUGE Core Criterion: Governance and Structure (GS)

Core Criterion — Governance and Structure (GS) — 100 Points

## 1. Purpose

- Evaluates the strength, transparency, and inclusiveness of an institutions governance framework.

- Ensures that policies, oversight bodies, and regulatory compliance mechanisms are in place to uphold academic quality, accountability, and ethical practices.

## 2. Detailed Breakdown of Indicators

### GS1 – Ethics Committee (10 points)

- **Definition:** An official ethics committee that reviews:

  - Research proposals

  - Ph.D. dissertations

  - Innovations & ideas

  - Institutional publications

- **Requirement:** At least **1** agenda item and call-to-action recorded in the last 12 months.

- **Purpose:** Ensures academic and research integrity.

### GS2 — Board of Studies (10 points)

- **Scoring:**

  - **2 points:** Presence of a Board of Studies (BoS) with meeting minutes. o 4 points: Policy on teaching enhancement & curriculum development. o 4 points: Policy ensuring faculty participation in BoS.

- **Purpose:** Involves academic experts in curriculum and teaching strategy development. GS3 — Faculty Resource Management (10 points)

- **Scoring:**

  - **5 points:** Faculty recruitment policy.

  - **5 points:** Faculty appraisal/promotion policy.

- **Purpose:** Establishes transparent and merit-based faculty management practices. GS4 — Compliance and Reporting (10 points)

- **Scoring:**

  - **5 points:** Code of conduct policy for students, faculty, and administrative staff. o 5 points: Publication of annual report or institutional development plan within the last year.

- **Purpose:** Maintains institutional accountability and adherence to governance norms. GS5 — Accreditation (50 points)

- **Full Points:** Institutions holding accreditation from one or more recognised agencies: o NAAC, NABH, AACSB, AMBA, EFMD, EQUIS, NBA.

  - **Special Note:** Institutions with Institute of Eminence (IOE) or Institute of National Importance (INI) status automatically receive full points.

- **Purpose:** Validates institutional quality through recognised external evaluation. GS6 —- Alumni & Senior Student Representation (10 points)

- **Requirement:** At least **1** alumnus/alumna and **1** post-graduate student actively participating in governance.

- **Purpose:** Incorporates stakeholder perspectives into decision-making.

## 3. Scoring Approach

- **Data-Based Indicators:** GS1–GS6 (require minutes, policy documents, reports, and accreditation certificates).

- **Evidence Required:**

  - Committee formation and meeting records. » Policy documents.

  - Annual reports.

  - Accreditation proof.

  - Governance body membership lists.

# 63. Ethics Committee

## 1. Definition & Purpose

- **The Ethics Committee (Indicator Code:** GS1) is a formal institutional body that ensures academic integrity, research ethics, and responsible innovation.

- **Purpose:**

  - Review research proposals, Ph.D. dissertations, innovations, ideas, and institutional publications to ensure they comply with ethical standards. » Prevent plagiarism, misconduct, and unethical practices in academic and research work.

  - Promote responsible conduct in research, teaching, and institutional operations.

## 2. Scoring Model

- **Maximum Points:** 10 points.

- **Criteria for Points Allocation:**

  - The institution must have a formally constituted Ethics Committee.

  - The committee should have met at least once in the last 12 months.

  - **At least one agenda item and call to action from the meeting should relate to:** - Research proposal approval.

- Review of Ph.D. dissertations.

- Institutional publications and innovations.

- Academic or research misconduct cases.

## 3. Composition of the Ethics Committee

- Chairperson – Senior academic or administrator (e.g., Dean/Director/VC nominee).

- Faculty Members — At least 2 senior professors from different departments.

- External Expert — From another institution or industry with expertise in research ethics.

- Legal/Compliance Officer — For regulatory oversight.

- Student Representative — Especially for research involving student participation.

## 4. Evidence Required

- Committee Constitution Document (with roles & responsibilities).

- Minutes of Meetings from the last 12 months.

- Agenda & Action Taken Reports (showing review of ethical matters).

- **Policies/Guidelines on:**

  - Plagiarism and research misconduct.

  - Intellectual property rights.

  - Human/animal subject protection (if applicable).

- List of reviewed proposals/publications.

## 5. Best Practices to Achieve Full Points

- Ensure quarterly meetings instead of just the minimum annual meeting.

- Publish ethical guidelines on the institutional website.

- Maintain a transparent research approval workflow.

- Integrate Turnitin/Urkund plagiarism checks before thesis submission.

- Keep records of decisions, corrective actions, and follow-ups.

3\) Ensure the 'Call to Action / Decision Taken' is documented for compliance.

4\) Maintain evidence such as meeting minutes, attendance records, and supporting documents.

5\) The ‘Summary’ sheet auto-calculates total meetings and those with recorded actions.

## Features:

- **Tracker Sheet:** Records meeting date, agenda items, type of review, decisions taken, responsible person/department, and evidence links.

- **Summary Sheet:** Auto-counts total meetings and those with documented calls to action.

- **Guidance Sheet:** Step-by–step instructions to ensure QS I-GAUGE compliance for GS1 (Ethics Committee)

# 64. Board of Studies

## 1. Definition & Purpose

The Board of Studies (BoS) is a statutory academic body in higher education institutions that plays a critical role in curriculum planning, review, and enhancement. Its primary function is to ensure that academic programs remain relevant, updated, and aligned with the latest industry, research, and societal needs.

## 2. Scoring Model

- **Maximum Points:** 10 points.

- **Points Distribution:**

  - 2 Points — Presence of a formal Board of Studies (BoS) with documented *minutes* of meetings in the last academic year.

  - 4 Points — Policy for Teaching Enhancement and Curriculum Development. o 4 Points — Policy ensuring Faculty Participation in the BoS.

## 3. Roles & Responsibilities of the BoS

- Curriculum Design & Revision — Review syllabi for relevance and incorporate new developments.

- Approval of New Courses — Recommend new academic programs aligned with institutional strategy.

- Industry Integration — Invite experts from industry and academia to make the curriculum industry-ready.

- Student & Stakeholder Input — Integrate feedback from students, alumni, and employers.

- Pedagogical Improvements — Suggest teaching methods, learning resources, and assessment enhancements.

- Compliance — Ensure programs meet UGC, AICTE, PCI, MC\], or other regulatory body requirements.

## 4. Composition of the Board of Studies

- Chairperson – Head of the department/school.

- Internal Members — Senior faculty from the department.

- External Subject Experts — From industry, research organizations, or other universities.

- Alumni Representative – Brings graduate perspective.

- Student Representative — Typically a high-performing or senior student.

- Special Invitees — For specific agenda items (e.g., curriculum experts, guest faculty).

## 5. Evidence Required for QS I-GAUGE

- Notification/Order constituting the BoS.

- Meeting minutes from at least the last academic year.

- **Policies on:**

  - Teaching Enhancement – Faculty training, technology adoption, innovative pedagogy.

  - Curriculum Development – Process for periodic revision, approval workflows.

- Records showing faculty participation in meetings.

- Examples of implemented BoS recommendations.

## 6. Best Practices for Scoring Full Points

- Conduct at least two BoS meetings per year.

- Invite industry experts regularly.

- Document changes and action taken clearly in meeting minutes.

- Ensure cross-disciplinary representation for diverse perspectives.

- Maintain a BoS section on the institution’s website for transparency. Mee Agenda Item ting

# 65. Faculty Resource Management

## 1. Definition & Purpose

Faculty Resource Management refers to the policies and systems an institution has in place to effectively recruit, retain, evaluate, and promote its teaching staff. It ensures high-quality teaching, academic excellence, and faculty career growth through transparent and fair processes.

In QS I-GAUGE, it is a Governance & Structure (GS) indicator with maximum 10 points.

## 2. Scoring Model

- **Total Points Available:** 10

- **Point Breakdown:**

  - 5 points — Policy on Faculty Recruitment

  - 5 points — Policy on Faculty Appraisal/Promotion

## 3. Key Components

## A. Faculty Recruitment Policy (5 Points)

## A strong recruitment policy should:

- Define clear eligibility criteria (qualification, experience, research output).

- Specify transparent selection processes (advertisement, screening, interview, demo lectures).

- Follow regulatory guidelines (UGC, AICTE, MCI, PCI, COA, etc.).

- Include equal opportunity clauses (no discrimination based on gender, religion, etc.).

- Maintain selection committee structure (internal + external experts).

- **Publish job advertisements widely (institution website, national portals). Evidence for QS I-GAUGE:**

- Recruitment policy document.

- Recent recruitment notifications.

- Selection process records and committee minutes.

## B. Faculty Appraisal/Promotion Policy (5 Points)

## A well-structured appraisal and promotion system should:

- **Define Key Performance Indicators (KPIs) such as:**

  - Teaching quality (student feedback, peer review).

  - Research output (publications, projects, patents).

  - Contribution to administration and community service.

  - Participation in faculty development programs.

- Align promotions with regulatory frameworks (e.g., UGC Career Advancement Scheme).

- Include annual appraisal forms with self-assessment + HOD review.

- Offer mentoring and support for underperforming faculty.

- Ensure transparency by documenting and communicating results.

## Evidence for QS I-GAUGE:

- Approved appraisal/promotion policy.

- Filled appraisal forms from recent years.

- Records of promotions granted.

- Proof of faculty training for career development.

## 4. Best Practices for Full Score

- Maintain digital HR records for faculty recruitment and promotions.

- Update recruitment and promotion policies every 2–3 years.

- Conduct faculty orientation for new recruits.

- Implement online appraisal systems integrated with MIS.

- Publish faculty recruitment and promotion outcomes on the institutional website. Policy Area

# 66. Faculty Recruitment Policy

## Purpose & Alignment

- Establishes a transparent, merit-based system for hiring faculty members aligned with institutional goals and regulatory norms.

- Ensures fair opportunity for all applicants, supporting diversity, inclusion, and academic excellence.

## Core Policy Elements

- **Eligibility Criteria:**

  - Minimum educational qualifications (e.g., Master’s/Ph.D. as per UGC norms). . Relevant teaching/research/industry experience.

  - Research output requirements (peer-reviewed publications, patents, funded projects).

- **Selection Process:**

  - **Clear procedural flow:** Advertisement → Application Screening → Shortlisting → Interview/Demo Lecture → Final Approval.

  - **Transparency:** Maintain documented scoring matrix and evaluation reports.

- **Regulatory Compliance:**

  - **Adhere to applicable authority guidelines:** UGC, AICTE, MCI, PCI, COA, etc.

  - Include reservation norms as per state/national policy.

- **Equal Opportunity Clause:**

  - No discrimination based on gender, religion, caste, ethnicity, disability, or age (within legal limits).

  - Accessibility provisions for Persons with Disabilities (PwD).

## Committee Structure

- **Internal Members:** HoD, senior faculty, HR representative.

- **External Experts:** Subject matter experts from other institutions/industries (mandatory for credibility).

## Advertisement & Outreach

- **Publish job notifications on:**

  - Institutional website.

  - National job portals (e.g., Naukri, LinkedIn, faculty.in).

  - Print media (national dailies for senior posts).

  - Professional academic forums.

## Documentation & Record-Keeping

- Maintain recruitment registers with applicant data, selection committee minutes, and score sheets.

- Archive all job postings and selection communications for audit & accreditation. Recrui Eligibili tment ty

# 67. Faculty Appraisal/Promotion Policy

## Purpose & Alignment

- Establishes a transparent, performance-based framework for evaluating and promoting faculty members.

- Encourages continuous improvement in teaching, research, and service contributions.

- Ensures alignment with regulatory bodies (e.g., UGC Career Advancement Scheme, AICTE norms).

## Core Components of the Policy

- **Key Performance Indicators (KPIs):**

  - **Teaching Quality:**

- Student feedback scores (quantitative + qualitative). - Peer reviews and classroom observations.

- Course innovations, curriculum design, and pedagogical improvements.

  - **Research Output:**

- Publications in peer-reviewed journals.

- Books, book chapters, patents, and copyrights.

- Funded projects and grants secured.

  - **Administrative & Community Service:**

- Departmental coordination, committee work, event organisation. - Outreach programs, extension activities, and societal contributions. . Professional Development:

- Participation in FDPs, workshops, certifications.

- Engagement with academic/professional networks.

- **Regulatory Compliance:**

  - Promotions linked to UGC Career Advancement Scheme (CAS) stages and API scores.

  - Compliance with AICTE, COA, PCI, or other discipline-specific guidelines.

- **Appraisal Process:**

  - **Annual Appraisal Forms — Combination of:**

- **Self-Assessment:** Faculty details achievements against KPIs. - HOD/Committee Review: Validation and scoring of performance.

  - Multilevel approval (HOD → Dean → Principal/ Vice-Chancellor). - Digital record-keeping for audit and accreditation.

- **Mentoring & Support:**

  - Identification of underperforming areas.

  - Assignment of senior mentors or professional development plans.

  - Periodic progress reviews.

- **Transparency Measures:**

  - Clear weightage and scoring for each KPI.

  - Written communication of appraisal results to faculty.

  - Opportunity for appeal/review.

# 68. Compliance and Report

## 1. Definition & Purpose

Compliance and Report in QS I-GAUGE’s Governance & Structure (GS) framework measures whether an institution:

1\. Has formal policies for ethical conduct among students, faculty, and administrative staff.

2\. Demonstrates transparency and accountability through the publication of annual reports or institutional development plans.

This ensures good governance, ethical operations, and stakeholder trust.

## 2. Scoring Model

- **Total Points Available:** 10

- **Breakdown:**

  - 5 points — Existence of a Code of Conduct policy for students, faculty, and administrative staff.

  - 5 points — Publication of annual report/institutional development plan within the last 12 months.

## 3. Key Components

## A. Code of Conduct Policy (5 Points)

- **Should clearly outline ethical guidelines, responsibilities, and prohibited behaviours for:**

  - Students

  - Teaching staff

  - Administrative staff

- **Covers:**

  - Academic integrity (no plagiarism, cheating).

  - Professional behaviour (respect, non-discrimination).

  - Dress code and discipline.

  - Anti-harassment and anti-ragging measures.

  - Use of institutional resources.

- **Must be approved by governing bodies and publicly accessible (e.g., website). Evidence for QS I-GAUGE:**

- Official “Code of Conduct” policy document.

- Proof of dissemination (emails, website link).

- Orientation/training session records on ethics.

## B. Annual Report / Institutional Development Plan (5 Points)

- Should summarise the academic, administrative, research, and community activities over the past year.

- **Includes:**

  - Academic achievements.

  - Infrastructure developments.

  - Research publications and grants.

  - Student placements and progression.

  - Financial summary.

  - Future goals and development strategies.

- Must be published within the last 12 months.

- **Format can be PDF or printed, but public availability strengthens the score. Evidence for QS I-GAUGE:**

- Annual report document (latest version).

- Institutional Development Plan (IDP) with timelines and measurable goals.

- Proof of publication (website link, printed copy).

## 4. Best Practices for Full Score

- Keep the Code of Conduct signed by all new faculty, staff, and students.

- Display the policy in common areas and publish it on the website.

- Make the annual report visually appealing with infographics for easy reading.

- Link institutional goals in the IDP to national and international benchmarks (e.g., SDGs, NEP 2020).

- Maintain a version history of reports for audit readiness. Aca Policy/ Document de Report Title mic Type

# 69. Code of Conduct Policy

## Purpose of the Policy

- Establishes a clear, unified ethical framework for all members of the institution.

- Promotes a safe, respectful, and professional academic environment.

- Acts as a reference document for disciplinary actions and conflict resolution.

## Scope & Applicability

- **Students:** Expected academic behaviour, attendance, participation, and respect for peers and staff.

- **Teaching Staff:** Ethical teaching practices, fair assessment, professional conduct, and mentorship responsibilities.

- **Administrative Staff:** Confidentiality, impartiality, adherence to institutional procedures, and professional communication.

## Core Coverage Areas

- **Academic Integrity:**

  - Prohibition of plagiarism, cheating, and falsification of records. » Proper citation and acknowledgment of sources.

  - Guidelines for responsible research conduct.

- **Professional Behaviour:**

  - Respectful treatment of all individuals regardless of gender, caste, religion, or background.

  - No use of abusive language or disruptive behaviour.

- **Dress Code & Discipline:**

  - Dress appropriately for academic and professional settings.

  - Maintain punctuality and decorum on campus.

- **Anti-Harassment & Anti-Ragging Measures:**

  - Zero tolerance for sexual harassment, bullying, and ragging. - Clear complaint mechanisms with protection for whistleblowers.

- **Use of Institutional Resources:**

  - Proper use of labs, libraries, equipment, and digital infrastructure. - No misuse for personal or illegal purposes.

## Governance & Approval

- Draft reviewed by ethics/disciplinary committee.

- Approved by governing council or board of management.

- Updated periodically to reflect regulatory changes and best practices. Accessibility & Communication

- Published on institutional website and included in student and faculty handbooks.

- Mandatory briefing during orientation for students, staff, and faculty.

- Signed acknowledgment form from all stakeholders to confirm understanding. # 70. Accreditation

## 1. Definition & Purpose

In QS I-GAUGE'’s Governance & Structure (GS) framework, the Accreditation indicator evaluates whether an institution holds recognised national or international accreditations that validate its quality of education, governance, and institutional processes. It also checks for elite recognition like *Institute* of *Eminence* (IOE) or *Institute* of *National* *Importance* (INI), which automatically reflect the highest standards.

## 2. Scoring Model

- **Total Points Available:** 50

- **Full Points Criteria:**

  - **Institution holds valid accreditation(s) from recognised bodies such as:** - NAAC — National Assessment and Accreditation Council - NBA — National Board of Accreditation

- NABH – National Accreditation Board for Hospitals & Healthcare Providers (for medical/health institutions)

- AACSB, AMBA, EQUIS, EFMD – Global business school accreditations o Automatic Full Points if the institution has:

- Institute of Eminence (IOE) status (as notified by the Government of India).

- Institute of National Importance (INI) status (e.g., IITs, NITs, AIIMS).

- **Partial Points may be awarded for:**

  - Accreditation in specific departments or programmes instead of full institutional accreditation.

  - Accreditation process initiated but not completed.

## 3. Importance in QS I-GAUGE

- Acts as a quality assurance seal, assuring stakeholders (students, faculty, employers) that the institution meets predefined academic and governance benchmarks.

- Increases national and global recognition.

- Supports international collaborations and student mobility.

- Directly impacts overall QS I-GAUGE rating.

## 4. Evidence Required

- Accreditation certificates (valid at the time of QS I-GAUGE audit).

- Accreditation body’s official letter or confirmation.

- Accreditation report from the awarding body.

- Link to public listing on accrediting agency’s website.

- Proof of renewal applications if re-accreditation is ongoing.

## 5. Best Practices for Full Score

- Maintain updated records of all accreditations and renewals.

- Display accreditation details prominently on the institution’s website.

- Align internal quality audits with accrediting body requirements.

- Pursue multiple accreditations for different programmes to strengthen credibility.

- Ensure continuous improvement plans are documented and implemented postaccreditation.

7\) Maintain a digital repository of all accreditation-related documents for audit purposes.

## Contents:

- **Accreditation Records:** Logs accrediting body, type, grade, coverage, and validity with evidence links.

- **Summary:** Auto-counts national vs. international accreditations and total records.

- **Policy Evidence:** Stores accreditation policies, renewal guidelines, and governance documents.

- **Guidance:** Step-by-step instructions to ensure complete and timely accreditation tracking

# 71. Alumni and Senior Students’ Representation

## 1. Definition & Purpose

This indicator in the Governance

of alumni and senior involvement

governance. & Structure (GS) category evaluates the extent of students (especially postgraduates) in institutional

The aim is to ensure that decision-making bodies benefit from the experience, feedback, and industry insights of past and senior learners.

## 2. Scoring Model

- **Total Points Available:** 10

- **Full Points Criteria:**

  - **The institution must formally include:**

- At least one alumnus/alumna

- Atleast one senior student (typically a postgraduate student) - Both must have active participation in institutional governance — not just symbolic membership.

- **Partial Points:**

  - May be awarded if only alumni OR senior student participation is present, but not both.

## 3. What Counts as Governance Participation

- **Serving as members in:**

  - Governing/Executive Council

  - Academic Council/Senate

  - Board of Studies

  - Quality Assurance/Accreditation Committee

  - Student Welfare or Placement Committees

- Participating in policy formulation, curriculum review, or strategic planning.

- Having documented roles and meeting attendance records.

## 4. Evidence Required

- Official committee meeting minutes showing alumni and senior student participation.

- List of committee members with their designations and roles.

- Institutional

## governance. policy document stating alumni and student representation in

- Signed appointment/nomination letters.

- Photographs or news reports of governance meetings or events.

## 5. Why It Matters

- Alumni bring real-world industry experience and networking opportunities.

- Senior students offer current campus perspectives to refine policies.

- Encourages inclusive decision-making and continuous feedback loops.

- Enhances employability focus, curriculum relevance, and student engagement.

## 6. Best Practices to Achieve Full Points

- Include alumni from diverse batches and career domains.

- Select senior students from different departments for broad representation.

- Conduct regular governance meetings where alumni and students can voice inputs.

- Maintain transparent nomination and rotation policy.

- Leverage alumni networks for mentorship, placements, and fundraising.

# 73. Concerts and Exhibitions

## 1. Definition

This indicator measures the number of cultural performances and exhibitions organised by your institution within the last 12 months, either:

- On-campus (e.g., college auditorium, amphitheatre, cultural hall, open grounds)

- Off-campus (e.g., collaboration with city art galleries, theatres, museums, public spaces)

It reflects the vibrancy of the cultural ecosystem within the institution and its engagement with the community.

## 2. What Counts as a Concert or Exhibition

## Concerts may include:

- Music concerts (solo, ensemble, choir, band, orchestra, traditional music)

- Dance performances (classical, folk, contemporary, fusion)

- Theatre plays and drama competitions

- Poetry recitals, spoken-word performances

## Exhibitions may include:

- Art exhibitions (painting, sculpture, digital art, photography)

- Craft fairs, handicraft exhibitions

- Book fairs and literary fests

- Science & innovation fairs with a cultural/arts focus

- Cultural heritage and traditional costume exhibitions

## Note:

- Both student–led and institution-led events count.

- Inter-college events hosted at your campus are valid.

- Only events actually organised or co-organised by your institution are counted.

## 3. Scoring Method

- Full Points (25) are awarded if your institution organises 100 events in a year.

- Scoring is proportional for fewer events.

## Formula:

Points=(Number of events in last 12 months/100)x25

## Example Calculations:

| **Criterion / condition** | **Points / scoring**                |
|---------------------------|-------------------------------------|
| 100 events                | (100/100)×25=25 points (full score) |
| 50 events                 | (50/100)×25=12.5 points             |
| 30 events                 | (30/100)×25=7.5 points              |

## 4. Evidence Required by QS I-GAUGE

## You must prove each event through verifiable documentation:

- **Event list in Excel/Word with:**

  - Event name

  - Date

  - Venue

  - Organiser

  - Number of attendees

- Event photos or posters showing the date, venue, and institution name/logo

- Brochures / Flyers / Invitations

- Press clippings / Media coverage (if available)

- Links to social media posts by the official institutional accounts

- Collaboration letters (if co-organised with an external body)

## 5. Best Practices for Higher Points

- Create an Annual Cultural Calendar at the start of the academic year.

- Encourage student clubs and societies to conduct regular events.

- Record every small cultural activity—not just large-scale festivals.

- Partner with local art councils, municipal cultural bodies, and NGOs to increase event count.

- Use multiple venues on campus to host parallel events.

## 6. Common Mistakes to Avoid

- Counting academic seminars/conferences – only artistic/cultural events are counted here.

- Double-counting repeat performances on the same day – these are considered one event.

- Not keeping attendance records — QS may request participant proof.

- Including events older than 12 months — only the last academic year counts.

## 7. Category Impact

AC1 contributes up to 25% of the total Arts & Culture category score. A higher AC1 score significantly boosts chances of getting Gold, Diamond, or Platinum in this category. # 74. Credits and Cultural Awards

This measures the recognition and achievements of an institution’s students, faculty, or the institution itself in arts and culture, awarded by external organizations. It reflects the quality, talent, and outreach of cultural engagement beyond the campus. Key Elements

## 1. Eligible Award Recipients

- Students – Individual or group recognition in arts, culture, or creative activities.

- Faculty Members — Awards for cultural contributions, art exhibitions, performances, or cultural research.

- Institution — Institutional-level awards from cultural bodies, NGOs, heritage organizations, or government agencies.

## 2. Scope of Arts & Culture

## Awards can be in any of the following (not exhaustive):

- **Performing Arts:** Music, dance, theatre, drama, film-making.

- **Visual Arts:** Painting, sculpture, photography, digital art.

- **Literary Arts:** Poetry, storytelling, creative writing.

- **Cultural Preservation:** Heritage conservation, folk art promotion, traditional crafts.

- **Multimedia & Design:** Animation, costume design, stage production.

## 3. Awarding Bodies (External)

## The awards must be from:

- Government cultural boards (e.g., Ministry of Culture, Sangeet Natak Akademi).

- Non-profit cultural foundations.

- Art councils and academies.

- Reputed cultural festivals (national or international).

- Heritage or tourism boards.

**Note:** Internal campus awards or recognition given only by the institution do not count.

## 4. Calculation & Points

- **Maximum Count Considered:** 25 awards.

- **Scaling:**

  - If the institution reports 25 awards, it gets full 25 points.

  - **If fewer awards, points are proportionally reduced:**

Points=(Awards Reported/25)x25

**Minimum considered:** 0 awards = 0 points.

- **Points Ceiling:** This indicator caps at 25 points but is later scaled down to 10 points in the overall category calculation.

## 5. Evidence Required for QS I-GAUGE Submission

## You must provide:

1\. Award Title & Description —- Name of the award and cultural field.

2\. Recipient — Student name(s), faculty, or institution.

3\. Awarding Body — Name of organization presenting the award.

4\. Level – Local, state, national, or international.

5\. Date Awarded —- Month & year.

6\. Proof — Certificate copy, official award letter, press release, or web link.

# 75. Investment towards arts and culture

## Indicator Objective

- To assess the institution’s commitment to fostering arts and cultural heritage through direct financial investment.

- Measures how much of the institution’s annual revenue is allocated to projects, events, or initiatives that promote arts, culture, or cultural preservation on campus or in the community.

## Definition of “Investment”

- **Direct Funding:** Allocations from institutional revenue for arts festivals, exhibitions, performances, cultural research, and heritage conservation projects.

- **Capital Expenditure:** Infrastructure for cultural spaces (e.g., auditoriums, art galleries, cultural centers).

- **Operational Costs:** Salaries of arts/cultural program coordinators, artist honorariums, event logistics, and marketing for cultural activities.

- **Collaborations & Sponsorships:** Funding for cultural activities through partnerships with local/national cultural bodies.

## Calculation Method

- **Example:**

  - **Total annual revenue:** ¥50 crore

  - **Arts & culture investment:** 20.5 crore

  - Percentage contribution = (0.5 / 50) × 100 = 1%

## Scoring in QS I-GAUGE

| **Criterion / condition** | **Points / scoring**                                         |
|---------------------------|--------------------------------------------------------------|
| 1% or more                | Maximum scaled score (full points for the sub-indicator=10). |

- Less than 1% → Scaled proportionally down to 0.

| **Criterion / condition**    | **Points / scoring** |
|------------------------------|----------------------|
| If no investment is reported | Score = 0.           |

## Eligible Activities for Counting Investment

- On-campus concerts, theatre productions, art exhibitions, film festivals.

- Restoration or preservation of historical buildings/monuments under institutional care.

- Scholarships or grants for cultural studies and fine arts.

- Organizing intercultural exchange programs and heritage walks.

- Digitization of rare manuscripts, archives, and artworks.

## Evidence Required for QS I-GAUGE Submission

- Financial statements highlighting arts & culture expenditure.

- Event/program reports with budgets and expenditure breakdown.

- MoUs or agreements with cultural bodies.

- Photographic/video documentation of funded cultural initiatives.

- Receipts/invoices for capital and operational expenses related to cultural activities. Good Practices for Higher Scores

- Maintain a dedicated arts & culture budget line in annual planning.

- Align investments with sustainable cultural development (e.g., green event practices, traditional craft revival).

- Showcase cultural engagement on the institutional website and annual report.

- Engage alumni and corporate partners for co-funding cultural initiatives. Finan Total Total Percentage Description cial Annual Investment in Contributio of Funded Year n (%) Activities Evidence/ QS IDocumen GAUGE t Link

# 76. Arts & Culture Facilities

## Indicator summary (max 30 points)

- **Scope:** Physical Arts & Culture Facilities on campus currently built, functional, and in use.

- **Scoring:** 3 points per facility present and compliant; Auditorium must have ≥100 seats; total 10 items × 3 = 30.

## Eligibility rules (what “counts”)

- **General:** Facilities must be institution-owned/leased, accessible to students, operational in last 12 months, and documented on official channels.

- **Exclusivity:** Where stated “built and used exclusively for art,” the space should have primary, regular arts use (not multi-purpose classrooms without clear arts designation).

- **Evidence time-bounds:** Provide evidence dated within the assessment period (photos, timetables, events, audits).

## Facility-wise compliance checklist (proof you should keep)

- \(i\) Auditorium (≥100 capacity)

  - **Must have:** Fixed/portable seating plan proving ≥100 seats, stage, AV, safety compliance.

  - **Evidence:** Seating certificate or plan, photos, recent event schedule, fire/S&E certificates.

- \(ii\) Amphitheatre

  - **Must have:** Purpose-built open-air performance space with stepped/seating arrangement.

  - **Evidence:** Site photos, campus map marking, recent performance schedule.

- \(iii\) Museum

  - **Must have:** Curated collection with cataloguing, display, and public/student access.

  - **Evidence:** Museum page, catalogue excerpt, photos, visitor log/events in last 12 months.

- \(iv\) Gallery (exclusive art; indoor/outdoor)

  - **Must have:** Dedicated exhibition space primarily for art (rotating shows/curation).

  - **Evidence:** Exhibition calendar, curatorial notes, photos, press/website listings.

- \(v\) Arts/Design Studio

  - **Must have:** Dedicated studio(s) for visual arts/design (easels, drafting tables, tools).

  - **Evidence:** Inventory, photos in use, timetable allocating to arts courses.

- \(vi\) Campus radio station

  - **Must have:** Student-run or institution station; recording/production booth, mixer, scheduler.

  - **Evidence:** Station page/stream link, program schedule, equipment photos, training logs.

- \(vii\) Dedicated recording studio

  - **Must have:** Acoustically treated room, audio interface, mics/monitors, DAW workstation.

  - **Evidence:** Gear list, booking logs, photos, samples of student projects.

- \(viii\) Music practice rooms

  - **Must have:** One or more sound-isolated rooms with instruments/stands. - Evidence: Room list, booking calendar, photos, maintenance logs (tuning, etc.).

- \(ix\) Sculpture garden

  - **Must have:** Outdoor area with installed sculptures (permanent /rotational) accessible to campus.

  - **Evidence:** Site photos, map, plaque list, curation/maintenance notes.

- \(x\) Cinema Hall

  - **Must have:** Fixed screening venue with projection, screen, sound, seating plan.

  - **Evidence:** Screening schedule, equipment specs, photos, safety capacity note. Documentation pack (recommended for submission)

- **Facility register:** Name, location code, ownership, floor area/capacity, commissioning year.

- **Usage proof:** Last-12-month schedules (events, classes, rehearsals, exhibitions).

- **Visuals:** 2–3 timestamped photos per facility (exterior, interior, in-use).

- **Compliance:** Safety/fire NOCs (auditorium/cinema), acoustic reports (recording/practice), seating certificate (auditorium).

- **Web presence:** Official URL(s) showing facility description/schedules.

- **Maps/plans:** Campus map with facilities marked; seating/layout plans where relevant. Scoring logic (apply internally before submission)

- **Auditorium:** 3 points only if capacity ≥100; else 0.

- **All others:** Yes = 3, No = 0.

- **Total:** Sum of all ten items /30; keep an audit trail for any shared/multi-use spaces to justify “exclusive arts use.”

## Common pitfalls (avoid these)

- Counting multi-purpose halls as gallery/studio without proof of primary arts use.

- Dormant facilities with no usage in the last 12 months.

- Missing capacity proof for the auditorium (no seating plan/certificate).

- Temporary pop-ups claimed as permanent facilities.

- No public-facing evidence (website pages missing or outdated).

# 77. \[ADVANCED\] Entrepreneurship (ET)

- ET1 – Training (Max 30 Points)

  - **Definition:** Students who completed ≥25 hours of structured entrepreneurship training (on-campus, off-campus, or online) in the last 12 months.

  - **Scoring:**

| **Criterion / condition**      | **Points / scoring** |
|--------------------------------|----------------------|
| ≥50% of total students trained | full 30 points.      |

- Linear scaling for 0–50% (e.g., 25% trained = **15** points).

  - **Evidence Required:**

- Training program outline, attendance list, completion certificates. - Total current student count for percentage calculation. - Photos/screenshots of sessions, platform analytics (for online).

- ET2 – Incubation Centre (Max 25 Points)

  - **Part A – Funding for setup/capacity (15 points):**

| **Criterion / condition**                | **Points / scoring**                                 |
|------------------------------------------|------------------------------------------------------|
| ≥10 Lakhs invested in the last 12 months | full 15 points. - 5–10 Lakhs → proportional scaling. |
| \<5 Lakhs                                | 0 points.                                            |

- **Part B – Business ideas incubated (10 points):**

| **Criterion / condition**  | **Points / scoring** |
|----------------------------|----------------------|
| ≥5 ideas in last 12 months | full 10 points.      |

- Linear scaling for 0–5 ideas.

  - **Evidence Required:**

- Funding sanction letters, audited accounts showing spend. - List of incubated ideas with founders, summaries, start date. - Photos of incubation facility, MoUs, mentor details.

- ET3 – Alumni Entrepreneurs (Max 10 Points)

  - **Definition:** Alumni who are running/co-founded a startup within 5 years of graduation.

  - **Scoring:**

| **Criterion / condition**             | **Points / scoring**                        |
|---------------------------------------|---------------------------------------------|
| ≥10% of total alumni (last 5 batches) | full 10 points. - Linear scaling for 0–10%. |

- **Evidence Required:**

<!-- -->

- Alumni tracking database, LinkedIn/company registry proof. - Alumni consent forms (if used for official reporting).

- ET4 – Student Aspirations (Max 20 Points)

  - **Definition:** Students indicating willingness to start a business in a formal survey.

  - **Scoring:**

| **Criterion / condition** | **Points / scoring** |
|---------------------------|----------------------|
| 210% ≥                    | full 20 points.      |

- Linear scaling for 0–10%.

  - **Evidence Required:**

- Survey questionnaire, sampling method, raw response data. - Summary statistics, screenshots from survey platform.

- ET5 – Faculty with Entrepreneurship Experience (Max 15 Points)

  - **Definition:** Faculty with prior experience working in or running a startup.

  - **Scoring:**

| **Criterion / condition** | **Points / scoring** |
|---------------------------|----------------------|
| ≥10% of total faculty     | full 15 points.      |

- Linear scaling for 0–10%.

  - **Evidence Required:**

- Faculty CVs with startup experience, company registration proofs. - Faculty employment history records.

**QS I-GAUGE Entrepreneurship (ET) – Scoring Guidance ET1:** Full 30 points at ≥50% students trained for ≥25 hours in the last 12 months; linear scaling below 50%.

**ET2 Part A:** Full 15 points at ≥¥10 Lakhs investment in last 12 months; 0–15 linear between ¥5–10 Lakhs.

**ET2 Part B:** Full 10 points at ≥5 business ideas incubated; linear scaling from 0—5. ET3: **Full** 10 points at ≥10% alumni entrepreneurs (last 5 graduating batches); linear scaling below 10%.

**ET4:** Full 20 points at ≥10% student aspirations; linear scaling below 10%. ET5: Full 15 points at ≥10% faculty with entrepreneurship experience; linear scaling below 10%. Value / Status column should contain percentage values for ET1, ET3, ET4, ET5 and absolute values for ET2.

Evidence Link should be an accessible URL to proof documents or hosted content.

# 78. Training

- **Indicator Name:**

Training – Number of students who completed at least 25 hours of entrepreneurship training course (on-campus/off-campus/online) in the last 12 months.

- **Maximum Points:** 30

- **Objective of the Indicator:**

  - To measure the extent of entrepreneurship skill development among the student body.

  - Encourages institutions to offer structured, substantial training (minimum 25 hours) that equips students with business startup, innovation, and venture management skills.

- Definition of “Completed at least 25 hours”

  - **Minimum duration:** Total training time must be ≥25 hours per student (can be spread over multiple sessions).

  - **Completion requirement:** Student must have attended and successfully

## finished the program (certificate of completion or attendance record). o Training type:

- **Oncampus:** Conducted by the institution’s own faculty or invited experts.

- **Off-campus:** Conducted by external institutions/industry bodies (with verifiable proof).

- **Online:** MOOCs or virtual training (with completion proof and course duration).

  - **Content areas may include:**

- Business ideation and validation

- Business planning and modelling

- Marketing and branding

- Finance and funding for startups

- Legal and compliance for entrepreneurs

- Innovation and design thinking

- Pitching and networking skills

- **Scoring Logic:**

  - **Full 30 points:** When 50% or more of the total student population (current academic year) completed such training in the last 12 months.

  - **Scaling:** Points are awarded proportionally between 0%–50% participation. - Formula:

Score=min(1,%Students Trained/50%)×30

- **Examples:**

| **Criterion / condition** | **Points / scoring** |
|---------------------------|----------------------|
| 50% trained               | 30 points            |
| 25% trained               | 15 points            |
| 10% trained               | 6 points             |
| 0% trained                | O points             |

- **Evidence Requirements:**

  - **Training records:**

- Attendance sheets with student names and IDs.

- Course duration proof (session schedules, learning management system logs).

  - **Certificates:** Issued to each participant, showing completion and total hours.

  - **Program documents:**

- Training agenda or syllabus.

- Trainer/facilitator details.

  - **Public proof:** Institution website link/news article with training highlights. c Online training: Screenshots or platform certificates showing duration and completion date.

- **Good Practices for Higher Scores:**

  - Conduct multiple training batches to cover a large percentage of students. » Collaborate with industry partners or incubators for credible training programs.

  - Offer hybrid mode (offline + online) to maximise participation. - Record participation on a centralised tracking system for quick reporting.

  - Integrate entrepreneurship training into the curriculum for compulsory exposure.

- **Common Pitfalls (to avoid losing points):**

  - Counting students who attended only part of the 25 hours (must meet full minimum).

  - Mixing in general skill training (not entrepreneurship-focused). - Lack of date-specific proof showing completion within the last 12 months. - Incomplete attendance records or certificates missing duration details. # 79. Incubation Centre

## Indicator Overview

- **Name:** Incubation Centre

- **Max Points:** 25 (split into Part A - 15 points and Part B – 10 points)

- **Purpose:** To evaluate the institution’s capacity and commitment to nurturing entrepreneurial ideas and ventures through a dedicated incubation ecosystem. Part A — Setting up of Business Incubation Centre (15 Points)

## Definition:

- Institution must have established and operationalised a business incubation centre aimed at supporting student/alumni/community startups.

- **Investment should be infrastructure + operational funding for the incubation centre. Scoring Logic:**

- Full 15 points if investment in the last 12 months is ≥ ¥10 Lakhs.

- Proportional scaling if investment is between ¥5–310 Lakhs.

- 0 points if investment is below ¥5 Lakhs.

## Formula:

Score={15, if Investment≥10 Lakhs

(Investment–5)/5×15, 5≤Investment\<10

0, if Investment\<5

## Eligible Expenses:

- Setting up physical space (furniture, interiors, networking infrastructure).

- Office and co-working facilities for startups.

- Utilities and maintenance for the incubation space.

- Staff salaries (manager, mentors, technical staff).

- Marketing and outreach for incubation programs.

## Evidence Required:

- **Financial Proof:** Audited accounts, sanctioned budget, invoices, receipts.

- **Physical Proof:** Photos, floor plans, equipment lists.

- **Operational Proof:** List of startups currently hosted, services offered.

- **Web/Media Proof:** Official webpage describing the centre and its activities. Part B— Number of Business Ideas Incubated in Last 12 Months (10 Points) Definition:

- Measures the volume of entrepreneurial activity supported by the institution in the past year.

- A “business idea incubated” means a student/alumni/community startup that has been accepted into the incubation program and received at least one form of support (workspace, mentorship, funding, technical guidance).

## Scoring Logic:

- Full 10 points if ≥5 business ideas incubated in the last 12 months.

- Proportional scaling from 0–5 ideas.

## Formula:

Score=min(1,Ideas Incubated/5)x10

## Evidence Required:

- **List of Incubated Ideas:** Title, founders, date of entry, sector/industry.

- **Proof of Incubation:** MoUs, acceptance letters, mentorship session records.

- **Publicity:** Website profiles, news coverage, pitch event documentation.

## Best Practices for Maximising Points

- Maintain a dedicated incubation webpage with profiles of incubated startups and success stories.

- Encourage faculty and alumni to mentor incubated ventures.

- Run hackathons and idea competitions to feed the incubation pipeline.

- Build partnerships with investors and industry experts for funding and mentorship.

- Keep detailed documentation for quick submission (budgets, startup profiles, events).

## Common Pitfalls to Avoid

- Reporting planned centres without operational proof.

- Counting old/defunct startups that are no longer in the program.

- Lacking financial documentation for investment figures.

- Claiming shared spaces without clear demarcation as an “incubation centre.” Parameter

# 80. Alumni with Entrepreneurship Experience

## Indicator Overview

- **Name:** Alumni with Entrepreneurship Experience

- **Max Points:** 10

- **Purpose:** To assess the institution’s success in producing graduates who transition into entrepreneurship within five years of completing their studies. Definition of “Alumni with Entrepreneurship Experience”

- **An alumnus/alumna is counted if they:**

  - Own or have co-founded a legally registered startup/company.

  - Are actively involved in running the business (full-time or part–time). » Started the business within 5 years of graduating from the institution.

## Inclusions:

- Registered private limited companies, LLPs, partnerships, or sole proprietorships.

- Social enterprises, NGOs (if operationally managed by the alumnus/alumna).

## Exclusions:

- Alumni who only invested but have no operational role.

- Freelancers without formal company registration.

- Businesses started more than 5 years after graduation.

## Scoring Logic

- Full 10 points if ≥ 10% of the total alumni (from the last 5 graduating batches) are entrepreneurs.

- Linear scaling for percentages between 0% and 10%.

## Formula:

Score=min(1,% Alumni Entrepreneurs/10%)×10

## Example:

- Total alumni (last 5 years) = 1,000

- Alumni entrepreneurs = 80 (8%)

- Score = (8% + 10%) x 10 = 8 points

## Evidence Required

- **Alumni Database:** List of graduates from the last 5 years with contact info.

- **Entrepreneurship Proof:**

  - Company registration documents (ROC, MSME, GST).

  - Website/LinkedIn profiles showing founder/co-founder role.

  - Press releases, product launches, awards.

- **Time Proof:** Degree completion date and company founding date.

- **Verification:** Alumni declaration forms or LinkedIn screenshots (timestamped). Best Practices to Maximise Score

- Maintain an active alumni tracking system that logs employment and entrepreneurial data annually.

- Create an Alumni Entrepreneurs Network for visibility and peer support.

- Encourage alumni to update their records via an online self-reporting form.

- Feature alumni entrepreneurs on the institution’s website to build brand credibility.

- Collaborate with the incubation centre to mentor new student entrepreneurs. Common Pitfalls to Avoid

- Counting alumni who only intend to start a business but haven’t launched.

- Including businesses without legal proof of registration.

- Not verifying the 5-year graduation window.

- Using outdated alumni data without confirmation of current business activity. Alumn Progr Grad Entre Role Co Compan Registrati Co Withi Pr N Co us/Alu am / uati prene (Founder/ mp y Type on No. mp 1 5 0 ot unt mna Depa on ur?

# 81. Students with Entrepreneurship Aspirations

## Indicator Overview

- **Name:** Students with Entrepreneurship Aspirations

- **Max Points:** 20

- **Purpose:** To measure the entrepreneurial mindset and willingness among the current student body to start their own businesses.

- **Assessment Method:** Based on formal survey responses from students conducted within the last 12 months.

## Definition of “Entrepreneurship Aspirations”

- Counted as “Yes” if a student explicitly expresses the intention to start their own business (full-time or part-time) in the near future.

- **The aspiration can be linked to:**

  - Starting a commercial startup.

  - Launching a social enterprise or NGO.

  - Becoming self-employed in a structured business.

## Scoring Logic

- Full 20 points if ≥10% of total surveyed students express willingness.

- Linear scaling for 0–10%.

## Formula:

Score=min(1,% Aspirations/10%)x20

## Example:

- Total students surveyed = 2,000

- Students aspiring to start business = 150 (7.5%)

- Score = (7.5% + 10%) × 20 = 15 points

## Evidence Required

- **Survey Report:**

  - Survey questionnaire including the specific entrepreneurship aspiration question (e.g., “Do you intend to start your own business within the next 5 years?”).

  - Methodology (online form, offline survey, sample size, date conducted).

  - Raw data file (Excel, CSV) with anonymised responses.

- **Summary Statistics:**

  - Total responses.

  - Number and percentage of “Yes” responses.

- **Authenticity Proof:**

  - Screenshots from survey platform (Google Forms, SurveyMonkey, etc.) showing timestamps.

  - Public or internal report on findings (if published on the institution website). Best Practices to Maximise Score

- Conduct the survey across all departments to capture a true representation.

- Use clear, direct questions to avoid ambiguity in responses.

- Encourage participation through awareness drives and incentives for completing surveys.

- Analyse data promptly and store it in the Entrepreneurship Dashboard for reporting.

- Use the data to design targeted entrepreneurship programs to convert aspirations into actual startups.

## Common Pitfalls to Avoid

- Using outdated surveys (\>12 months old).

- Poor survey wording leading to ambiguous answers.

- Low response rate (not representative of the student population).

- Counting students who answered “Maybe” or “Not sure” as “Yes”. student Ip Departmen Program Year / Email

# 82. Faculty with Entrepreneurship Experience

## Indicator Overview

- **Name:** Faculty with Entrepreneurship Experience

- **Max Points:** 15

- **Purpose:** To measure the extent of practical entrepreneurial exposure among the teaching staff, which can directly enhance the quality of entrepreneurship education and mentorship for students.

Definition of “Entrepreneurship Experience”

## A faculty member qualifies if they have any one of the following within their career history:

1\. Founded or co-founded a legally registered startup/company.

2\. Worked in a startup in a managerial, technical, or leadership capacity.

3\. Served as a co-owner or operational partner in a business venture.

## 4. Held a CXO–level or key decision-making role in an early-stage company. Exclusions:

- Faculty who only invested capital but had no operational role.

- Faculty who worked in large corporates unless they were in a startup–like innovation unit (must have proof).

- Faculty who participated in student projects without real market launch. Scoring Logic

- Full 15 points if ≥10% of total faculty have entrepreneurship experience.

- Linear scaling for values between 0–10%.

## Formula:

Score=min(1,% Faculty Entrepreneurs/10%)×15

## Example:

- Total faculty = 120

- Faculty with entrepreneurship experience = 9 (7.5%)

- Score = (7.5% + 10%) × 15 = 11.25 points

## Evidence Required

- **Faculty List:** Name, designation, department, entrepreneurship background.

- **Proof of Experience:**

  - Company registration documents naming the faculty as founder/co-founder. - Employment letters from startups, showing role and tenure. Media coverage, website bios, or LinkedIn profiles verifying the role.

  - Patent/innovation records linked to startup work.

- **Time Validity:** The experience can be from any time in career, not necessarily current employment, but must be verifiable.

## Best Practices to Maximise Score

- Maintain an updated faculty CV repository with entrepreneurship credentials.

- Feature faculty entrepreneurs on the institution’s entrepreneurship webpage.

- Invite faculty to mentor students in the incubation centre.

- Host entrepreneurship talks where faculty share real-world startup experiences.

- Encourage faculty to register consulting ventures officially for proof. Common Pitfalls to Avoid

- Inflating numbers by counting faculty who only had short internships in startups.

- No official proof to back entrepreneurship claims.

- Counting faculty with only research collaborations but no operational business role.

- Not keeping evidence up to date before QS I-GAUGE verification.

# 83. \[ADVANCED\] Academic Development (AD)

### AD1 – Learning and Development Centre (20 points)

- **Max Points:** 5 each × 4 sub-points = 20.

- **Scoring Items:**

## 1. Presence of Learning & Development Centre (≥50 capacity)

- Physical/virtual space dedicated to faculty/student learning enhancement.

- **Must have:** seating ≥50, member profiles, annual report.

## 2. Continuous Learning/Training Programmes for faculty

- Minimum 2 weeks duration in the last 12 months.

- Can be internal or with external agencies.

## 3. Teaching, Learning and Feedback Questionnaire (TLFQ) Implementation

- Standardised tool to collect teaching effectiveness feedback from students.

- Evidence of questionnaire + analysis reports.

## 4. Faculty Development Workshops based on TLFQ

- Minimum 4 weeks duration in the last 12 months.

- Must link workshop themes to feedback findings.

- **Evidence:** Photos, agendas, attendance lists, reports, TLFQ tool copy, faculty training calendars.

### AD2 – Building Academic Networks (15 points)

- **Max Points:** 5 each × 3 sub-points = 15.

- **Scoring Items:** Institution’s recognised membership in:

1\. International level academic networks (e.g., IAU, ACU, EUA).

2\. National level academic networks (e.g., AIU, NAAC committees, NIRF advisory boards).

3\. Local/State level academic collaborations (state university associations, regional consortia).

- **Evidence:** Membership certificates, MoUs, payment receipts, listing on network websites.

### AD3 – Programme Strength (20 points)

- **Definition:** Ratio of completed applications to total available seats in the last enrolment cycle.

- **Scoring:**

  - **Full 20 points at ≥10:**1 ratio.

  - **Scaled down to Oat ≤2:**1.

  - **Linear scaling formula:**

Score=min(1,(Ratio–2)/(10–2))×20

- **Evidence:** Admissions data (applications received, seats available), official admission cycle reports.

### AD4 — Academic Freedom (20 points)

- **Max Points:** 4 each × 5 sub-points = 20.

- **Scoring Items:** Documented policies ensuring:

1\. Freedom for intellectual debate without censorship.

2\. Right to pursue pedagogical methodologies (teaching style choice).

3\. Freedom to choose courses to deliver/study.

4\. Right to disagree with administrative policies.

5\. Right to decide course delivery and assessment mode.

- **Evidence:** Academic governance policies, faculty/student handbooks, policy circulars.

## ADS – Best Academic Practices (25 points)

- **Max Points:** 5 each × 5 sub-points = 25.

- **Scoring Items:**

1\. Academic integrity policies (including plagiarism, research ethics).

2\. Workshops on plagiarism — at least 2 in last 12 months.

3\. Course content revision —- minimum 20% change in manuals/materials in last 12 months.

4\. Access to e-resources — at least one international repository (e.g., JSTOR, IEEE, Springer).

5\. Policies/tools to increase academic engagement (e.g., active learning strategies, LMS analytics).

- **Evidence:** Policy documents, workshop agendas, revised syllabus copies, subscription proof, engagement strategy documentation.

## Submission Best Practices

- **Maintain a centralised AD Evidence Tracker with:**

  - Raw data, policy docs, membership certificates, photos, reports.

  - Direct URL links to publicly available proof.

- For indicators with duration requirements (e.g., 2 weeks/4 weeks programs), explicitly show dates and hours.

- For ratios (e.g., AD3 Programme Strength), maintain calculation sheets with clear formula and data sources.
