# 84. Learning and Development Centre

## Overview

- **Code:** AD1

- **Max Points:** 20 (4 sub-indicators × 5 points each)

- **Purpose:** To assess how effectively the institution invests in capacity building for faculty, ensuring continuous improvement in teaching quality through structured development, feedback, and learning mechanisms.

## (i) Presence of Learning & Development Centre (≥50 capacity) — 5 Points

- **Definition:**

  - A dedicated physical or virtual space within or outside campus designed to deliver structured faculty/staff development initiatives.

  - Must have seating for at least 50 participants.

  - Should maintain member profiles (faculty/staff associated with the centre).

  - Should publish an annual report of activities.

- **Evidence Required:**

  - Photos of the centre (interior & exterior).

  - Seating capacity certificate/floor plan.

  - Profiles of centre members (roles, qualifications, expertise). - Annual report with summary of events, training sessions, and participation statistics.

  - URL of official webpage.

## (ii) Continuous Learning/Training Programmes for Faculty (≥2 weeks) – 5 Points

- **Definition:**

  - Faculty development programs (FDPs) conducted continuously for a minimum duration of 2 weeks in the last 12 months.

  - Can be organised by the institution or in collaboration with external agencies.

  - Should focus on teaching methodologies, curriculum design, research skills, leadership, and subject expertise.

- **Evidence Required:**

  - Training agenda and curriculum.

  - Attendance sheets with faculty names and designations.

  - Photos/videos of training sessions.

  - Certificates issued to participants.

  - Partner MoUs (if co-organised with external agencies).

  - Reports summarising outcomes and feedback from participants.

## (iif) Implementation of Teaching, Learning & Feedback Questionnaire (TLFQ) – 5 Points

- **Definition:**

  - A structured feedback tool to evaluate teaching effectiveness, course delivery, and student learning experience.

  - **Administered to students systematically (end of each semester/module). . Should cover key dimensions:** clarity, engagement, course content, assessment fairness, learning support.

- **Evidence Required:**

  - Copy of the TLFQ questionnaire.

  - Policy document outlining implementation process.

  - Records of administration dates.

  - Consolidated analysis report (quantitative & qualitative feedback).

  - Examples of action plans derived from TLFQ results.

## (iv) Faculty Development Workshops based on TLFQ (≥4 weeks) — 5 Points

- **Definition:**

  - **Faculty workshops designed directly from the analysis of TLFQ results. Minimum cumulative training time:** 4 weeks in the last 12 months (can be split into multiple sessions).

  - Should target identified areas for improvement (e.g., technology integration, active learning strategies, curriculum redesign).

- **Evidence Required:**

  - TLFQ analysis highlighting improvement areas.

  - Workshop agendas linked to those areas.

  - Attendance lists of participating faculty.

  - Photos/videos of workshops.

  - Certificates for participants.

  - Post-workshop evaluation reports measuring effectiveness. Scoring

- Full 5 points for each sub-indicator if all requirements are met.

| **Criterion / condition**                                                                    | **Points / scoring** |
|----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|----------------------|
| Partial points if only some elements are satisfied (e.g., centre exists but no annual report | reduced score).      |

- Total = Sum of points from all 4 sub-indicators (max 20).

## Best Practices for Maximum Score

- Maintain a dedicated L&D webpage with annual reports, member profiles, and training schedules.

- Conduct needs assessments before planning training programs.

- Link FDPs and workshops to measurable outcomes (teaching quality improvements, student satisfaction).

- Use digital tools (LMS, survey platforms) for TLFQ administration and analysis.

- Document impact stories showing how training improved teaching or research outcomes.

**AD1.1:** Provide photos, seating capacity proof, member profiles, and annual report. AD1.2: Attach training program agenda, attendance sheets, photos, certificates. AD1.3: In-clude TLFQ questionnaire, policy, and analysis reports.

# 85. Building Academic Networks

## Overview

- **Code:** AD2

- **Max Points:** 15 (3 sub-indicators × 5 points each)

- **Purpose:** To evaluate the institution’s collaboration strength through formal memberships in recognised academic networks at international, national, and local/state levels.

- **Rationale:** Such memberships provide access to global best practices, research collaborations, policy frameworks, and faculty/student exchange opportunities. (i) International

## Level Membership – 5 Points

- **Definition:**

  - Formal, active membership in globally recognised university/college associations or consortia.

  - **Examples:**

- IAU – International Association of Universities

- ACU – Association of Commonwealth Universities

- EUA – European University Association

- IAUP – International Association of University Presidents

  - Membership must be institutional, not individual faculty memberships.

- **Evidence Required:**

  - Membership certificate or official confirmation letter.

  - Receipt of membership fee payment (if applicable).

  - Listing on the network’s official website.

## (ii) National Level Membership – 5 Points

- **Definition:**

  - Recognised membership in country–level higher education networks.

  - **Examples (India):**

- AIU – Association of Indian Universities

- NAAC advisory councils

- UGC–accredited higher education networks

- National research or innovation consortia

- **Evidence Required:**

  - Valid membership proof.

  - Participation in national academic events as a network member.

  - Inclusion in network’s public directory.

## (iif) Local/State Level Membership — 5 Points

- **Definition:**

  - Membership in state-level or regional higher education forums.

  - **Examples:**

- State University Associations

- Regional Academic Clusters

- District Higher Education Consortia

  - Can be government-led or private consortium recognised by state authorities.

- **Evidence Required:**

  - Membership certificate or official MoU.

  - Evidence of participation in events or projects under the network.

  - Listing on official websites or newsletters.

## Scoring Logic

- 5 points awarded for each level where the institution is a recognised member.

- Partial points not applicable – full points only if valid proof is provided.

- Total = Sum of points from all three levels (max 15).

## Best Practices for Maximum Score

- Maintain an updated record of all memberships with renewal dates.

- Keep a dedicated Academic Collaborations web page showing memberships, MoUs, and benefits.

- Leverage memberships for joint research, faculty exchange, and conferences.

- Publicise events and collaborations arising from these memberships. Common Pitfalls to Avoid

- Submitting expired membership certificates.

- Counting individual faculty memberships instead of institutional.

- Claiming MoUs with single institutions (not part of an academic network).

- Lacking participation proof (being a “passive” member).

# 86. Programme Strength

## Overview

- **Code:** AD3

- **Max Points:** 20

- **Purpose:** Measures the demand for the institutions academic programmes by assessing the ratio of completed applications to total available seats in the most recent enrolment cycle.

- **Rationale:** A higher ratio reflects greater competitiveness, reputation, and perceived value of the institution’s offerings.

## Definition of Key Terms

- **Completed Applications:**

  - Applications that are fully submitted with all required documentation and fees (if applicable).

  - Excludes incomplete or withdrawn applications.

- **Total Available Seats:**

  - The approved intake capacity for all programmes combined in the last admission cycle.

  - Seats counted as per regulatory/approval body limits.

## Scoring Logic

- **Full 20 points at ≥10:**1 ratio (i.e., at least 10 completed applications for every seat).

- **0 points at ≤2:**1 ratio.

- **Linear scaling between 2:**1 and 10:1.

## Formula:

Score={20, if Ratio≥10

0, if Ratio≤2

(Ratio–2)/8×20, otherwise

## Example Calculations:

| **Criterion / condition** | **Points / scoring**         |
|---------------------------|------------------------------|
| **Ratio = 10:**1          | Score = 20 points            |
| **Ratio = 6:**1           | Score = (6–2)/8×20=10 points |
| **Ratio = 2:**1           | Score = 0 points             |

## Evidence Required

- **Admissions Data:**

  - Official records showing total completed applications received. « Approved seat capacity for each programme.

  - Aggregated total for all programmes.

- **Supporting Proof:**

  - Admissions report signed by registrar/admissions head.

  - Screenshots or exports from the online application system.

  - Regulatory approval documents for intake capacity.

## Best Practices for Maximum Score

- Maintain a digital admissions dashboard showing real-time applications vs seats.

- Offer early-bird and priority application windows to boost numbers.

- Actively market programmes in national and international forums.

- Track application-to-admission conversion rates for improvement planning.

## Common Pitfalls to Avoid

- Counting incomplete applications.

- Using outdated or unapproved intake capacity numbers.

- Misreporting numbers by including multiple programme applications from the same applicant (unless counted separately per programme as per QS I-GAUGE rules). Parameter Total Completed Applications (last enrolment cycle)

# 87. Academic freedom for faculty and students

## overview

- **Max points:** 20 (5 sub-indicators × 4 points each)

- **Scope:** Documented policies + demonstrable practice for faculty and students; applies institutionwide and to the last 12 months’ academic operations

## Scoring logic

- Full 4 points per sub-item when (a) a clear, approved policy exists, (b) communication to stakeholders is evidenced, and (c) recent practice/examples are documented

- Partial (2–3) points if policy exists but practice/communication evidence is weak or limited to a subset

- 0 points if no policy or contradictory practices (e.g., censorship incidents) are recorded (i) Right to engage in intellectual debate without fear of censorship (4 pts)

- **Policy essentials:** Commitment to free inquiry; no retaliation for academic opinions; procedures for addressing alleged suppression

- **Operationalisation:** Open forums, debates, research colloquia; grievance redressal channel; whistleblower protection coverage

- **Evidence pack:** Policy document; event calendars; minutes showing diverse viewpoints; grievance SOP; 12-month case log (redacted)

## (ii) Right to pursue pedagogical methodology (4 pts)

- **Policy essentials:** Faculty autonomy to select teaching methods (e.g., flipped, PBL, case-based) consistent with learning outcomes and ethics

- **Operationalisation:** Course plans specifying pedagogy; L&D support; peer review without prescriptive conformity

- **Evidence pack:** Academic regulations; sample course plans; workshop attendance; peer-review notes referencing chosen pedagogy

## (iif) Right to choose the courses of delivery or study (4 pts)

- **Policy essentials (faculty):** Preference/rotation system for teaching assignments; transparent workload norms

- **Policy essentials (students):** Open electives/minors, add/drop rules, cross-registration, credit transfer

- **Operationalisation:** Semester-wise preference collection, seat allocation logic, published elective baskets

- **Evidence pack:** Teaching allocation policy; preference forms; elective lists; add/drop logs; enrolment stats; student handbook excerpts

## (iv) Right to disagree with administrative policies and proposals (4 pts)

- **Policy essentials:** Structured dissent/consultation mechanisms (academic council, BoS, student council) with no adverse action

- **Operationalisation:** Consultation windows on major policy drafts; recorded votes; anonymous feedback channels

- **Evidence pack:** Meeting notices/minutes capturing dissent; revision logs showing changes adopted; grievance outcomes

## (v) Right to determine the mode of course delivery and assessment (4 pts)

- **Policy essentials (faculty):** Autonomy to select delivery mode (in-person/online/hybrid) and assessment mix (formative/summative) aligned to CLOs and university/regulatory

## constraints

- **Policy essentials (students):** Reasonable accommodations (PwD/medical), reassessment/IA improvement policies

- **Operationalisation:** Course outlines with justified mode/assessment weightage;

## moderation/board of examiners for fairness

- **Evidence pack:** Course dossiers; assessment blueprints and rubrics; moderation reports; accommodation records (anonymised)

## Governance & safeguards

- **Approvals:** Senate/Academic Council ratification dates; version-controlled policy

## repository

- **Communication:** Website policy page; induction briefings; faculty/student handbooks;

## email circulars

- **Monitoring:** Annual audit checklist; complaint turnaround SLAs; periodic climate

## survey on academic freedom

- **Non-retaliation clause:** Explicit statement + whistleblower route; escalation matrix with timelines

## Common pitfalls (to avoid score loss)

- Policies that are generic, undated, or lack approval records

- **Practice contradiction:** e.g., elective caps without rationale; coercive pedagogy

## mandates

- Missing 12-month evidence (minutes, logs, course files)

- No grievance trail or unresolved/undocumented dissent cases

## Quick wins to strengthen evidence

- Publish a single “Academic Freedom Charter” page with links to detailed policies and

## recent minutes

- Run an annual Academic Freedom & Ethics briefing (slides + attendance)

- Standardise course dossier templates (methodology, delivery mode, assessment plan,

## TLFQ actions)

- Maintain a Dissent Register (summaries, outcomes) and a Pedagogy Preference Form archive

## Suggested policy snippets (adaptable)

- “The institution safeguards the right to free inquiry and civil discourse; no academic

## shall face adverse action for scholarly viewpoints expressed in good faith.”

- “Faculty may determine pedagogical methods and assessment strategies consistent

## with approved learning outcomes and fairness standards.”

- “Students may choose courses within capacity and prerequisite constraints; transparent add/drop and appeal processes apply.”

## Verification checklist (internal pre-audit)

- Approved policy docs with version/date

- Public URL screenshots (timestamped)

- Sample course files evidencing pedagogy/delivery/assessment autonomy

- Minutes showing debate/dissent + resulting actions

- Grievance logs + resolutions within SLA

- Annual summary note signed by Registrar/Dean (last 12 months)

## Offer: ready-to-use AD4 Evidence Tracker Excel

- **Tabs:** Sub-indicator status, policy links, practice examples, minutes/grievances, course

# 88. Right to Determine the Mode of Course Delivery and Assessment

- **Indicator Name:** *Right* to *Determine* the Mode of Course Delivery and Assessment

- **Points:** 4 (part of AD4 — Academic Freedom for Faculty & Students)

- **Purpose:**

  - Support faculty academic autonomy in choosing delivery and assessment formats.

  - Ensure flexibility and inclusivity for students with special needs or exceptional circumstances.

  - Maintain fairness and quality standards through institutional oversight.

## 1. Policy Essentials — Faculty Perspective

- Autonomy to Select Delivery Mode

  - **Faculty can choose the most suitable delivery mode for their course:** - In-person (traditional classroom)

- Online (synchronous/asynchronous)

- Hybrid (blended)

- Assessment Mix Flexibility

  - **Freedom to set assessment weightage (formative vs. summative) as long as:** - Learning outcomes (CLOs) are met.

- University/regulatory body constraints are respected.

- Alignment with Curriculum & CLOs

  - Mode and assessment must clearly link to desired course learning outcomes.

- Oversight

  - Departmental/Board of Studies (BoS) review to ensure academic integrity and standardisation across programmes.

## 2. Policy Essentials — Student Perspective

- Reasonable Accommodations

  - **Alternate delivery/assessment for students with:**

- Disabilities (PwD)

- Medical conditions

- Temporary impairments (e.g., injury)

- Reassessment & Improvement Opportunities

  - **Policies for:**

- Internal Assessment (IA) improvement

- Reattempt of assessments (subject to conditions)

- Transparency

  - Communication of delivery and assessment modes in course outlines before semester start.

## 3. Operationalisation

- Course Outlines

  - Must specify chosen delivery mode, assessment type, and their justification.

- Assessment Weightage Documentation

  - Percentage breakdown (e.g., 40% formative, 60% summative) with rationale.

- Moderation Process

  - Moderation committees or Board of Examiners ensure fairness and consistency.

- Evidence Maintenance

  - Store policy documents, course outlines, BoS approvals, and student accommodation records.

## 4. Compliance & Evidence Checklist

- Approved academic policy granting delivery and assessment autonomy.

- Course outlines containing delivery mode, assessment breakdown, and justification.

- Accommodation and reassessment policy for special student cases.

- Moderation committee/BoE meeting records ensuring fairness. Cour Cours Facu se e Title 1ty Cod Nam e e Delivery Mode (In person/Online/ Hybrid)

# 89. Right to Engage in Intellectual Debate Without Fear of Censorship

- **Indicator Name:** Right to Engage in Intellectual Debate Without Fear of Censorship

- **Points:** 4 (part of AD4 — Academic Freedom for Faculty & Students)

- **Purpose:**

  - Protect academic freedom of expression.

  - Encourage open dialogue and exchange of diverse perspectives.

  - Prevent administrative or political interference in scholarly discourse.

## 1. Policy Essentials

- Commitment to Free Inquiry

  - Explicit policy clause safeguarding the right to engage in academic discussion, research presentation, and scholarly critique.

- Non-Retaliation Provision

  - Written guarantee that no disciplinary or professional consequences will result from lawful, respectful academic opinions.

- Procedure for Addressing Suppression

  - Clear process for reporting and investigating alleged censorship or suppression of views.

- Inclusion in Codes & Handbooks

  - Integration of this right into faculty and student codes of conduct, research policies, and institutional governance documents.

## 2. Operationalisation

- Open Forums & Debates

  - Regularly organised events encouraging multi-perspective discussions (e.g., public debates, panel discussions, thematic colloquia).

- Research Colloquia

  - Platforms for sharing research findings without pre-approval censorship (except for ethics/legal compliance).

- Grievance Redressal Mechanism

  - Designated authority or committee to address complaints of suppression within a fixed response timeline.

- Whistleblower Protection

  - Coverage under institutional whistleblower policy for individuals reporting censorship or academic freedom violations.

## 3. Compliance & Evidence Checklist

- Written policy explicitly affirming the right to engage in intellectual debate.

- Calendar and records of debates, colloquia, or public lectures.

- Documentation of grievance procedures and resolution logs.

- Inclusion of protection clauses in contracts, handbooks, and academic policies.

## 4. Common Pitfalls to Avoid

- Having the policy in writing but no evidence of actual implementation.

- Overly restrictive content approval processes that limit free discourse.

- Lack of anonymous reporting channels for suppression complaints.

# 90. Right to Pursue Pedagogical Methodology

- **Indicator Name:** *Right* to Pursue Pedagogical Methodology

- **Points:** 4 (part of AD4 — Academic Freedom for Faculty & Students)

- **Purpose:**

  - Safeguard faculty autonomy in selecting teaching methods.

  - Encourage innovation in pedagogy aligned with intended learning outcomes. » Maintain ethical and professional standards while enabling methodological flexibility.

## 1. Policy Essentials

- Faculty Autonomy Statement

  - Clear articulation in institutional policy that faculty have the freedom to choose pedagogical approaches appropriate to course objectives.

- Scope of Pedagogical Choices

  - Explicit mention of *flipped* learning, project-based learning (PBL), case-based *teaching,* *experiential* learning, seminars, and research-based assignments.

- Ethical Alignment

  - Requirement that chosen methods adhere to professional ethics, academic integrity, inclusivity, and accessibility.

- Institutional Support Clause

  - Commitment to provide Learning & Development (L&D) resources, training, and technology needed to implement diverse pedagogies.

## 2. Operationalisation

- Course Plans

  - Faculty include selected pedagogical methodology in official course plans/syllabi at the start of the term.

- L&D Support

  - Access to workshops, peer mentoring, and resource libraries for innovative teaching methods.

- Peer Review without Prescription

  - Review mechanisms focus on alignment with learning outcomes rather than enforcing uniform teaching styles.

- Documentation

  - Maintain evidence in course files, faculty portfolios, and accreditation records showing chosen pedagogy and rationale.

## 3. Compliance & Evidence Checklist

- Approved Academic Freedom Policy including pedagogical autonomy.

- Sample course plans with declared methodologies.

- L&D session records supporting faculty pedagogy development.

- Peer review or feedback forms that confirm non-prescriptive evaluation.

## 4. Common Pitfalls to Avoid

- Lack of written policy — relying on informal understanding of autonomy.

- Peer review processes that indirectly enforce uniform teaching methods.

- No institutional support to implement diverse pedagogies.

1\) In’Methodology Tracker", record each faculty member’s chosen pedagogical method per course.

2\) Mark ‘Aligned with Learning Outcomes’ as Y/N based on course objectives. 3) Note if the course plan contains a policy reference confirming faculty autonomy. 4) Indicate if L&D support was provided and specify the type (training, materials, tech tools).

5\) Document whether peer review was conducted, ensuring it was non-prescriptive. 6) Include evidence links (course plan, workshop records, peer review notes). 7) The 'Summary' sheet auto-calculates key QS I-GAUGE compliance percentages. 8) Use ‘Policy Evidence! to store formal autonomy policy documents and approval details.

## Contents:

- **Methodology Tracker:** Logs faculty teaching methods, policy references, L&D support, peer reviews, and evidence.

- **Summary:** Auto-calculates key compliance metrics for QS I-GAUGE AD4.

- **Policy Evidence:** Records official autonomy policy documents, approval dates, and review cycles.

- **Guidance:** Step-by-step instructions for tracking and documenting faculty pedagogical autonomy

# 91. Right to Choose the Courses of Delivery or Study

- **Indicator Name:** *Right* to *Choose* the Courses of Delivery or Study

- **Points:** 4 (part of AD4 — Academic Freedom for Faculty & Students)

- **Purpose:**

  - Promote faculty satisfaction and expertise alignment through teaching assignment choice.

  - Empower students with academic flexibility, enabling personalized learning pathways.

  - Foster a transparent, equitable, and well-communicated course selection process.

## 1. Policy Essentials — Faculty

- Teaching Assignment Preference System

  - Faculty submit ranked preferences for courses they wish to deliver each semester/year.

- Rotation & Fairness Mechanism

  - System ensures equitable distribution of preferred courses across faculty over time.

- Transparent Workload Norms

  - Clear rules for maximum and minimum teaching hours, lab loads, and project supervision.

- Approval Process

  - Academic heads review preferences and publish final allocations with justifications if deviations occur.

## 2. Policy Essentials — Students

- Open Electives & Minors

  - Opportunity to choose courses outside the core discipline, from an approved elective basket.

- Add/Drop Rules

  - Time-bound provision for students to adjust their registered courses within the semester.

- Cross Registration

  - Enabling enrolment in courses offered by other departments, subject to prerequisites and seat availability.

- Credit Transfer

  - Policy for recognising credits earned from MOOCs, exchange programs, or partner institutions.

## 3. Operationalisation

- Semester-wise Preference Collection

  - Online forms or academic portal for both faculty and students to submit course preferences before timetable finalization.

- Seat Allocation Logic

  - Transparent algorithm or criteria (e.g., CGPA, seniority, lottery) for elective allotment.

- Published Elective Baskets

  - Detailed list of electives, seat counts, prerequisites, and faculty assigned, published ahead of registration.

- Audit Trail

  - Maintain logs of preference submissions, allocation decisions, and waitlists for accountability.

## 4. Compliance & Evidence Checklist

- Policy document covering faculty and student course choice rights.

- Archived preference forms and allocation reports for last academic cycle.

- Published elective baskets with allocation rules.

- Records of cross-registration and credit transfer approvals.

## 5. Common Pitfalls to Avoid

- Ad-hoc course allocation without recorded preferences.

- Elective baskets published too late for informed decision-making.

- No formal approval or transparency in allocation changes.

6\) Ensure transparency by keeping records of allocation logic and any deviations from preferences.

- **Student Course Choice:** Logs elective/minor preferences, seat allocation logic, cross registration, and credit transfer.

- **Policy Evidence:** Records course choice policy details, approval dates, and responsible departments.

- **Guidance:** Instructions for maintaining transparency and compliance.

# 92. Right to Disagree with Administrative Policies and Proposals

- **Indicator Name:** *Right* to Disagree *with* *Administrative* *Policies* and *Proposals*

- **Points:** 4 (part of AD4 — Academic Freedom for Faculty & Students)

- **Purpose:**

  - Encourage constructive dissent in institutional governance.

  - Safeguard against retaliation for opposing administrative proposals. . Ensure academic and student voices are integrated into policy-making.

## 1. Policy Essentials

- Structured Dissent Mechanisms

  - **Formal avenues for expressing disagreement, such as:**

- Academic Council discussions for faculty.

- Board of Studies (BoS) meetings for curriculum-related policies. - Student Council forums for student-related policies.

- Protection Clause

  - Explicit statement that dissent will not result in adverse academic, professional, or disciplinary action.

- Documentation Requirement

  - All dissenting views must be recorded in meeting minutes without alteration.

- Inclusivity Provision

  - Equal opportunity for all eligible faculty/students to voice opinions regardless of hierarchy or academic year.

## 2. Operationalisation

- Consultation Windows on Policy Drafts

  - Defined period (e.g., 10–30 days) for stakeholders to review proposed policies and submit feedback.

- Recorded Votes

  - Use of formal voting systems in councils/committees with transparent result publication.

- Anonymous Feedback Channels

  - Online or physical submission boxes allowing confidential dissent without fear of identification.

- Evidence Maintenance

  - Archive feedback forms, voting records, and policy change logs for audit and accreditation.

## 3. Compliance & Evidence Checklist

- Written policy granting faculty and students the right to disagree.

- Records of consultation processes for at least one recent major policy.

- Meeting minutes with dissenting opinions explicitly noted.

- Feedback system logs and archives from anonymous submissions.

## 4. Common Pitfalls to Avoid

- Informal or undocumented dissent that cannot be evidenced during audits.

- Lack of anonymity in feedback channels for sensitive issues.

- Retaliation or perception of retaliation against dissenters.

2\) Record details such as consultation dates, methods, votes, and feedback summaries.

# 93. Best academic practices

- overview

  - **Max points:** 25 (5 sub-indicators × 5 points each)

  - **Scope:** Focuses on policies, activities, and infrastructure that directly improve teaching quality, uphold academic standards, and enhance learning engagement.

  - **Evidence requirement:** Policies, activity reports, participation records, access logs, revision documentation, and dated proof from the last 12 months.

- \(i\) Policies encompassing academic integrity (5 pts)

  - **Policy essentials:** Clear definitions of academic misconduct (plagiarism, cheating, falsification), scope (students, faculty, researchers), sanctions, and grievance procedures.

  - **Operationalisation:** Inclusion in student/faculty handbooks; signed acknowledgment from stakeholders; induction briefings.

  - **Evidence pack:**

- Approved policy document (version/date)

- Website link to policy

- Communication proof (emails, induction slides)

- \(ii\) Workshops relating to plagiarism — at least 2 workshops in last 12 months (5 pts)

  - **Activity design:** Cover Turnitin/Urkund usage, citation styles, paraphrasing, and consequences of misconduct.

  - **Operationalisation:** Organised by L&D, IQAC, or library; expert-led; mandatory for new students/faculty.

  - **Evidence pack:**

- Workshop agendas, attendance sheets, photos/screenshots - Resource materials used

- Feedback forms and summary reports

- \(iii\) Revision of course manuals/structure and materials — at least 20% revision in 12 months (5 pts)

  - **Policy essentials:** Curriculum review cycle, percentage calculation method, approval workflow.

  - **Operationalisation:** Mapping revised components (syllabi, assessments, lab manuals) to version control.

  - **Evidence pack:**

- Revised vs. previous versions with change logs

- Approval minutes (BoS/Academic Council)

- Course revision percentage calculation

- \(iv\) Access to e-resources, e-books, e-journals, and periodicals — at least 1 international repository (5 pts)

  - **Access essentials:** Active subscription to platforms like IEEE Xplore, ScienceDirect, SpringerLink, JSTOR.

  - **Operationalisation:** Remote/off-campus login; faculty/student training on usage.

  - **Evidence pack:**

- Subscription invoices or MoUs

- Access URLs and IP authentication screenshots

- Usage reports (last 12 months)

- **(v) Strategies/Policy/Tools to increase academic engagement (5 pts) o Policy essentials:** Guidelines for active learning (flipped classrooms, projectbased learning), technology integration, mentoring programmes.

  - **Operationalisation:** Faculty training, engagement tracking, peer mentoring, learning analytics dashboards.

  - **Evidence pack:**

- Approved strategy documents

- Reports of engagement activities

- Analytics data showing improvement in participation

- Governance & monitoring

  - Maintain a Best Practices Register with columns for sub-indicator, policy/document, activity date, participants, and evidence link. Review and update policies annually; track KPIs (e.g., plagiarism rates, resource usage).

- Quick wins for scoring

  - Publish an Academic Integrity and Engagement webpage linking all relevant policies and activities.

  - Use version-controlled repositories for curriculum changes to prove 20% revision.

  - Leverage LMS analytics to support engagement improvement claims. Sub Description Ma Stat Manual Evidence Link Notes Sco

How to use — AD5 Best Academic Practices Tracker

1\) In 'AD5 Inputs’, mark Status = Yes when the sub-indicator is fully met (policy/activity in place with proof from last 12 months).

2\) Use 'Manual Points' for partial compliance (1–4) or award full points (5) via Yes if complete.

3\) Link evidence directly in 'Evidence Link’ or use the 'Evidence Register’ tab for detailed references.

4\) Keep all proofs dated within the last 12 months for QS I-GAUGE compliance. 5) The ‘Summary’ tab calculates total score, max possible, and percentage achieved automatically.

## 6) Recommended evidence examples:

- **AD5.2:** Workshop agendas, attendance, feedback reports.

- **AD5.3:** Course manual revision logs and approval minutes.

- **AD5.4:** Subscription invoices, access screenshots, usage reports.

- **AD5.5:** Policy documents on engagement strategies, activity records, analytics dashboards.

# 94. Revision of Course Manuals / Structure / Materials

- **Indicator Name:** *Revision* of Course Manuals / Structure / *Materials* — At *least* 20% *revision* in 12 *months*

- **Points:** 5 (part of AD5 — Best Academic Practices)

- **Purpose:**

  - Ensures curricula remain relevant to industry trends, academic research, and pedagogical best practices.

  - Demonstrates a continuous improvement cycle backed by data, approval processes, and documented change logs.

## 1. Policy Essentials

- Curriculum Review Cycle

  - Define the frequency (e.g., annually, biennially) and scope (full programme vs. course-level review).

  - Assign responsibility (e.g., Board of Studies, Academic Council, IQAC).

- Percentage Calculation Method

  - **Establish a clear formula to determine if 20% revision threshold is met:** « Ensure changes are substantive (content update, assessment redesign, inclusion of new topics) rather than cosmetic.

- Approval Workflow

  - Draft → Department Committee Review → Board of Studies → Academic Council → Implementation.

  - Include final publication date to validate 12-month compliance.

## 2. Operationalisation

- Mapping Revised Components

  - **Maintain a Curriculum Change Register listing:**

- Course code/name

- Nature of change (syllabus, assessments, lab manuals, delivery mode) - % change in content

- Date of approval and implementation.

- Version Control

  - Use unique document version numbers (e.g., CM-ENG101-v3.2).

  - Archive previous versions for audit comparison.

  - Highlight changes in tracked documents or side-by-side comparison tables.

- Evidence Practices

  - Side-byside “Before vs. After” content snapshots.

  - Minutes of BoS/Academic Council meetings approving changes.

  - Revised syllabi and manuals with approval stamps/dates.

## 3. Compliance & Evidence Checklist

- Approved Curriculum Revision Policy stating minimum % revision requirement.

- Change logs showing ≥20% of courses/modules revised in the last academic cycle.

- BoS/Academic Council minutes approving revisions.

- Archived and revised versions of syllabi/manuals for proof.

- Publication records showing updated manuals uploaded/issued within 12 months.

## 4. Common Pitfalls to Avoid

- Counting minor typos/format changes as revisions.

- Missing dated approvals, which can lead to disqualification for the 12-month window.

- Lack of centralised version control—scattered documents reduce audit readiness.

How to use — Curriculum Revision Evidence Tracker

1\) In 'Revision Tracker’, log all courses reviewed in the last academic cycle.

2\) For each course, record % change in content. Mark ‘Revision Significant?’ = Y if ≥20% change.

3\) Maintain version numbers for traceability (e.g., ENG101-v2.1).

4\) Attach approval dates and link to official BoS/Academic Council meeting minutes.

5\) 'Summary' sheet auto-calculates % of courses revised significantly and compliance with QS I-GAUGE ≥20% rule.

6\) Use 'Version Archive’ to store file links of old and new course manuals for direct comparison.

7\) Ensure all revisions are approved and implemented within the last 12 months for eligibility.

# 95. Policies Encompassing Academic Integrity

- **Indicator Name:** *Policies* Encompassing Academic Integrity

- **Points:** 5 (part of AD5 — Best Academic Practices)

- **Purpose:**

  - Establishes a formal, institution-wide framework to uphold ethical standards in academic work.

  - Reduces incidents of academic misconduct by defining clear rules, sanctions, and enforcement mechanisms.

## 1. Policy Essentials

- Clear Definitions of Academic Misconduct

  - ***Plagiarism:*** Using others’ work/ideas without attribution.

  - ***Cheating:*** Unauthorized assistance or use of prohibited materials in assessments.

  - ***Falsification:*** Fabricating or altering research data, citations, or records.

  - ***Collusion:*** Unauthorized collaboration on assignments.

- Scope of Application

  - Applies to students, *faculty,* researchers, *administrative* *staff* involved in teaching, learning, and research.

- Sanctions and Penalties

  - Graded levels of severity (warning, grade reduction, course failure, expulsion for students; warnings, suspension, dismissal for staff).

- Grievance and Appeals Procedures

  - Transparent process for accused parties to respond.

  - Timelines for investigation, decision-making, and appeal hearing.

## 2. Operationalisation

- Integration into Institutional Documents

  - Include in *student* *handbooks,* *faculty* manuals, and research *ethics* *guidelines.*

- Acknowledgement from Stakeholders

  - Require all new students, faculty, and researchers to sign a *formal* acknowledgment of understanding the policy.

- Training & Induction

  - Conduct academic integrity briefings during *orientation* weeks for students and induction sessions for new faculty.

  - Periodic workshops or refreshers to reinforce awareness.

- Accessibility

  - Policy document hosted on the institution’s website and intranet.

  - Translations or simplified versions available for diverse audiences.

## 3. Compliance & Evidence Checklist

- Approved Academic Integrity Policy with clear definitions, sanctions, and grievance processes.

- Evidence of inclusion in handbooks/manuals.

- Signed acknowledgment forms (digital or paper) from stakeholders.

- Training/induction session records (agenda, attendance, materials).

- Online publication link for public access.

## 4. Common Pitfalls to Avoid

- Having a policy but not ensuring awareness or acknowledgment from stakeholders.

- Missing sanctions or grievance processes, making enforcement unclear.

- Limiting scope only to students instead of all academic participants. stakeholder Type

3\) Included in Handbook?' should be marked Y if the stakeholder group has access to the policy in their official handbook/manual.

4\) 'Trained/Inducted?' is Y if the stakeholder attended induction or training sessions on academic integrity.

- **Summary:** Auto-calculates total stakeholders, acknowledgment coverage %, and training coverage %.

- **Policy Evidence:** Store details of policy documents, approval dates, review dates, and responsible departments.

# 96. Strategies/Policy/Tools to Increase Academic Engagement

- **Indicator Name:** Strategies/Policy/Tools to Increase Academic Engagement

- **Points:** 5 (part of AD5 — Best Academic Practices)

- **Purpose:**

  - Strengthen student participation and interaction in academic activities.

  - Foster deeper learning through structured engagement strategies. - Enable faculty to use modern teaching tools and practices that promote active learning.

## 1. Policy Essentials

- Active Learning Guidelines

  - Adoption of *flipped* classrooms, project-based *learning,* case study teaching, and problem-solving workshops.

  - Clear expectations on faculty-student interaction frequency, peer collaboration, and formative feedback.

- Technology Integration

  - Policies for using Learning Management Systems (LMS), virtual labs, online collaboration platforms (e.g., Microsoft Teams, Google Classroom), and interactive tools (e.g., Kahoot, Mentimeter).

- Mentoring Programmes

  - Faculty mentoring for academic guidance.

  - Peer mentoring systems to encourage cross-learning.

- Assessment of Engagement

  - Requirement to integrate engagement indicators (e.g., attendance in collaborative activities, forum participation) into course reviews.

## 2. Operationalisation

- Faculty Training

  - Regular professional development on active learning methods, edtech tools, and mentoring skills.

- Engagement Tracking Systems

  - Digital dashboards showing participation rates, assignment submission patterns, and class interaction metrics.

- Peer Mentoring

  - Structured mentor-mentee pairings with documented meeting logs.

- Learning Analytics Dashboards

  - Use of LMS data interventions.

- Annual Engagement Report o Summary of tools improvement plans.

## 3. Compliance & Evidence Checklist to identify low-engagement students and target

## used, training provided, participation rates, and

- Approved Academic Engagement Policy document.

- Faculty training session records (agenda, attendance, materials).

- MS usage reports and engagement dashboards.

- Mentoring programme documentation and meeting logs.

- Examples of active learning activities implemented in courses.

## 4. Common Pitfalls to Avoid

- Only having informal practices without documented policy or tracking.

- No measurable indicators of engagement in course delivery.

# 97. \[ADVANCED\] Research (RE)

## 1. Purpose

- Measures research quality, productivity, funding, and institutional research culture.

- Focuses on citations, publications, research centres, funding, and scholarly platforms.

- Encourages a sustainable research ecosystem with external engagement.

## 2. Indicator-Wise Details

### RE1 – Citations per Faculty (20 points)

- **Definition:**

  - Average number of Scopus® citations received per faculty in the last 5 years.

- **Benchmarks:**

| **Criterion / condition**         | **Points / scoring**                                                                       |
|-----------------------------------|--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| **STEM:** 5 citations/faculty     | 20 points, scaled down to 0.                                                               |
| **Non-STEM:** 3 citations/faculty | **20 points, scaled down to 0. Mixed:** 4 citations/faculty → 20 points, scaled down to 0. |

- **Evidence Required:**

  - Scopus® citation reports (downloaded in CSV/PDF).

  - Faculty list with Scopus Author IDs.

- **Impact:** Reflects research visibility and influence.

### RE2 – Citations per Paper (20 points)

- **Definition:**

  - Average number of citations per paper published in the last 5 years.

- **Benchmarks:**

| **Criterion / condition**       | **Points / scoring**                                                                       |
|---------------------------------|--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| **STEM:** 6 citations/paper     | 20 points, scaled down to 0.                                                               |
| **Non-STEM:** 2 citations/paper | **20 points, scaled down to 0. 0 Mixed:** 4 citations/paper → 20 points, scaled down to 0. |

- **Evidence Required:**

  - Scopus® publication & citation report with paper-wise details.

- **Impact:** Shows quality of research output over sheer quantity.

### RE3 – Specialised Research Centres (10 points)

- **Scoring:** 5 points each for:

1\. Number of research centres on-campus (min 2).

| **Criterion / condition**                                                                                           | **Points / scoring** |
|---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|----------------------|
| 2\. International conferences organised + research reports published in last 12 months (2 events/reports per centre | full score).         |

- **Evidence Required:**

1\. Centre establishment documents.

2\. Conference proceedings, invitations, agendas.

3\. Research reports/publications list.

- **Impact:** Indicates institutional research capacity.

### RE4 – External Research Funding (10 points)

- **Definition:**

  - % of total revenue from external grants in last 12 months.

- **Benchmarks:**

| **Criterion / condition** | **Points / scoring** |
|---------------------------|----------------------|
| **STEM:** 5% revenue      | 10 points.           |
| **Non-STEM:** 1% revenue  | 10 points.           |
| **Mixed:** 2.5% revenue   | 10 points.           |

- **Evidence Required:**

  - Audited accounts showing grant income.

  - Grant sanction letters.

- **Impact:** Shows ability to attract competitive research funding.

## RES – Seed Funding (15 points)

- **Definition:**

  - % of institutional revenue allocated for internal research funding in last 12 months.

- **Benchmarks:**

| **Criterion / condition** | **Points / scoring** |
|---------------------------|----------------------|
| **STEM:** 5% revenue      | 15 points.           |
| **Non-STEM:** 1% revenue  | 15 points.           |
| **Mixed:** 2.5% revenue   | 15 points.           |

- **Evidence Required:**

  - Budget allocation & disbursement records.

- **Impact:** Encourages support for early-stage research.

## REG6 – Proposals Submitted and Accepted (10 points)

- **Scoring:** 5 points each for:

1\. Research funding proposals submitted per faculty (≥1 per 5 faculty in last 3 years).

2\. Ratio of approved & active proposals to submitted ones in last **1** year (≥25% success rate).

- **Evidence Required:**

1\. Proposal submission records.

2\. Approval letters for funded projects.

- **Impact:** Reflects research competitiveness & success rate.

### RE7 – In-house Indexed Journals (15 points)

- **Scoring:** 5 points each for:

1\. At least 2 in-house peer-reviewed journals indexed in Scopus®/UGC-CARE.

2\. Independent editorial board with external reviewers.

3\. At least 2 issues published in last 12 months.

- **Evidence Required:**

1\. Journal indexing proof.

2\. Editorial board composition.

3\. Publication records.

- **Impact:** Promotes scholarly publishing culture.

## 3. Scoring Formula Summary

# 98. Citations per Faculty

## 1. Definition

- Measures the average number of citations received per faculty member in the last 5 years according to Scopus® data.

- Focus is on quality and impact of research, not just publication volume.

## 2. Formula

Citations per Faculty=Total Citations (last 5 years)/Total Faculty Count

## 3. Benchmarks & Scoring

- STEM Institutions

| **Criterion / condition**          | **Points / scoring** |
|------------------------------------|----------------------|
| **Benchmark:** 5 citations/faculty | Full 20 points.      |

- If lower than benchmark, score is proportionally scaled down to 0.

<!-- -->

- Non-STEM Institutions

| **Criterion / condition**          | **Points / scoring** |
|------------------------------------|----------------------|
| **Benchmark:** 3 citations/faculty | Full 20 points.      |

- Mixed Institutions (offering both STEM and Non-STEM programs)

| **Criterion / condition**          | **Points / scoring** |
|------------------------------------|----------------------|
| **Benchmark:** 4 citations/faculty | Full 20 points.      |

## Example Scaling Formula (Excel):

=MIN(20, (Citations_per_Faculty / Benchmark) \* 20)

## 4. Data Sources

- **Scopus® Database — using Author Search and Affiliation Search to get:** o Faculty list with Author IDs.

  - Citation counts for the last 5 years.

- Institution’s HR Database – to get faculty headcount (full-time teaching & research faculty only).

## 5. Evidence Required

- Faculty list with names, departments, Scopus Author IDs.

- Citation count report exported from Scopus (CSV/PDF).

- Calculation sheet showing total citations, faculty count, citations per faculty, and benchmark-based score.

## 6. Important Rules

- Only Scopus®-indexed citations are counted.

- Include only current faculty members.

- Exclude visiting, honorary, adjunct faculty unless officially counted in institutional HR records.

- Citation window is rolling 5 years from the date of assessment.

## 7. Example Calculation

- **Faculty Count:** 100 (STEM)

- **Total Citations (5 years):** 420

- **Citations per Faculty:** 420/100=4.2420/ 100 = 4.2420/100=4.2

- **Benchmark for STEM:** 5 citations/faculty

- **Score:**

Points=4.25x20=16.8 (rounded to 17)

## 8. QS I-GAUGE Alignment

- **Weight:** 20% of total RE score (100 points).

- **Impact:**

  - Rewards institutions where faculty research is widely cited.

  - Encourages high-impact publications over quantity-based publishing. Promotes research visibility in global academic networks.

# 99. Citations per Paper

## 1. Definition

- Measures the average number of citations per paper published in the last 5 years, based on Scopus® data.

- Focuses on research quality and scholarly impact rather than just publication volume.

## 2. Formula

Citations per Paper=Total Citations (last 5 years)/Total Papers (last 5 years)

## 3. Benchmarks & Scoring

- STEM Institutions

| **Criterion / condition**        | **Points / scoring** |
|----------------------------------|----------------------|
| **Benchmark:** 6 citations/paper | Full 20 points.      |

- Non-STEM Institutions

| **Criterion / condition**        | **Points / scoring** |
|----------------------------------|----------------------|
| **Benchmark:** 2 citations/paper | Full 20 points.      |

- Mixed Institutions (both STEM & Non-STEM programs)

| **Criterion / condition**        | **Points / scoring** |
|----------------------------------|----------------------|
| **Benchmark:** 4 citations/paper | Full 20 points.      |

## Excel Scoring Formula:

=MIN(20, (Citations_per_Paper / Benchmark) \* 20)

## 4. Data Sources

- **Scopus® Database — Author and affiliation search to get:** o Paper list with Scopus EIDs.

  - Citation counts for the last 5 years.

- Institutional research database – to faculty/students of the institution.

## 5. Evidence Required

- Publication list (title, authors, year, journal, DOI/Scopus EID).

- Citation counts for each paper (from Scopus).

- Calculation sheet showing total papers, total citations, citations per paper, benchmark, and final score.

## 6. Important Rules

- Only Scopus®-indexed papers are included.

- Citation window is last 5 years from assessment date.

- Exclude conference abstracts, editorials, and non-research notes unless indexed as research articles in Scopus.

## 7. Example Calculation

- **Total Papers (5 years):** 60

- **Total Citations (5 years):** 240

- **Citations per Paper:** 240/60=4.0

- **Benchmark for Mixed:** 4 citations/paper

- **Score:**

4.0/4x20=20 points

## 8. QS I-GAUGE Alignment

- **Weight:** 20% of total RE score (100 points).

- **Impact:**

  - Encourages publication of high-impact research.

  - Rewards institutions for publishing in journals that gain recognition. # 100. Advanced Research

## 1. Purpose

- **Measures an institution’s ability to:**

  - Maintain formal, on-campus research centres. Generate research outputs through those centres.

- **Encourages:**

  - Institutional investment in research infrastructure. Knowledge production via conferences and research reports. » Active contribution to global academic discourse.

## 2. Components & Points Allocation

**Total = 10 points, split into Part A and Part B:**

<img src="media/20. Institutional Values - Independent Rating Framework/media/image3.png" style="width:6.53543in;height:1.08924in" />

Outputs per centre (international conferences

B organised + research reports published) in last 12 5 months

2 outputs per centre

## 3. Definitions

- **On-campus research centre:**

Scaling

Linear scale down to 0

## Linear scale down to 0

- Established through formal governance approval (BoG, Academic Council).

- Located physically or administratively at the main institution campus. » Has staff, budget, ongoing projects.

- Works in specific research domains (e.g., Al, Renewable Energy, Public Health).

<!-- -->

- **International conference:**

  - Organised (solely or jointly) by the research centre.

  - Has international participants/speakers.

  - **Evidence:** brochure, programme, proceedings.

- **Research report:**

  - Official output authored by the centre.

  - Can be white papers, technical reports, policy briefs.

  - Must be published in the last 12 months.

## 4. Scoring Logic

## Part A– On-Campus Research Centres

| **Criterion / condition** | **Points / scoring** |
|---------------------------|----------------------|
| If 0 centres              | O points.            |
| If 1 centre               | 2.5 points.          |
| If ≥ 2 centres            | full 5 points.       |

Part B– Outputs per Centre

Points=min(5,0utputs per Centre/2×5)

- If centres = 0, Part B automatically = O points.

- If outputs per centre ≥ 2, full 5 points.

Total RE3 Score

RE3 Points=Part A Points+Part B Points

## 5. Example Calculation

Metric Value

Number of on-campus centres 3

Conferences (last 12 months) 4

Research reports (last 12 months) 2

Outputs per centre (4+2)/3 = 2.0

Part A Points min(5,(3/2)x5)=

Part B Points min(5,(2/2)×5)=5

Total RE3 Points 10/10

## 6. Evidence Required

- **For Centres:**

  - Governing body approval document.

  - **Centre profile:** name, domain, date established, director.

- **For Outputs:**

  - Conference brochure, programme, website, proceedings. - Research report PDF/DOI with publication date and centre name.

- **For Verification:**

  - Links to institutional website pages listing centres and outputs.

## 7. Common Mistakes to Avoid

- Counting departmental labs as research centres without formal recognition.

- Including off-campus partner hubs.

- Listing events where the centre was just a sponsor.

- Counting outputs older than 12 months.

- Failing to match outputs to the centres that produced them.

## 8. Strategy to Maximise Score

- Ensure ≥ 2 formal research centres.

- Encourage each centre to produce ≥ 2 outputs/year.

- Plan one conference + one report per centre every year.

- Maintain a live tracker with evidence links for instant scoring.

# 101. In-house Indexed Journals

## 1. Overview

- **Weight:** 15 points total.

- **Three equally weighted sub-criteria (5 points each):**

1\. Indexed Journals Count — Number of in-house peer-reviewed journals indexed in Scopus® / UGC-CARE.

2\. Independent Editorial Board – Editorial board includes external members and follows a formal peer-review process.

3\. Publication Periodicity — At least 2 issues published in the last 12 months per journal.

## 2. What counts as “In-house Indexed Journal”

- Owned and managed by the institution (or its constituent unit), not just hosted.

- Indexed in Scopus® or UGC-CARE at the time of assessment.

- Follows peer-review standards with documented editorial policies.

- Publishes research articles, reviews, or scholarly outputs (not just newsletters or bulletins).

## 3. Sub-Criterion Details

## (i) Indexed Journals Count — 5 points

- **Full points:** 2 or more in-house indexed journals.

- **Scoring formula:**

- Points = MIN(5, (Indexed \_Journal_Count / 2) \* 5)

| **Criterion / condition** | **Points / scoring**              |
|---------------------------|-----------------------------------|
| **Example:** 1 journal    | 2.5 points; 2 or more → 5 points. |

## (ii) Independent Editorial Board – 5 points

- **Requirement:**

  - Editorial boards must have external members (outside the institution).

  - Clear separation between authors and reviewers to ensure impartiality.

  - Published peer-review process (single-blind, double-blind, or open review).

- **Evidence:**

  - Editorial board list with affiliations (showing external representation).

  - Peer-review guidelines (web or PDF).

- **Scoring formula:**

- Points = MIN(5, (Journals_With_Independent_Boards / Indexed_Journal_Count) \* 5)

| **Criterion / condition**                                           | **Points / scoring**                                 |
|---------------------------------------------------------------------|------------------------------------------------------|
| **Example:** 2 indexed journals but only 1 has an independent board | 2.5 points. (iii) Publication Periodicity – 5 points |

- **Requirement:**

  - At least 2 issues in the last 12 months for each journal.

  - For continuous publication models, show article release frequency equivalent to ≥2 issues.

- **Evidence:**

  - Issue archive with dates.

  - DOI records showing publication dates.

- **Scoring formula:**

- Points = MIN(5, (Average_Issues_Per_Journal / 2) \* 5)

- **Example:**

  - **Journal A:** 3 issues

  - **Journal B:** 2 issues

| **Criterion / condition** | **Points / scoring**      |
|---------------------------|---------------------------|
| Average = 2.5             | Points = 5 (capped at 5). |

## 4. Evidence Required

- **For Indexing:** Screenshots from Scopus/UGC-CARE listings (dated).

- **For Editorial Board:** Web page or document listing members with affiliations.

- **For Periodicity:** Links/screenshots of issue archives or published DOIs.

- **Peer-Review Process:** Publicly available editorial and review policy.

## 5. Common Pitfalls

- Counting department newsletters as journals.

- Journals hosted but owned externally.

- Outdated indexing status (removed from Scopus/UGC-CARE).

- Editorial board with only internal members.

- No publication dates in archives.

## 6. Scoring Example

Metric Value Points

Indexed Journals 2 5.0

Journals with Independent Board 1 2.5

Avg. Issues per Journal (12m) 2.5 5.0

Total RE7 Points — 12.5 / 15

## 7. Best Practices to Maximise Score

- Maintain ≥2 journals indexed at all times.

- Ensure external board members for every journal.

- Standardise biannual or quarterly publication schedules.

- Assign a journal manager to oversee compliance and evidence tracking.

# 102. External Research Funding

## 1. Indicator Overview

- **Code:** RE4

- **Max Points:** 10

- **Definition:** Measures the proportion of institutional revenue in the last 12 months that comes from external research funding.

- **Purpose:** Recognises institutions that successfully secure competitive or contractual research funding from outside sources, indicating research credibility and impact.

## 2. Scope & Inclusions

## ‘What counts as External Research Funding

- Competitive grants from government bodies (national/international) o e.g., DST, DBT, ICMR, CSIR (India); NSF (US); EU Horizon; UKRI (UK)

- Industry-sponsored research projects

- Research contracts with NGOs, corporates, or public agencies

- International collaborations where funds are transferred to the institution

- Philanthropic/research foundation grants earmarked for research

- Consultancy income directly tied to research deliverables

## What is excluded

- Affiliating institutions’ income (fees from affiliated colleges)

- Internal/seed funding from the institution itself (counted under RES, not here)

- Training-only contracts unless research output is a deliverable

- Revenue from tuition, conferences, or commercial services unless directly linked to a funded research project

## 3. Measurement & Formula

Base Formula

External Research Funding % =

## (External Research Funding in last 12 months + Total Institutional Revenue in last 12 months) x 100

- Institutional Revenue = All income sources excluding pass-through funds for affiliates.

## 4. Scoring Thresholds (Field-specific)

- **STEM:**

  - **Full 10 points if ≥ 5% of total revenue from external research funding. » Scale proportionally:**

  - Points = MIN(10, (Funding% + 5%) × 10)

- **Non-STEM:**

  - **Full 10 points if ≥ 1% of total revenue from external research funding. » Scale proportionally:**

  - Points = MIN(10, (Funding% + 1%) × 10)

- **Mixed disciplines:**

  - **Full 10 points if ≥ 2.5% of total revenue from external research funding. Scale proportionally:**

  - Points = MIN(10, (Funding% + 2.5%) x 10)

## 5. Evidence Requirements

- **Audited financial statements for the last 12 months, showing:**

  - Total institutional revenue (excluding affiliate pass-throughs) o Research-specific external income (itemised by source)

- Grant award letters and project agreements

- Bank receipts or ledger entries for fund transfers

- Project completion or ongoing reports (for context, not scoring)

- Source-wise funding breakdown (government, industry, international, etc.)

## 6. Example Scenarios

## Example 1 – STEM Institution

- Total revenue = ¥100 Cr

- External research funding = 36 Cr

| **Criterion / condition** | **Points / scoring**               |
|---------------------------|------------------------------------|
| Funding % = 6%            | ≥ 5% threshold → Full 10/10 points |

## Example 2 — Mixed Institution

- Total revenue = 380 Cr

- External research funding = 31.2 Cr

| **Criterion / condition** | **Points / scoring**           |
|---------------------------|--------------------------------|
| Funding % = 1.5%          | (1.5 + 2.5) x 10 = 6/10 points |

## Example 3 – Non-STEM Institution

- Total revenue = 350 Cr

- External research funding = 20.5 Cr

| **Criterion / condition** | **Points / scoring** |
|---------------------------|----------------------|
| Funding % = 1%            | Full 10/10 points    |

## 7. Best Practices to Maximise Score

- Establish a dedicated research office to identify and apply for funding opportunities.

- Diversify funding sources (government + industry + international).

- Maintain transparent accounting for research income streams.

- Build long-term industry partnerships with annual research contracts.

- Track funding ratio quarterly to adjust strategies before the assessment year ends. External Research Funding – Tracker

## Purpose:

- Maintain structured records of externally funded research projects for accreditation, audits, rankings, and internal reporting.

## How to use:

1\) Enter one row per funded project in Funding_Data.

2\) Capture details (title, PI, department, sponsor, funding, duration, status). 3) Use dropdowns for Funding Source, Status, Currency, Proof Type.

4\) Link evidence (sanction letter, agreement, invoice, completion report) in Proof URL. 5) Use Summary for KPIs and Funding_Matrix for year × department analysis. Key defini-

## tions:

- **Funding Source:** Government, Industry, NGO, International, Other.

- **Status:** Ongoing, Completed, Terminated.

- **Currency:** Default (can adapt to USD/EUR).

- **Proof:** Sanction Letter, Agreement, Invoice, Completion Report, Other.

***Tip:*** Use *filters* in Funding_Data for sponsor– or year-specific views.

# 103. Seed Funding

- Definition

  - Measures the financial commitment made by the institution itself (from its own budget or reserves) to support research, innovation, or special academic projects.

  - Does not include external grants, consultancy revenue, or donations—this is internal investment into research activities.

- Calculation Formula

Seed Funding %-=Total Institutional Seed Funding (3)/Total Institutional Revenue (%,1ast 12 months)

- **Numerator:** Total seed funding disbursed in the last 12 months (all streams combined).

- **Denominator:** Total institutional revenue in the last 12 months (excluding affiliating income).

<!-- -->

- Benchmarks for Full Points (15 max)

| **Criterion / condition**  | **Points / scoring** |
|----------------------------|----------------------|
| **STEM:** 5% of revenue    | 15 points            |
| **NonSTEM:** 1% of revenue | 15 points            |
| **Mixed:** 2.5% of revenue | 15 points            |

- Scoring Logic

| **Criterion / condition**          | **Points / scoring**                                          |
|------------------------------------|---------------------------------------------------------------|
| If actual % ≥ benchmark            | full 15 points.                                               |
| If actual % \< benchmark           | **points are scaled proportionally:**                         |
| E.g., STEM institution giving 2.5% | (2.5/5)×15=7.5(2.5 / 5) \times 15 = 7.5(2.5/5)×15=7.5 points. |

- Eligible Seed Funding Types (examples)

  - **Internal Research Grants:** Competitively awarded to faculty/researchers.

  - **Innovation & Startup Support:** Incubator funding, prototype development grants.

  - **Conference Support:** Travel & registration grants for presenting research. Lab Equipment Upgrades: Institution-funded purchases for R&D use.

  - **Research Assistantships:** In-stitution-funded stipends for student researchers.

  - **Pilot Project Funding:** Small-scale initial funding for experimental projects.

- Ineligible Items (should be excluded)

  - Funding sourced from external agencies.

  - Infrastructure costs unrelated to R&D (general maintenance, classrooms).

  - Operational salaries for non-research administrative staff.

- Evidence Required (QS I-GAUGE Audit)

  - Financial records showing approved budget and actual disbursement.

  - Policy documents on internal research funding schemes.

  - List of recipients/projects funded with amounts.

  - Annual report sections or Board minutes approving allocations.

- Impact & Purpose

  - Shows institution’s self-reliance in driving research.

  - Encourages a sustainable internal R&D culture.

  - Bridges early-stage funding gaps before external grants are secured. Stream_Type Funding Category Status STEM Internal Research Grant Approved Non-STEM Innovation/ Startup Support Disbursed Mixed Conference Support Closed

# 104. Proposals Submitted and Accepted

## Purpose

- Measures research engagement and success rate in securing funding.

- Encourages faculty to actively apply for research grants and maintain a healthy proposal-to-approval ratio.

## Structure & Weightage

- **Total:** 10 points split equally between:

## 1. Proposal Submission Activity — 5 points

## 2. Approval & Activation Rate – 5 points

## Component 1 – Proposal Submission Activity (5 Points)

- **Metric:**

Proposals per Faculty (last 3 years)=Total Proposals Submitted (last 3 years)/Total Fac ulty Count

- **Benchmark for full 5 points:**

  - **≥ 1 proposal submitted per 5 faculty members in the last 3 years. - Example:** If the institution has 100 faculty, at least 20 proposals over 3 years are required for full points.

- **Scoring Formula:**

Points=min(5,Proposals per Faculty/1/5x5)

- **Evidence Required:**

  - List of all submitted proposals with title, PI name, funding agency, submission date, and status.

  - Institutional research records or Office of Sponsored Research (OSR) logs. Component 2 — Approval & Activation Rate (5 Points)

- **Metric:**

Approval Ratio (last 1 year)

=Approved and Active Proposals/Total Proposals Submitted (last **1** year)

- **Benchmark for full 5 points:**

  - ≥ 25% approval & activation rate in the last year.

  - **Example:** If 12 proposals were submitted in the last year, at least 3 approved and active for full score.

- **Scoring Formula:**

Points=min(5,Approval Ratio/0.25×5)

- **Evidence Required:**

  - Grant award letters, project start notices, funding agency approval documents.

  - List of active projects with funding details and start dates.

## Key Notes for Compliance

- **Faculty Count:** Should match institutional HR records as of the start of the measurement period.

- **Proposal Count:** Must be research funding proposals (not conference travel, training grants, or purely internal proposals unless funded from internal competitive schemes).

- **Active Projects:** Approved projects that have commenced in the reporting year.

- **Time Windows:**

  - **Submission metric:** 3 years rolling window.

  - **Approval ratio:** last 12 months only.

# 105. \[ADVANCED\] Innovation (IV)

## 1. Indicator IV1 – Intellectual Property

- **Max Points:** 40

- **Definition:** Counts the number of active Intellectual Property (IP) rights registered with national or international offices.

- **Types of IP considered:**

  - Patents (utility, design, provisional → only if granted/active) o Copyrights (literary, software, artistic, musical, research datasets, etc.) o Trademarks (institutional brands, prod-

## uct/service marks)

- Geographical Indicators (GI) registered to the institution

<!-- -->

- **Field-specific thresholds for full points:**

| **Criterion / condition**  | **Points / scoring** |
|----------------------------|----------------------|
| **STEM:** 15 active IPs    | 40 points            |
| **Non-STEM:** 5 active IPs | 40 points            |
| **Mixed:** 10 active IPs   | 40 points            |

- **Scoring Formula:**

- Points = MIN(MaxPoints, (Active_IP_Count / Benchmark) × MaxPoints)

- **Evidence:**

  - IP registration certificates from national/international registries (e.g., WIPO, Indian Patent Office, USPTO)

  - Proof of active status (renewals, maintenance fee receipts)

## 2. Indicator IV2 – Spin-off Companies

- **Max Points:** 30

- **Definition:** Measures the number of spin-off companies/foundations created by faculty, staff, or alumni in the last 5 years, which are:

  - Still operational

  - No longer dependent on institutional financial/administrative support

| **Criterion / condition**                 | **Points / scoring** |
|-------------------------------------------|----------------------|
| **Threshold for full points:** 5 spinoffs | 30 points            |

- **Scoring Formula:**

- Points = MIN(30, (SpinOff_Count / 5) × 30)

- **Evidence:**

  - Company incorporation certificates (Registrar of Companies, etc.) o Documentation of institutional origin (incubation records, licensing agreements)

  - Confirmation of independence (self-sustainability)

## 3. Indicator IV3 – External Consultations

- **Max Points:** 20

- **Definition:** Percentage of faculty with parallel non-academic appointments (e.g., in industry, NGOs, think tanks, advisory boards, government committees).

- **Full score benchmark:** 5% of total faculty have such roles.

- **Scoring Formula:**

- Points = MIN(20, (Faculty_with_External_Appointment% / 5%) x 20)

- **Evidence:**

  - Faculty CVs listing external positions

  - Appointment letters or MoUs from external organisations

  - Records from HR/Faculty database

## 4. Indicator IV4 – Commercialisation of Intellectual Property

- **Max Points:** 10

- **Definition:** Measures the percentage of royalty income from IP commercialisation relative to total institutional revenue.

- **Full score benchmark:** 10% of revenue from royalties

- **Scoring Formula:**

- Points = MIN(10, (Royalty_Income% / 10%) x 10)

- **Evidence:**

  - Royalty receipts/contracts from licensees

  - Audited financial statements showing royalty income

  - IP licensing agreements

## Best Practices for Maximising IV Scores

- Maintain a central IP register with renewal alerts.

- Establish a Technology Transfer Office (TTO) to facilitate commercialisation and spinoff creation.

- Encourage faculty-industry collaboration through MoUs.

- Track royalty income separately in financial statements for easy auditing.

- Partner with national IP facilitation centres to speed up filings.

# 106. Intellectual Property (IP)

## 1. Indicator Overview

- **Code:** V1

- **Max Points:** 40

- **Definition:** Counts the number of active Intellectual Property (IP) assets registered with recognised national or international offices.

- **IP Types Included:**

  - Patents (granted, not just applied)

  - Copyrights (software, creative works, instructional material) o Trademarks (logos, brand names, institutional marks)

  - Geographical Indications (GI tags, if institution-related products) Industrial Designs (where applicable)

## 2. Purpose

- Recognises research-to-market outcomes by measuring how innovations are formally protected.

- Encourages a culture of patenting, branding, and IP protection among faculty, staff, and students.

- Provides evidence of knowledge transfer, applied research, and innovation output.

## 3. Scoring Logic

- **Benchmark Thresholds for Full 40 Points:**

| **Criterion / condition** | **Points / scoring**       |
|---------------------------|----------------------------|
| STEM Institutions         | 15 active IPs → 40 points. |
| Non-STEM Institutions     | 5 active IPs → 40 points.  |
| Mixed Institutions        | 10 active IPs → 40 points. |

- **Scaled Proportional Scoring Formula:**

Points=min (40, Active IP Count/Benchmark×40)

- **Examples:**

  - **STEM with 12 patents:** (12+15) ×40=32 points.

  - **Non-STEM with 3 trademarks:** (3+5) ×40=24 points.

  - **Mixed with 8 IPs:** (8+10) ×40=32 points.

## 4. Evidence Required

- **Official certificates:** Grant certificates from Patent Office, Copyright Office, Trademark Registry, WIPO, or equivalent.

- Registration numbers and dates (applications don’t count unless granted and active).

- Renewal status proof for patents/trademarks (to confirm “active” status).

- **Database references:** Links from Scopus Patents, INPASS (India Patent Database), USPTO, WIPO, EUIPO, etc.

- Institutional IP Policy document, showing ownership and rights.

## 5. Measurement Scope

- Active = Granted + in force (renewal paid, not expired).

- **Excludes:** Pending applications, expired or abandoned IP, and IP not owned/assigned to the institution.

- **Includes:** IP owned by institution, faculty, or students if officially assigned or coassigned to the university/college.

## 6. Best Practices to Maximise Score

- Establish an IPR (Intellectual Property Rights) Cell to guide filing and renewal.

- Subsidise filing fees and renewal costs for faculty and student innovations.

- Encourage student projects and theses to result in patent filings.

- File international patents (PCT/WIPO route) for high-value inventions.

- Develop a regular IP audit to track upcoming renewals and avoid lapses.

- Publicise institutional IP achievements to attract industry collaboration. Intellectual Property (IP) – Tracker

## Purpose:

- Maintain structured records of all institutional IP (patents, copyrights, trademarks, designs) for accreditation, audits, rankings, and internal monitoring.

## How to use:

1\) Enter one row per IP filing or grant in IP_Data.

2\) Capture key metadata (title, inventor, department, dates, type, status). 3) Use dropdowns for IP Type, Status, Proof Type.

4\) Link evidence (patent certificates, copyright registration, trademark docs) in Proof URL. 5) Use Summary sheet for KPIs and Filing_Matrix for year × type analysis. Key defini-

## tions:

- **IP Type:** Patent, Copyright, Trademark, Design, Other.

- **Status:** Filed, Published, Granted, Rejected, Expired.

- **Proof:** Filing Receipt, Publication Notice, Grant Certificate, Renewal, Other.

- **Academic Year:** Use institution’s academic cycle (e.g., 2023-24).

***Tip:*** Use *filters* in IP_Data to view active vs *expired* IP or department-wise *trends.*
